(b)	the communication was made or the document was prepared in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act or the abuse of power.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 125
157.	Loss of client legal privilege: related communications and documents
If, because of the application of section 152, 153, 154, 155 or 156, this Division does not prevent the adducing of evidence of a communication or the contents of a document, those sections do not prevent the adducing of evidence of another communication or document if it is reasonably necessary to enable a proper understanding of the communication or document.
Example for this section:
A legal representative advises a client to understate the client’s income for the previous year to evade taxation because of potential tax liability “as set out in my previous letter to you dated 11 August 2019”. In a proceeding against the taxpayer for tax evasion, evidence of the contents of the letter dated 11 August 2019 may be admissible (even if that letter would otherwise be privileged) to enable a proper understanding of the second letter.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 126
	Division 2 — Professional confidential relationship privilege
158.	Terms used
(1)	In this Division —
confidant —
(a)	means a person to whom a communication is made in confidence; and
(b)	includes a journalist (as defined in section 179);
protected confidence means a communication made by a person in confidence to a confidant —
(a)	in the course of a relationship in which the confidant was acting in a professional capacity; and
(b)	when the confidant was under an express or implied obligation not to disclose its contents, whether or not the obligation arises under law or can be inferred from the nature of the relationship between the person and the confidant;
protected confider means a person who made a protected confidence;
protected identity information means information about, or enabling a person to ascertain, the identity of a protected confider.
(2)	For the purposes of this Division, if a person makes a communication in confidence to a minister of religion acting in the minister’s capacity as a minister of religion and in accordance with the tenets of the minister’s faith or religion, the communication is made in the course of a relationship in which the minister is acting in a professional capacity.
(3)	For the purposes of this Division, a communication may be made in confidence even if it is made in the presence of a third party if the third party’s presence is necessary to facilitate communication.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20A
159.	Exclusion of evidence of protected confidences
(1)	A court may direct that evidence not be adduced in a proceeding if the court finds that adducing it would disclose —
(a)	a protected confidence; or
(b)	the contents of a document recording a protected confidence; or
(c)	protected identity information.
(2)	The court may give a direction under subsection (1) —
(a)	on its own initiative; or
(b)	on the application of the protected confider or confidant concerned (whether or not either is a party).
(3)	The court must give a direction under subsection (1) if satisfied that —
(a)	it is likely that harm would or might be caused (whether directly or indirectly) to a protected confider if the evidence is adduced; and
(b)	the nature and extent of the harm outweighs the desirability of the evidence being given.
(4)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account for the purposes of this section, the court must take into account the following matters —
(a)	the probative value of the evidence in the proceeding;
(b)	the importance of the evidence in the proceeding;
(c)	the nature and gravity of the relevant offence, cause of action or defence and the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding;
(d)	the availability of any other evidence concerning the matters to which the protected confidence or protected identity information relates;
(e)	the likely effect of adducing evidence of the protected confidence or protected identity information, including the likelihood of harm, and the nature and extent of harm that would be caused to the protected confider;
(f)	the means (including any ancillary orders that may be made under section 162) available to the court to limit the harm or extent of the harm that is likely to be caused if evidence of the protected confidence or the protected identity information is disclosed;
(g)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding — whether the party seeking to adduce evidence of the protected confidence or protected identity information is an accused or the prosecutor;
(h)	whether the substance of the protected confidence or the protected identity information has already been disclosed by the protected confider or any other person;
(i)	the public interest in preserving the confidentiality of protected confidences;
(j)	the public interest in preserving the confidentiality of protected identity information.
(5)	The court must state the reason for giving or refusing to give a direction under this section.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20C
160.	Loss of professional confidential relationship privilege: consent
This Division does not prevent the adducing of evidence given with the consent of the protected confider concerned.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20D
161.	Loss of professional confidential relationship privilege: misconduct
(1)	This Division does not prevent the adducing of evidence of a communication made or the contents of a document prepared in the furtherance of the commission of a fraud or an offence or the commission of an act that renders a person liable to a civil penalty.
(2)	If the commission of the fraud, offence or act is a fact in issue, the court may find that the communication was made, or the document was prepared, in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act if there are reasonable grounds for finding that —
(a)	the fraud, offence or act was committed; and
(b)	the communication was made or the document was prepared in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (New South Wales) s. 126D
162.	Ancillary orders
Without limiting any action that the court may take to limit the possible harm, or extent of the harm, likely to be caused by the disclosure of evidence of a protected confidence or protected identity information, the court may —
(a)	order that all or part of the evidence be heard in private; and
(b)	make any order relating to the suppression of publication of all or part of the evidence given before the court that, in its opinion, is necessary to protect the safety and welfare of the protected confider and is in the interests of justice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20F
163.	Relationship between this Division and other privileges
(1)	This Division does not apply to a protected communication.
(2)	However, a court may direct that evidence not be adduced under this Division whether or not the evidence is privileged under another section of this Part or would be privileged except for a limitation or restriction imposed by that section.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20B(3) and (6)
	Division 3 — Counselling communications relating to sexual offences
164.	Terms used
(1)	In this Division —
counselling communication has the meaning given in section 165(2);
counsels has the meaning given in section 165(4);
discloses, in relation to a protected communication, has the meaning given in section 166(2);
protected person, in relation to a protected communication, means —
(a)	the sexual offence complainant; or
(b)	any person who made the protected communication; or
(c)	an interpreter through whom the protected communication was made;
require disclosure, of a protected communication, has the meaning given in section 166(3);
supporting affidavit has the meaning given in section 167(1)(b);
support person, for a complainant, means a parent, carer or other supportive person who is present when a person counsels the complainant to facilitate communication between the complainant and the counsellor or to further the counselling process in some other way.
(2)	In this Division, a reference to a document recording a protected communication includes a reference to a document recording any report, observation, opinion, advice, recommendation or other matter that relates to a protected communication made by a protected person.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19A and 19B
165.	Counselling communications that are protected communications
(1)	A counselling communication that is made by, to or about a sexual offence complainant is a protected communication.
(2)	Each of the following is a counselling communication —
(a)	a communication made in confidence by a sexual offence complainant to another person (the counsellor) who is counselling the complainant in relation to any harm the complainant may have suffered;
(b)	a communication made in confidence to or about a sexual offence complainant by the counsellor in the course of the counselling process;
(c)	a communication made in confidence about a sexual offence complainant by a support person in the course of the counselling process;
(d)	a communication made in confidence by or to the counsellor to or by another person who is counselling, or has at any time counselled, a sexual offence complainant.
(3)	For the purposes of subsection (2), a communication can be regarded as being made in confidence even if it is made in the presence or with the assistance of a support person or an interpreter.
(4)	A person counsels another person if —
(a)	the person has undertaken training or study or has experience that is relevant to the process of counselling persons who have suffered harm; and
(b)	the person does any of the following, whether or not for fee or reward —
(i)	listens to and gives verbal or other support or encouragement to the other person;
(ii)	advises, gives therapy to or treats the other person.
(5)	This section extends to a counselling communication —
(a)	whether it was made before or after the commission, or alleged commission, of a sexual offence; and
(b)	whether or not it was made in connection with a sexual offence or an alleged sexual offence or any condition arising from a sexual offence or alleged sexual offence; and
(c)	whether it was made before, on or after the day on which this section comes into operation.
(6)	This section extends to a communication made through an interpreter.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19A
166.	Protected communications not to be disclosed
(1)	A person cannot disclose or require disclosure of a protected communication in or in connection with any criminal proceeding unless the court gives leave.
(2)	A person discloses a protected communication if the person discloses, or adduces or produces anything that would disclose —
(a)	the protected communication; or
(b)	the contents of a document recording the protected communication.
(3)	To require disclosure of a protected communication includes the following —
(a)	to require (whether by the issue of a subpoena or any other process or procedure) the production of a document recording the protected communication;
(b)	to seek an order of the court that will, if made, result in the disclosure of the protected communication or the production of a document recording the protected communication.
(4)	If a subpoena that purports to require disclosure of a protected communication is issued without the leave of the court, the subpoena is of no effect.
(5)	Evidence that cannot be disclosed or required to be disclosed under this section without the leave of the court is not admissible in a criminal proceeding unless that leave is given.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19A, 19C and 19K
167.	Application for leave
(1)	An application for leave of the court to disclose or require disclosure of a protected communication —
(a)	must be made to the court in writing; and
(b)	must be accompanied by an affidavit (a supporting affidavit).
(2)	The application must be made to the court at least 28 days before the date set for the start of a trial in the proceeding, unless the applicant satisfies the court that exceptional circumstances justify the application being made at a later date.
(3)	The supporting affidavit must state why the applicant has a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of the protected communication.
(4)	If an application is duly made and the court considers that the supporting affidavit establishes that, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, the applicant has a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of the protected communication, the court must fix a day and time for hearing the application.
(5)	The court must hear the application before the start of the trial in the proceeding, if practicable.
(6)	The court must give notice of when the application will be heard, together with copies of the application and the supporting affidavit, to the following —
(a)	the applicant and each other party to the criminal proceeding;
(b)	each protected person identified in the application or the supporting affidavit;
(c)	any other person identified in the application or the supporting affidavit as a person to whom the protected communication was made.
(7)	The notice must also inform each protected person, or person to whom the protected communication was made, who is identified in the application or supporting affidavit, of the person’s right to appear, or be represented by legal counsel, at the hearing.
(8)	If the court does not consider that the supporting affidavit establishes that, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, the applicant has a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of the protected communication, the court must —
(a)	refuse leave to disclose or require disclosure of the protected communication, without proceeding to a hearing of the application; and
(b)	notify the applicant that leave has been refused.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19C
168.	Hearing of application
(1)	A hearing of an application for leave to disclose or require the disclosure of a protected communication must be held in the absence of the jury (if any).
(2)	The following persons are entitled to appear, or be represented by legal counsel, at the hearing —
(a)	each protected person identified in the application or the supporting affidavit;
(b)	any other person identified in the application or the supporting affidavit as a person to whom the protected communication was made.
(3)	The court may, if it thinks it necessary to enable it to determine the application, conduct a preliminary examination of the evidence that is the subject of the application.
(4)	For the purpose of a preliminary examination, the court may require a protected person —
(a)	to provide written answers to questions; or
(b)	to appear for oral examination.
(5)	A preliminary examination must be conducted in the absence of the parties.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19D and 19F
169.	Test to be applied by court
A court may, after hearing an application for leave, give leave to disclose or require disclosure of a protected communication if and only if it is satisfied that —
(a)	the applicant has a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of the protected communication; and
(b)	other evidence to the same effect as the protected communication or the document recording the protected communication is not available; and
(c)	it is in the public interest to give leave to disclose or require disclosure of the protected communication.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19E(1) and 19G(1)
170.	Establishing legitimate forensic purpose
(1)	In order to establish a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring the disclosure of a protected communication, it is not sufficient for an applicant for leave to assert, without proving, 1 or more of the following —
(a)	that the protected communication discloses a prior inconsistent statement of the sexual offence complainant;
(b)	that the protected communication relates to the credibility of the sexual offence complainant or another witness in the criminal proceeding;
(c)	that the protected communication relates to the reliability of the testimony of the sexual offence complainant merely because the complainant has received or is receiving counselling.
(2)	It is also not sufficient for the applicant to prove 1 or more of the following —
(a)	that the protected communication exists;
(b)	that the protected communication relates to activity that is the subject of the criminal proceeding;
(c)	that the protected communication was made close in time to the activity that is the subject of the criminal proceeding;
(d)	that the protected communication reveals allegations of the commission of a sexual offence against the sexual offence complainant by a person other than the applicant.
(3)	Subsections (1) and (2) do not limit —
(a)	the matters that the court can consider in deciding whether the applicant has a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of a protected communication; or
(b)	the grounds on which the court can decide that the applicant does not have a legitimate forensic purpose for disclosing or requiring disclosure of a protected communication.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19E(2) and (3)
171.	Public interest test
For the purpose of determining whether it is in the public interest to give leave to disclose or require disclosure of a protected communication, the court must have regard to the following —
(a)	the extent to which disclosing or requiring the disclosure of the protected communication is necessary to allow the applicant for leave to make a full defence;
(b)	whether the evidence of the protected communication or the contents of the document recording the protected communication will have substantial probative value;
(c)	the likelihood that disclosing or requiring the disclosure of the protected communication will affect the outcome of the proceeding;
(d)	the public interest in ensuring that sexual offence complainants receive effective counselling, and the extent to which failure to preserve the confidentiality of protected communications may dissuade complainants from seeking counselling or diminish the effectiveness of counselling;
(e)	the public interest in ensuring that adequate records are kept of counselling communications;
(f)	the likelihood that disclosing or requiring the disclosure of the protected communication will cause harm to the sexual offence complainant, and the nature and extent of that harm;
(g)	any other matter that the court considers relevant.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19G(2)
172.	Effect of consent
(1)	This Division does not prevent a protected communication from being disclosed or being required to be disclosed in, or in connection with, any criminal proceeding if the sexual offence complainant by, to or about whom the protected communication is made has consented to the disclosure.
(2)	Consent is not effective for the purposes of this section unless —
(a)	it is given in writing; and
(b)	it is given expressly for the purposes of this Division.
(3)	This section does not apply if the complainant is a child.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19H
173.	Loss of privilege: misconduct
(1)	This Division does not prevent the production or adducing of evidence of a communication made, or the production of a document prepared, in the furtherance of the commission of a fraud or an offence or the commission of an act that renders a person liable to a civil penalty.
(2)	If the commission of the fraud, offence or act is a fact in issue, the court may find that the communication was made, or the document was prepared, in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act if there are reasonable grounds for finding that —
(a)	the fraud, offence or act was committed; and
(b)	the communication was made or the document was prepared in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19I
174.	Ancillary orders
Without limiting any action that the court may take to limit the possible harm, or extent of the harm, likely to be caused by the disclosure of evidence of, or the contents of a document recording, a protected communication, the court may —
(a)	order that all or part of the evidence be heard or document produced in private; and
(b)	make any order relating to the production, inspection, copying or reproduction of the document that, in its opinion, is necessary to protect the safety and welfare of any protected person and is in the interests of justice; and
(c)	make any order relating to the suppression of publication of all or part of the evidence given before the court that, in its opinion, is necessary to protect the safety and welfare of any protected person and is in the interests of justice; and
(d)	make any order relating to disclosure of information that would tend to identify any protected person that, in its opinion, is necessary to protect the safety and welfare of any protected person and is in the interests of justice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19J
175.	Application of other laws
(1)	A provision of this Division does not affect the operation of any rule of law in relation to evidence in a criminal proceeding except to the extent that it expressly or necessarily has that effect.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), this Division does not affect the operation of a rule of law so far as it relates to the inspection of a document required to be produced in, or in connection with, a criminal proceeding.
(3)	This Division does not affect the operation of the Children and Community Services Act 2004 sections 124F and 240.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19L
176.	Application to civil proceedings
If evidence of a protected communication is found to be privileged in a criminal proceeding under this Division, the evidence must not be adduced in a civil proceeding in which substantially the same acts are in issue as the acts that were in issue in relation to the criminal proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (New South Wales) s. 126H
177.	Regulations
The regulations may make provision for the following —
(a)	the forms to be used for an application for leave to disclose or require disclosure of a protected communication and supporting affidavit;
(b)	the manner in which a protected communication must be described in an application for leave or supporting affidavit;
(c)	other matters to be included in an application for leave or supporting affidavit;
(d)	the procedure relating to an application for leave.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 19M
	Division 4 — Journalist privilege
178.	Application of Division
(1)	This Division extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
(2)	This Division does not affect any power a person acting judicially has under any written law to take any action if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
Note for this section:
Section 15 provides that if a provision of this Act expressly extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially, a reference in the provision to a court includes a reference to a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20G and 20H
179.	Terms used
In this Division —
exclusion order has the meaning given in section 181(1);
informant means a person who gives information to a journalist in the normal course of the journalist’s work in the expectation that the information may be published in a news medium;
journalist means a person engaged in the profession or occupation of journalism in connection with the publication of information in a news medium;
journalist privilege means the privilege conferred by section 180(1);
misconduct, by a journalist or an informant, means any of the following conduct by the journalist or informant —
(a)	the commission of a fraud or an offence or the commission of an act that renders a person liable to a civil penalty;
(b)	deceit, dishonesty, inappropriate partiality or a breach of trust;
(c)	acting corruptly, or corruptly failing to act, in any capacity;
(d)	corruptly taking advantage of their position to obtain a benefit for themselves or another person or to cause a detriment to another person;
(e)	conduct that adversely affects, or could adversely affect, directly or indirectly, the honest or impartial performance of the functions of any person in any capacity;
(f)	misusing information or material that the journalist or informant acquired in any capacity, whether the misuse is to obtain a benefit for themselves or another person or to cause a detriment to another person;
(g)	conduct providing reasonable grounds for terminating the employment of the journalist or informant;
(h)	conduct providing reasonable grounds for disciplining the journalist or informant for unsatisfactory professional conduct or professional misconduct, or for the breach of a professional standard that applies to the profession of the journalist or informant, whether or not the journalist or informant is a member of the body that prescribed the standard;
news medium means any medium for the dissemination to the public or a section of the public of news and observations on news.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20G and 20K
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 126J
180.	Journalist privilege relating to identity of informant
(1)	If a journalist has promised an informant not to disclose the informant’s identity, neither the journalist nor the journalist’s employer is compellable to answer any question or produce any document that would disclose the identity of the informant or enable that identity to be ascertained.
(2)	This section does not apply if the court, on application of a party, makes an exclusion order in relation to the evidence.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 126K
181.	Exclusion of journalist privilege: public interest test
(1)	A court may make an order (an exclusion order) that journalist privilege does not apply to any evidence.
(2)	A court may make an exclusion order if it is satisfied that, having regard to the issues to be determined in that proceeding, the public interest in the disclosure of evidence of the identity of the informant (identifying evidence) outweighs —
(a)	any likely adverse effect of the disclosure on the informant or any other person; and
(b)	the public interest in the communication of facts and opinion to the public by the news media and, accordingly, in the ability of the news media to access sources of facts.
(3)	Without limiting the matters to which the court may have regard for the purposes of this section, it must have regard to the following —
(a)	the probative value of the identifying evidence in the proceeding;
(b)	the importance of the identifying evidence in the proceeding;
(c)	the nature and gravity of the relevant offence, cause of action or defence and the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding;
(d)	the availability of any other evidence concerning the matters to which the identifying evidence relates;
(e)	the likely effect of giving the identifying evidence, including the likelihood of harm, and the nature and extent of harm that would be caused to the informant or any other person;
(f)	the means (including any ancillary orders that may be made under section 184) available to the court to limit the harm or extent of the harm that is likely to be caused if the identifying evidence is given;
(g)	the likely effect of giving the identifying evidence in relation to —
(i)	a prosecution that has commenced but has not been finalised; or
(ii)	an investigation, of which the court is aware, into whether or not an offence has been committed;
(h)	whether the substance of the identifying evidence has already been disclosed by the informant or any other person;
(i)	the risk to national security or to the security of the State;
(j)	whether or not there was misconduct by the informant or the journalist in relation to obtaining, using, giving or receiving information.
(4)	The court may make the exclusion order subject to any conditions the court thinks fit.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20J
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 126K
182.	Special consideration: misconduct
(1)	The court may make, but is not required to make, an exclusion order if the court finds that there was misconduct by an informant or a journalist in relation to obtaining, using, giving or receiving information.
(2)	The court must have regard to the following when deciding whether or not to make an exclusion order in a case involving misconduct —
(a)	that generally an exclusion order should be made in the circumstances referred to in subsection (3);
(b)	the public interest in making or refusing to make the order.
(3)	The following are the circumstances in which generally an exclusion order should be made —
(a)	the misconduct involved the commission of an offence under The Criminal Code section 81 or a breach of a public sector standard, code of ethics or code of conduct, as those terms are defined in the Public Sector Management Act 1994 section 3(1), and the offence or breach involved the disclosure of information that —
(i)	was public interest information as defined in the Public Interest Disclosure Act 2003 section 3(1); and
(ii)	could have been, but was not, disclosed in accordance with the Public Interest Disclosure Act 2003;
(b)	the information given to the journalist could have been provided, in a way that did not constitute misconduct, to another person to deal with the concern;
(c)	the information given to the journalist could have been obtained by the journalist under the Freedom of Information Act 1992 or by other lawful means;
(d)	the misconduct involved a breach of privacy that was not warranted in the circumstances, having regard to the value to be attached to —
(i)	the privacy of information regarding private citizens generally; or
(ii)	the privacy of information regarding matters that may be commercial in confidence; or
(iii)	the principle of Cabinet confidentiality; or
(iv)	the principle of public interest immunity;
(e)	the communication made to the journalist, if published, would give rise to a risk to national security or to the security of the State.
(4)	If misconduct by a journalist or an informant is a fact in issue, the court may find that there was misconduct by the journalist or informant in relation to obtaining, using, giving or receiving information if there are reasonable grounds for making that finding.
(5)	This section does not limit —
(a)	the circumstances in which the court may make an exclusion order; or
(b)	the matters to which the court may have regard in deciding whether to make an exclusion order.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20K
183.	Application of journalist privilege to preliminary proceedings
(1)	In this section —
disclosure requirement —
(a)	means a court process or court order that requires the disclosure of information or a document; and
(b)	includes the following —
(i)	a summons or subpoena to produce documents or give evidence;
(ii)	pre-trial discovery;
(iii)	non-party discovery;
(iv)	interrogatories;
(v)	a notice to produce;
(vi)	a request to produce a document under Part 8 Division 4.
(2)	If, in response to a disclosure requirement, a person claims that the person is not compellable to answer a question or produce a document because of journalist privilege, a party who seeks disclosure of information or a document under the disclosure requirement may apply to the court for an exclusion order.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 131A
184.	Ancillary orders
Without limiting any action that the court may take to limit the possible harm, or extent of the harm, likely to be caused by the disclosure of evidence identifying an informant, the court may —
(a)	order that all or part of the evidence be heard in private; and
(b)	make any order relating to the suppression of publication of all or part of the evidence given before the court that, in its opinion, is necessary to protect the safety and welfare of the informant and is in the interests of justice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 20M
	Division 5 — Self-incrimination
185.	Terms used
In this Division —
disclosure certificate has the meaning given in section 188(7);
disclosure order —
(a)	means an order made by a court in a civil proceeding that requires a person to disclose information as part of, or in connection with, a freezing or search order made under the rules of the Supreme Court; but
(b)	does not include an order made by a court under the Criminal Property Confiscation Act 2000 or the Proceeds of Crime Act 2002 (Commonwealth);
evidence certificate has the meaning given in section 186(7);
incriminate, a person, means tend to prove that the person —
(a)	has committed an offence against or arising under an Australian law or a law of a foreign country; or
(b)	is liable to a civil penalty.
186.	Privilege in respect of self-incrimination in other proceedings
(1)	A witness may object to giving particular evidence, or evidence on a particular matter, on the ground that the evidence may incriminate the witness.
(2)	A witness is taken to have objected to giving evidence under this section if the witness expresses an unwillingness to give evidence on the ground that the evidence might incriminate the witness.
(3)	A witness may object to giving evidence under this section whether or not the witness is compellable to give that evidence.
(4)	The court must determine whether or not there are reasonable grounds for the objection.
(5)	If the court determines that there are reasonable grounds for the objection, the court —
(a)	must not require the witness to give the evidence, except under subsection (6); and
(b)	must inform the witness —
(i)	that the witness is not required to give the evidence unless the court requires it under subsection (6); and
(ii)	that the court will give the witness an evidence certificate if the witness gives the evidence (either with or without being required to do so); and
(iii)	of the effect of the evidence certificate.
(6)	The court may require the witness to give the evidence if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	the evidence does not tend to prove that the witness has committed an offence against or arising under, or is liable to a civil penalty under, a law of a foreign country; and
(b)	the interests of justice require that the witness give the evidence.
(7)	The court must cause a witness who objects to giving evidence under this section to be given a certificate (an evidence certificate) in respect of evidence if —
(a)	the court determines that there are reasonable grounds for the objection and the witness gives the evidence (whether or not after being required to do so under subsection (6)); or
(b)	the court overrules the objection but, after the evidence has been given, finds that there were reasonable grounds for the objection.
(8)	In a criminal proceeding, this section does not apply in relation to the giving of evidence by an accused, being evidence that the accused —
(a)	did an act the doing of which is a fact in issue; or
(b)	had a state of mind the existence of which is a fact in issue.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 128
187.	Effect of evidence certificate
(1)	In any proceeding in a court or before a person acting judicially (whether or not a court), the following evidence cannot be used against a person —
(a)	evidence given by the person in respect of which an evidence certificate has been given;
(b)	evidence of any information, document or thing obtained as a direct or indirect consequence of the person having given evidence in respect of which an evidence certificate has been given.
(2)	Subsection (1) —
(a)	does not apply to a criminal proceeding in respect of the falsity of the evidence; and
(b)	has effect despite any challenge, review, quashing or calling into question on any ground of the decision to give, or the validity of, the certificate concerned.
(3)	If an accused in a criminal proceeding is given an evidence certificate, subsection (1) does not apply in a proceeding that is a retrial of the accused for the same offence or a trial of the accused for an offence arising out of the same facts that gave rise to that offence.
(4)	If a person has been given a certificate under a corresponding provision in respect of evidence given in a proceeding to which the corresponding provision applies, the certificate has the same effect as an evidence certificate given under section 186.
(5)	Each of the following is a corresponding provision for the purposes of subsection (4) —
(a)	a provision of a law of a State or Territory that is substantially to the same effect as section 186;
(b)	a provision of a law of a State or Territory prescribed by the regulations to be a corresponding provision for the purposes of subsection (4).
Note for this section:
Subsections (2)(b) and (3) relate to the decision of the High Court of Australia in Cornwell v The Queen (2007) 231 CLR 260.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 128
188.	Privilege in respect of self-incrimination: disclosure orders
(1)	A person to whom a disclosure order is directed (a relevant person) may object to complying with the disclosure order on the ground that some or all of the information required to be disclosed may incriminate the person.
(2)	A relevant person who wishes to object to the disclosure of information under this section must —
(a)	disclose any information required to be disclosed to which no objection is made; and
(b)	prepare an affidavit (a privilege affidavit) containing the information required to be disclosed to which the objection is made and deliver it to the court in a sealed envelope; and
(c)	file and serve on each other party a separate affidavit setting out the basis of the objection.
(3)	The sealed envelope containing the privilege affidavit must not be opened except as directed by the court.
(4)	The court must determine whether or not there are reasonable grounds for the objection.
(5)	If the court finds that there are reasonable grounds for the objection, the court must not require the information contained in the privilege affidavit to be disclosed and must return it to the relevant person.
(6)	Despite subsection (5), the court may make an order requiring the whole or any part of the privilege affidavit to be filed and served on the parties, but only if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	the information to be disclosed does not tend to prove that the relevant person has committed an offence against or arising under, or is liable to a civil penalty under, a law of a foreign country; and
(b)	the interests of justice require the information to be disclosed.
(7)	The court must cause the relevant person to be given a certificate (a disclosure certificate) in respect of any information disclosed in the privilege affidavit (including by order under subsection (6)) that may tend to prove that the relevant person has committed an offence against or arising under, or is liable to a civil penalty under, an Australian law.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 128A
189.	Effect of disclosure certificate
(1)	In any proceeding in a court or before a person acting judicially (whether or not a court), the following evidence cannot be used against a person —
(a)	evidence of information disclosed by the person in respect of which a disclosure certificate has been given;
(b)	evidence of any information, document or thing obtained as a direct or indirect consequence of the person having disclosed that information.
(2)	Subsection (1) —
(a)	does not apply to a criminal proceeding in respect of the falsity of the evidence; and
(b)	has effect despite any challenge, review, quashing or calling into question on any ground of the decision to give, or the validity of, the certificate concerned.
(3)	Subsection (1) does not prevent the use against the person of any information disclosed by a document that —
(a)	is an annexure or exhibit to a privilege affidavit prepared by the person in response to a disclosure order; and
(b)	was in existence before the order was made.
(4)	If a person has been given a certificate under a corresponding provision in respect of information of a kind referred to in subsection (1), the certificate has the same effect as a disclosure certificate given under section 188.
(5)	Each of the following is a corresponding provision for the purposes of subsection (4) —
(a)	a provision of a law of a State or Territory that is substantially to the same effect as section 188;
(b)	a provision of a law of a State or Territory prescribed by the regulations to be a corresponding provision for the purposes of subsection (4).
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 128A
190.	Court may restrict publication of evidence
(1)	A court that causes a person to be given an evidence certificate or a disclosure certificate in respect of any evidence may make an order prohibiting the publication of a report of or relating to the evidence, or any part of the evidence, or of the giving of the certificate, if the court considers that publication of the report, or of the giving of the certificate, may tend to prejudice any prosecution that has been or may be brought against the person.
(2)	A person must not publish information in contravention of an order under subsection (1).
Penalty for this subsection: a fine of $6 000.
(3)	A person who contravenes subsection (1) —
(a)	is taken to be in contempt of the court that gave the evidence certificate or disclosure certificate; and
(b)	is punishable accordingly.
(4)	If a person’s act constitutes both an offence under subsection (2) and a contempt of court under subsection (3), the person is not liable to be punished twice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 11A
191.	Self-incrimination: witnesses in public revenue proceedings
(1)	A person who is required by a court under section 29 to give evidence in a proceeding to which that section applies may object to giving evidence, or evidence on a particular matter, on the ground that the evidence may incriminate the person.
(2)	If the court determines there are no reasonable grounds for the objection, the court may require the person to give the evidence.
(3)	If the court determines there are reasonable grounds for the objection, the court may require the person to give the evidence if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	the evidence does not tend to prove that the person has committed an offence against or arising under, or is liable to a civil penalty under, a law of the Commonwealth, another State, a Territory or a foreign country; and
(b)	it is in the public interest that the person give the evidence.
(4)	The court must, on application, cause the person to be given a certificate (an immunity certificate) in respect of any matter in relation to which the person gives evidence after being required to do so by the court if, in the opinion of the court, the evidence the person was required to give incriminates the person.
(5)	If an immunity certificate is given to the person —
(a)	the person is not liable for any offence against or arising under, or a civil penalty under, a law of this State in respect of a matter specified in the immunity certificate; and
(b)	a prosecution for an offence against or arising under, or a proceeding for a civil penalty under, a law of this State cannot be instituted or continued against the person for a matter specified in the immunity certificate.
(6)	An immunity certificate does not extend —
(a)	to an act or omission that occurs after the evidence is given in the proceeding in which the immunity certificate is given; or
(b)	to a criminal proceeding in respect of the falsity of evidence given in the proceeding in which the immunity certificate is given.
(7)	Sections 186 and 187 do not apply to a person who is required by a court to give evidence under section 29.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 13
	Division 6 — Evidence excluded in the public interest
192.	Exclusion of evidence of reasons for judicial and other decisions
(1)	A judge in an Australian or overseas proceeding, or an arbitrator in respect of a dispute submitted to arbitration (whether to 1 or more arbitrators), and any other person to whom this subsection extends, must not give evidence of the following —
(a)	the reasons for the decision made by the judge or arbitrator in the proceeding or arbitration;
(b)	the deliberations of the judge or arbitrator in relation to that decision.
(2)	Subsection (1) extends to a person who was, in relation to a proceeding or arbitration, under the direction or control of the judge or arbitrator.
(3)	Evidence that a person must not give under subsection (1) or (2) also must not be given by tendering as evidence any document prepared by the person.
(4)	This section does not prevent the admission or use, in a proceeding, of published reasons for a decision.
(5)	In a proceeding, evidence of the reasons for a decision made by a member of a jury in another Australian or overseas proceeding, or of the deliberations of a member of a jury in relation to the decision, must not be given by any of the members of that jury.
(6)	This section does not apply in a proceeding that is —
(a)	a prosecution for 1 or more of the following offences —
(i)	an offence against The Criminal Code Chapter XVI or XVII;
(ii)	an offence connected with an offence mentioned in subparagraph (i), including an offence of attempting to commit, conspiring with another person to commit, inciting another person to commit or attempting to procure another person to commit an offence mentioned in subparagraph (i);
or
(b)	in respect of a contempt of court; or
(c)	by way of appeal from, or judicial review of, a judgment, decree, order or sentence of a court; or
(d)	by way of review of an arbitral award; or
(e)	a civil proceeding in respect of an act of a judicial officer or arbitrator that was, and that was known at the time by the judicial officer or arbitrator to be, outside the scope of the matters in relation to which the judicial officer or arbitrator had authority to act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 129
193.	Exclusion of evidence of matters of state
(1)	If the public interest in admitting into evidence information or a document that relates to matters of state is outweighed by the public interest in preserving secrecy or confidentiality in relation to the information or document, the court may direct that the information or document not be adduced as evidence.
(2)	The court may give the direction either on its own initiative or on the application of any person (whether or not the person is a party).
(3)	In deciding whether to give the direction, the court may inform itself in any way it thinks fit.
(4)	Without limiting the circumstances in which information or a document may be taken for the purposes of subsection (1) to relate to matters of state, the information or document is taken for the purposes of that subsection to relate to matters of state if adducing it as evidence would —
(a)	prejudice the security, defence or international relations of Australia; or
(b)	damage relations between the Commonwealth and a State or a Territory or between States, Territories or a State and Territory; or
(c)	prejudice the prevention, investigation or prosecution of an offence; or
(d)	prejudice the prevention or investigation of, or the conduct of proceedings for recovery of civil penalties brought with respect to, other contraventions of the law; or
(e)	disclose, or enable a person to ascertain, the existence or identity of a confidential source of information relating to the enforcement or administration of a law of the Commonwealth, a State or a Territory; or
(f)	prejudice the proper functioning of the government of the Commonwealth, a State or a Territory.
(5)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account for the purposes of subsection (1), the court must take into account the following matters —
(a)	the importance of the information or the document in the proceeding;
(b)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding — whether the party seeking to adduce evidence of the information or document is an accused or the prosecutor;
(c)	the nature of the offence, cause of action or defence to which the information or document relates, and the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding;
(d)	the likely effect of adducing evidence of the information or document, and the means available to limit its publication;
(e)	whether the substance of the information or document has already been published;
(f)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding and the party seeking to adduce evidence of the information or document is an accused — whether the direction is to be made subject to the condition that the prosecution be stayed.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 130
194.	Exclusion of evidence of settlement negotiations
(1)	In this section —
dispute means a dispute of a kind in respect of which relief may be given in an Australian or overseas proceeding;
power means a power conferred by or under an Australian law.
(2)	Evidence must not be adduced of —
(a)	a communication that is made between persons in dispute, or between 1 or more persons in dispute and a third party, in connection with an attempt to negotiate a settlement of the dispute; or
(b)	a document (whether delivered or not) that has been prepared in connection with an attempt to negotiate a settlement of a dispute.
(3)	Subsection (2) does not apply if —
(a)	the persons in dispute consent to the evidence being adduced in the proceeding concerned or, if any of those persons has tendered the communication or document in evidence in another Australian or overseas proceeding, all the other persons consent; or
(b)	the substance of the evidence has been disclosed with the express or implied consent of all the persons in dispute; or
(c)	the substance of the evidence has been partly disclosed with the express or implied consent of the persons in dispute, and full disclosure of the evidence is reasonably necessary to enable a proper understanding of the other evidence that has already been adduced; or
(d)	the communication or document included a statement to the effect that it was not to be treated as confidential; or
(e)	the evidence tends to contradict or to qualify evidence that has already been admitted about the course of an attempt to settle the dispute; or
(f)	the proceeding in which it is sought to adduce the evidence is a proceeding to enforce an agreement between the persons in dispute to settle the dispute, or a proceeding in which the making of the agreement is in issue; or
(g)	evidence that has been adduced in the proceeding, or an inference from evidence that has been adduced in the proceeding, is likely to mislead the court unless evidence of the communication or document is adduced to contradict or to qualify that evidence; or
(h)	the communication or document is relevant to determining liability for costs; or
(i)	making the communication, or preparing the document, affects a right of a person; or
(j)	the communication was made, or the document was prepared, in furtherance of the commission of a fraud or an offence or the commission of an act that renders a person liable to a civil penalty; or
(k)	a person in dispute, or an employee or agent of a person in dispute, knew or ought reasonably to have known that the communication was made, or the document was prepared, in furtherance of a deliberate abuse of a power.
(4)	If the commission of a fraud, offence or act referred to in subsection (3)(j) is a fact in issue, a court may find that a communication was made or a document was prepared in furtherance of the commission of a fraud, offence or act if there are reasonable grounds for finding that —
(a)	the fraud, offence or act was committed; and
(b)	the communication was made or document was prepared in furtherance of the commission of the fraud, offence or act.
(5)	If an abuse of power referred to in subsection (3)(k) is a fact in issue, the court may find that a communication was made or a document was prepared in furtherance of an abuse of power if there are reasonable grounds for that finding.
(6)	This section does not apply to a communication made, or a document prepared, in connection with an attempt to negotiate the settlement of a criminal proceeding or an anticipated criminal proceeding.
(7)	For the purposes of this section —
(a)	a communication made by an employee or agent of a person is taken to have been made by the person; and
(b)	a consent given by an employee or agent of a person is taken to have been given by the person if the employee or agent is authorised to consent.
(8)	A reference in this section to doing an act includes a reference to failing to do that act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 131
	Division 7 — General
195.	Court to inform of rights to make applications and objections
If it appears to the court that a witness or a party may have grounds for making an application or objection under a provision of this Part, the court must satisfy itself (if there is a jury, in the absence of the jury) that the witness or party is aware of the effect of that provision.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 132
196.	Court may inspect documents
If a question arises under this Part in relation to a document, the court may order that the document be produced to it and may inspect the document for the purpose of determining the question.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 133
197.	Inadmissibility of evidence that must not be adduced or given
Evidence that, because of this Part, must not be adduced or given in a proceeding is not admissible in the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 134
198.	Derogation of privilege in civil proceedings
(1)	Rules of court relating to the use of expert evidence in a civil proceeding may provide for the derogation of any privilege under this Act that would otherwise attach to documents prepared —
(a)	for the purpose of pending or contemplated proceedings; or
(b)	in connection with the obtaining or giving of legal advice.
(2)	A privilege under this Act does not apply to the extent provided for by rules of court referred to in subsection (1).
(3)	Without limiting the generality of subsection (1) or any other power to make rules of court, rules of court relating to the use of expert evidence in a civil proceeding may make provision for —
(a)	the disclosure, by providing copies of reports or otherwise, of the nature and substance of expert evidence to be given in a civil proceeding; and
(b)	the exclusion of expert evidence because of non-compliance with the rules of court relating to disclosure of expert evidence or non-compliance with any order for the disclosure of the nature and substance of expert evidence; and
(c)	the conferral of a discretion on the court, tribunal or other person or body dealing with the proceeding in relation to the matters referred to in paragraphs (a) and (b).
(4)	This section extends to the following proceedings —
(a)	an arbitration;
(b)	a civil proceeding before a WA tribunal.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 32A
199.	No privilege against self-incrimination for bodies corporate
(1)	This section applies if, under a law of the State or in a proceeding to which this Act applies, a body corporate is required to —
(a)	answer a question or give information; or
(b)	produce a document or any other thing; or
(c)	do any other act whatever.
(2)	The body corporate is not entitled to refuse or fail to comply with the requirement on the ground that answering the question, giving the information, producing the document or other thing or doing that other act, as the case may be, might tend to incriminate the body or make the body liable to a penalty.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 187
200.	No confessional privilege in proceedings relating to child sexual offences or sexual abuse
(1)	In this section —
religious confession means a confession made by a person to a minister of religion acting in the minister’s capacity as a minister of religion and in accordance with the tenets of the minister’s faith or religion.
(2)	A person is not entitled to refuse or fail to give evidence on the ground that giving the evidence would require the person to disclose that a religious confession was made to the person, the contents of a religious confession or information gained in connection with a religious confession in the following proceedings —
(a)	a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence if the complainant was a child at the time of the offence;
(b)	a civil proceeding in which it is alleged a person is the victim of sexual abuse if the person was a child at the time of the sexual abuse.
(3)	This section does not affect the operation of any privilege conferred by this Part.
	Part 5 — Exclusion of evidence
201.	General discretion to exclude evidence
A court may refuse to admit evidence if its probative value is substantially outweighed by the danger that the evidence might —
(a)	be unfairly prejudicial to a party; or
(b)	be misleading or confusing; or
(c)	cause or result in undue waste of time.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 135
202.	General discretion to limit use of evidence
A court may limit the use to be made of evidence if there is a danger that a particular use of the evidence might —
(a)	be unfairly prejudicial to a party; or
(b)	be misleading or confusing.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 136
203.	Exclusion of prejudicial evidence in criminal proceeding
In a criminal proceeding, the court must refuse to admit evidence adduced by the prosecutor if its probative value is outweighed by the danger of unfair prejudice to the accused.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 137
204.	Exclusion of improperly or illegally obtained evidence
(1)	The following evidence must not be admitted unless the desirability of admitting the evidence outweighs the undesirability of admitting evidence that has been obtained in the way in which the evidence was obtained —
(a)	evidence obtained improperly or in contravention of an Australian law;
(b)	evidence obtained in consequence of an impropriety or of a contravention of an Australian law.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), evidence of an admission that was made during or in consequence of questioning, and evidence obtained in consequence of the admission, are taken to have been obtained improperly if the person conducting the questioning —
(a)	did, or omitted to do, an act in the course of the questioning even though the person knew or ought reasonably to have known that the act or omission was likely to impair substantially the ability of the person being questioned to respond rationally to the questioning; or
(b)	made a false statement in the course of the questioning even though the person knew or ought reasonably to have known that the statement was false and that making the false statement was likely to cause the person who was being questioned to make an admission.
(3)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account under subsection (1), it must take into account the following —
(a)	the probative value of the evidence;
(b)	the importance of the evidence in the proceeding;
(c)	the nature of the relevant offence, cause of action or defence and the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding;
(d)	the gravity of the impropriety or contravention;
(e)	whether the impropriety or contravention was deliberate or careless;
(f)	whether the impropriety or contravention was contrary to or inconsistent with a right of a person recognised by the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights;
(g)	whether any other proceeding (whether or not in a court) has been or is likely to be taken in relation to the impropriety or contravention;
(h)	the difficulty (if any) of obtaining the evidence without impropriety or contravention of an Australian law.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 138
205.	Cautioning of persons
(1)	For the purposes of section 204(1)(a), evidence of a statement made or an act done by a person during questioning is taken to have been obtained improperly if —
(a)	the person was under arrest for an offence at the time; and
(b)	the questioning was conducted by an investigating official who was at the time empowered, because of the office that the official held, to arrest the person; and
(c)	before starting the questioning the investigating official did not caution the person that the person does not have to say or do anything but that anything the person does say or do may be used in evidence.
(2)	For the purposes of section 204(1)(a), evidence of a statement made or an act done by a person during questioning is taken to have been obtained improperly if —
(a)	the questioning was conducted by an investigating official who did not have the power to arrest the person; and
(b)	the statement was made, or the act was done, after the investigating official formed a belief that there was sufficient evidence to establish that the person has committed an offence; and
(c)	the investigating official did not, before the statement was made or the act was done, caution the person that the person does not have to say or do anything but that anything the person does say or do may be used in evidence.
(3)	The caution must be given in, or translated into, a language in which the person is able to communicate with reasonable fluency, but need not be given in writing unless the person cannot hear adequately.
(4)	Subsections (1), (2) and (3) do not apply so far as any Australian law requires the person to answer questions put by, or do things required by, the investigating official.
(5)	A reference in subsection (1) to a person who is under arrest includes a reference to a person who is in the company of an investigating official for the purpose of being questioned, if —
(a)	the official believes that there is sufficient evidence to establish that the person has committed an offence that is to be the subject of the questioning; or
(b)	the official would not allow the person to leave if the person wished to do so; or
(c)	the official has given the person reasonable grounds for believing that the person would not be allowed to leave if the person wished to do so.
(6)	A person is not treated as being under arrest only because of subsection (5) if —
(a)	the official is performing functions in relation to persons or goods entering or leaving Australia and the official does not believe the person has committed an offence against a law of the Commonwealth; or
(b)	the official is exercising a power under an Australian law to detain and search the person or to require the person to provide information or to answer questions.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 139
206.	Exclusion of certain evidence relating to complainant or witness
(1)	The following evidence must not be adduced by or on behalf of an accused in a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence —
(a)	evidence relating to the sexual reputation of the complainant;
(b)	evidence relating to the disposition of the complainant in sexual matters.
(2)	In a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence, evidence relating to the sexual experiences of the complainant, of any kind, at any time and with any person, other than the acts or omissions that constitute the offence, must not be adduced by or on behalf of an accused except with the leave of the court.
(3)	The following evidence must not be adduced in a civil proceeding in which it is alleged that a witness is the victim of sexual abuse or sexual harassment —
(a)	evidence relating to the sexual reputation of the witness;
(b)	evidence relating to the disposition of the witness in sexual matters.
(4)	In a civil proceeding in which it is alleged that a witness is the victim of sexual abuse or sexual harassment, evidence relating to the sexual experiences of the witness, of any kind, at any time and with any person, other than the acts or omissions that are the subject of the alleged sexual abuse or sexual harassment, must not be adduced except with the leave of the court.
(5)	An application for leave of the court mentioned in subsection (2) or (4) must be heard in the absence of the jury (if any).
(6)	The court must not grant leave under subsection (2) or (4) unless satisfied that —
(a)	the evidence has substantial probative value; and
(b)	the probative value of the evidence outweighs any distress, humiliation or embarrassment that may be caused to the complainant or witness as a result of the admission of the evidence.
(7)	This section does not affect the operation of section 50.
(8)	Evidence that, because of this section, must not be adduced or given in a proceeding is not admissible in the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 36A, 36B, 36BA and 36BC
	Part 6 — Special arrangements for taking evidence
	Division 1 — Use of video links and audio links by courts and recognised courts
207.	Terms used
In this Division —
corresponding legislation means any provisions of an Act of another State or a Territory that substantially correspond with this Division;
participating jurisdiction means —
(a)	another State or a Territory in which corresponding legislation is in force; or
(b)	any jurisdiction outside the State prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this definition to be a participating jurisdiction;
recognised court means a court or tribunal of a participating jurisdiction —
(a)	that is authorised by corresponding legislation to direct that evidence be taken or a submission be made by video link or audio link from this State; or
(b)	that is prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this definition to be a recognised court.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 120
208.	Court may take evidence by video link or audio link
(1)	A court may, by order, direct that evidence be taken or a submission be received by video link or audio link from a person at a place, whether in or outside this State, that is outside the courtroom or other place where the court is sitting.
(2)	The order may be made —
(a)	on the application of a party to the proceeding; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(3)	The court must not make the order unless satisfied that the video link or audio link is available or can reasonably be made available.
(4)	The court must not make the order if satisfied that the direction is not in the interests of justice.
(5)	For the purposes of taking evidence or receiving a submission by video link or audio link from a place in this State in accordance with the direction, the place is taken to be part of the court.
(6)	For the purposes of taking evidence or receiving a submission by video link or audio link from a place in a participating jurisdiction, the court may exercise in that place any of its powers that the court is permitted, under the law of the jurisdiction, to exercise in that place.
(7)	This section extends to a proceeding before a WA tribunal.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 121
209.	Counsel entitled to practise
(1)	A person who is entitled to practise as the equivalent of legal counsel in a participating jurisdiction is entitled to practise as a legal practitioner in relation to —
(a)	the examination, cross-examination or re-examination of a witness in the participating jurisdiction whose evidence is being given by video link or audio link before a court; and
(b)	the making of a submission by video link or audio link from the participating jurisdiction in a proceeding before a court.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a WA tribunal.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 122
210.	Recognised court may take evidence or receive submission in this State
(1)	A recognised court may, for the purposes of a proceeding before it, take evidence or receive a submission by video link or audio link from a person in this State.
(2)	For the purposes of the proceeding the recognised court may exercise in this State any of its powers in relation to the taking of evidence or the receipt of a submission except its powers —
(a)	to punish for contempt; and
(b)	to enforce or execute its orders or process.
(3)	The laws of the participating jurisdiction (including rules of court) that apply to the proceeding in that jurisdiction also apply, by force of this subsection, to the practice and procedure of the recognised court in taking evidence or receiving a submission by video link or audio link from a person in this State.
(4)	For the purposes of the court exercising its powers under subsection (2), the place in this State where the evidence is given or the submission is made is taken to be part of the court.
(5)	Without limiting subsection (2), the recognised court may, by order —
(a)	direct that the proceeding, or a part of the proceeding, be conducted in private; or
(b)	require a person to leave a place in this State where the giving of evidence or the making of a submission is taking place or is going to take place; or
(c)	prohibit or restrict the publication of evidence given in the proceeding or of the name of a party to, or a witness in, the proceeding.
(6)	An order made by a recognised court under subsection (5) may be enforced by the Supreme Court as if it were an order of the Supreme Court.
(7)	Without limiting subsection (6), a person who contravenes the order —
(a)	is taken to be in contempt of the Supreme Court; and
(b)	is punishable accordingly.
(8)	Subsection (7) does not apply if the person establishes that the contravention should be excused.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 123, 124, 125 and 126
211.	Privileges, protection and immunity of participants in proceeding in recognised court
(1)	A judge in a proceeding before a recognised court has, in connection with evidence being taken or a submission being received by video link or audio link from a person in this State, the same privileges, protection and immunity as a judge of the Supreme Court.
(2)	A person appearing as legal counsel in a proceeding before a recognised court (including a person entitled to practise under section 209(1)) has, in connection with evidence being taken or a submission being received by video link or audio link from a person in this State, the same protection and immunity as a legal practitioner has in appearing for a party in a proceeding in the Supreme Court.
(3)	A person appearing as a witness in a proceeding before a recognised court by video link or audio link from this State has the same protection as a witness in a proceeding in the Supreme Court.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 127
212.	Recognised court may administer oath in this State
(1)	A recognised court may, for the purpose of obtaining by video link or audio link the testimony of a person in this State in a proceeding, administer an oath or affirmation in accordance with the practice and procedure of the recognised court.
(2)	Evidence given by a person on oath or affirmation is, for the purposes of The Criminal Code, testimony given in a judicial proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 128
213.	Assistance to recognised court
An officer of a court or WA tribunal may, at the request of a recognised court —
(a)	attend at the place in the State where evidence is to be or is being given, or submissions are to be or are being made, in a proceeding before the recognised court; and
(b)	take action as the recognised court directs to facilitate the proceeding; and
(c)	assist with the administering by the recognised court of an oath or affirmation.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 129
214.	Contempt of recognised court
(1)	A person must not, while evidence is being given or a submission is being made in this State by video link or audio link in a proceeding before a recognised court —
(a)	threaten, intimidate or insult any of the following —
(i)	a judge in the proceeding;
(ii)	a master, registrar, deputy registrar or other officer of that court who is taking part in or assisting in the proceeding;
(iii)	a person appearing in the proceeding as legal counsel (including a person entitled to practise under section 209(1));
(iv)	a witness in the proceeding;
(v)	a juror in the proceeding;
or
(b)	interrupt or obstruct the proceeding; or
(c)	without lawful excuse disobey an order or direction of the court.
(2)	A person who contravenes subsection (1) —
(a)	is taken to be in contempt of the Supreme Court; and
(b)	is punishable accordingly.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 130
215.	Operation of other laws
(1)	This Division does not affect the operation of any other law of the State that makes provision for the taking of evidence or the making of a submission —
(a)	in the State for the purpose of a proceeding in the State; or
(b)	outside the State for the purpose of a proceeding in the State; or
(c)	in the State for the purpose of a proceeding outside the State.
(2)	This Division does not affect the operation of Part 7.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 132
	Division 2 — Examination of witnesses outside the State
216.	Terms used
(1)	In this Division —
examination includes any proceeding for the taking of evidence of a person conducted by a judicial authority of a foreign country in relation to a request issued as a result of an order made by a court under this Act;
extra-territorial examination order has the meaning given in section 217(1);
inferior court means a court, except when exercising federal jurisdiction, not being a superior court;
judicial authority, in relation to a place outside the State, means a court or person prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this definition as an appropriate judicial authority for that place.
(2)	A reference in this Division to a place outside the State is a reference to a place outside the State whether within or outside Australia.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 109
217.	Court may make extra-territorial examination order
(1)	A court may make any of the following orders (each an extra-territorial examination order) in relation to any person outside the State —
(a)	an order for the examination of the person on oath or affirmation at any place outside the State before a judge of the court, an officer of the court or any other person the court appoints;
(b)	an order for the issue of a commission for the examination of the person on oath or affirmation at any place outside the State;
(c)	an order for the issue of a request to a judicial authority of a place outside the State to take, or to cause to be taken, the evidence of the person.
(2)	An extra-territorial examination order may be made —
(a)	only by a superior court; and
(b)	only if it appears to the court that it is in the interests of justice to do so.
(3)	A superior court may make an extra-territorial examination order for the purpose of any proceeding before the superior court, on application by a party to the proceeding.
(4)	The Supreme Court may also make an extra-territorial examination order for the purpose of any proceeding before an inferior court, on application by a party to the proceeding.
(5)	In determining whether it is in the interests of justice to make an extra-territorial examination order, the matters to which the court must have regard include the following —
(a)	whether the person is willing or able to come to the State to give evidence in the proceeding;
(b)	whether the person will be able to give evidence material to any fact in issue in the proceeding;
(c)	whether, having regard to the interests of the parties to the proceeding, justice will be better served by granting or refusing the order.
(6)	If a court makes an extra-territorial examination order of the kind referred to in subsection (1)(a) or (b), the court may, at the time of the making of the order or at a subsequent time, give directions relating to the procedure to be followed in and in relation to the examination, including directions as to the time, place and manner of the examination, and to any other matter that the court thinks relevant.
(7)	If a court makes an extra-territorial examination order of the kind referred to in subsection (1)(c), the court may include in the order a request as to any matter relating to the taking of evidence from the person concerned, including any of the following matters —
(a)	the examination, cross-examination or re-examination of the person, whether the evidence of the person is given orally, on affidavit or otherwise;
(b)	the attendance of the legal counsel of each party to the proceeding and the participation of those persons in the examination in appropriate circumstances;
(c)	any matter prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 110 and 111
218.	Admission of evidence taken under extra-territorial examination order
(1)	A court may permit a party to tender the following evidence in a proceeding —
(a)	evidence of a person taken in an examination held as a result of an extra-territorial examination order that was made for the purpose of that proceeding;
(b)	a record of the evidence referred to in paragraph (a).
(2)	However, evidence of a person must not be admitted under this section if —
(a)	at the hearing of the proceeding the court is satisfied that the person is in the State and is able to attend the hearing; or
(b)	the evidence would not have been admissible had it been given or produced at the hearing of the proceeding.
(3)	The court may exclude from the proceeding evidence taken in an examination held as a result of an extra-territorial examination order, even though it is otherwise admissible, if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
(4)	If evidence is admitted under this section, the hearsay rule does not apply to evidence of a previous representation taken in an examination, but only if the evidence would have been admissible had it been given or produced at the hearing of the proceeding.
(5)	This section extends to the following evidence —
(a)	a document produced at an examination;
(b)	answers made, whether in writing, or orally and reduced to writing, to any written interrogatories presented at an examination.
(6)	This section does not affect the functions of the court under Part 5.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 110, 111 and 112
219.	Operation of other laws
This Division does not affect the operation of any other law of the State that makes provision for the examination of witnesses outside the State for the purpose of a proceeding in the State.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 113
220.	Regulations and rules of court
(1)	The regulations may make further provision for extra-territorial examination orders and, in particular, for the practice and procedure of a superior court in a proceeding for the making of an extra-territorial examination order.
(2)	The power of a person or body to make rules of court extends to making rules, not inconsistent with this Act or the regulations, regulating the practice and procedure of a superior court in a proceeding for the making of an extra-territorial examination order.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 114
	Division 3 — Assistance to other Australian courts and foreign courts
221.	Terms used
(1)	In this Division —
assistance application has the meaning given in section 222(1);
assistance order has the meaning given in section 223(1);
requesting court means a court or tribunal that issues a request or on behalf of which a request is issued, as referred to in section 223(1)(a).
(2)	A reference in this Division to a proceeding before a court or tribunal includes a reference to a proceeding the institution of which before a court or tribunal is contemplated.
(3)	A reference in this Division to a request issued by or on behalf of a court or tribunal includes a reference to any commission, order or other process issued by or on behalf of a court or tribunal.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 115
