222.	Application to Supreme Court for assistance
(1)	This Division applies if an application (an assistance application) is made to the Supreme Court for assistance in obtaining evidence in the State for the purposes of a proceeding before a court or tribunal exercising jurisdiction in a place outside the State.
(2)	This Division applies only if the proceeding to which the assistance application relates is —
(a)	a criminal proceeding before a court exercising jurisdiction in a place outside the State that is in Australia or New Zealand; or
(b)	a civil proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 116
223.	Supreme Court may make order to assist in obtaining evidence
(1)	The Supreme Court may, on an assistance application, make an order (an assistance order) for obtaining evidence in the State if satisfied that —
(a)	the assistance application is made for the purposes of a request issued by or on behalf of a court or tribunal exercising jurisdiction in a place outside the State; and
(b)	the evidence to which the assistance application relates is to be obtained for the purposes of a proceeding before the requesting court.
(2)	An assistance order may include any provisions the Supreme Court considers appropriate for the purpose of giving effect to the request to which the assistance application relates.
(3)	An assistance order may require a specified person to take any steps the Supreme Court considers appropriate for the purpose of giving effect to the request to which the assistance application relates.
(4)	Without limiting subsection (2) or (3), an assistance order may make provision for the following —
(a)	the examination of witnesses, either orally or in writing;
(b)	the production of documents;
(c)	the inspection, photographing, preservation, custody or detention of any property;
(d)	the taking of samples of any property and the carrying out of any experiments on or with any property;
(e)	the medical examination of any person;
(f)	without limiting paragraph (e), the taking and testing of samples of blood from any person.
(5)	An assistance order must not include a requirement unless that requirement could be made to obtain evidence for the purposes of a proceeding in the Supreme Court (whether or not a proceeding of the same description as the proceeding to which the assistance application relates).
(6)	Subsection (5) does not prevent the making of an order requiring a person to give testimony (either orally or in writing) otherwise than on oath or affirmation if that is requested by the requesting court.
(7)	An assistance order must not require a person —
(a)	to state what documents relevant to the proceeding to which the assistance application relates are or have been in the person’s possession, custody or power; or
(b)	to produce any documents other than particular documents specified in the order and appearing to the Supreme Court to be, or to be likely to be, in the person’s possession, custody or power.
(8)	A person who is required to attend any place under an assistance order is entitled to any allowances and expenses that the person would be entitled to on attendance as a witness in a proceeding before the Supreme Court.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 116 and 117
224.	Privilege of witnesses
(1)	A person must not be compelled by an assistance order to give any evidence for the purpose of a proceeding before a requesting court if the person could not be compelled to give the evidence —
(a)	in a similar proceeding in the State; or
(b)	in a similar proceeding in the place in which the requesting court exercises jurisdiction.
(2)	Subsection (1)(b) does not apply unless the claim of the person to be exempt from giving evidence is either —
(a)	supported by a statement contained in the request of the requesting court (whether unconditionally or subject to conditions); or
(b)	conceded by the applicant for the assistance order.
(3)	If a claim by a person to be exempt from giving evidence is not supported or conceded as referred to in subsection (2), the person may (subject to subsection (1)(a)) be required to give the evidence to which the claim relates but that evidence must not be transmitted to the requesting court unless —
(a)	the claim is referred to the requesting court; and
(b)	the requesting court dismisses the claim.
(4)	In this section —
(a)	a reference to giving evidence includes a reference to producing any document or thing; and
(b)	a reference to transmitting evidence given includes a reference to transmitting any document or thing that is produced.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 118
225.	Rules of court
(1)	The Supreme Court may make rules of court under the Supreme Court Act 1935 for or with respect to the following —
(a)	the manner in which an assistance application must be made;
(b)	the circumstances in which an assistance order may be made;
(c)	the manner in which a claim of a person to be exempt from giving evidence under an assistance order must be made;
(d)	any other matters that are necessary or convenient for carrying out or giving effect to this Division.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not limit the rule-making powers conferred by the Supreme Court Act 1935.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 118A
226.	Offence of giving false testimony
A person who, in giving any testimony (either orally or in writing) otherwise than on oath or affirmation when required to do so by an assistance order, makes a statement that the person knows is false or that the person does not believe is true commits a crime.
Penalty: imprisonment for 7 years.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 118B
227.	Operation of other laws
This Division does not affect the operation of any other law of the State that makes provision for the taking of evidence in the State for the purpose of a proceeding outside the State.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 118C
	Part 7 — Witness assistance measures
	Division 1 — Preliminary
228.	Terms used
(1)	In this Part —
affected child, in relation to a child protection proceeding, means the child in respect of whom an application under the Children and Community Services Act 2004 Part 4 is made;
alter —
(a)	a recording, includes edit the recording; and
(b)	a transcript of a recording, includes edit the transcript;
child protection proceeding —
(a)	means protection proceedings as defined in the Children and Community Services Act 2004 section 3; and
(b)	includes any related proceedings and any protection proceedings dealt with under Part 5 of that Act;
destroy, a recording, includes —
(a)	erase, delete or obliterate all or any part of the recording; and
(b)	render any images or sounds recorded in the recording irrecoverable;
out-of-court representation has the meaning given in section 235;
pre-recording procedure means a procedure by which a witness’s evidence in a proceeding is given at a special hearing (as defined in section 261) and recorded on a visual recording that is later presented to the court;
recorded interview has the meaning given in section 251;
recorded statement has the meaning given in section 239;
special procedure for giving evidence means any of the following procedures for giving evidence —
(a)	a pre-recording procedure;
(b)	a video link separation procedure;
(c)	a screening procedure;
special witness means a witness in a proceeding —
(a)	who must be treated as a special witness under section 229; or
(b)	who the court declares to be a special witness under section 230;
visual recording —
(a)	means any recording on any medium from which a moving image may be produced by any means; and
(b)	includes the accompanying soundtrack;
witness assessment has the meaning given in section 289(1);
witness intermediary means a person appointed as a witness intermediary for a witness in a proceeding under section 291(1).
(2)	A provision of this Part that applies to a child protection proceeding applies as if —
(a)	a reference to an accused were a reference to any party to the proceeding, other than the affected child or an applicant under the Children and Community Services Act 2004 Part 4 or 5 who is an officer as defined in section 3 of that Act; and
(b)	a reference to a complainant were a reference to the affected child.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106A
229.	Witnesses who must be treated as special witness
(1)	In a criminal proceeding, a witness must be treated as a special witness —
(a)	if the proceeding is a proceeding in a superior court for a sexual offence and the witness is the complainant; or
(b)	if the proceeding is for a criminal or identified organisation offence and the witness is —
(i)	the complainant; or
(ii)	any witness for the prosecution;
or
(c)	if the proceeding is a proceeding in a superior court for a family violence offence and the witness is the complainant; or
(d)	if the witness has a mental impairment that affects the witness’s ability to give evidence; or
(e)	in any other circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph.
(2)	A power to specify circumstances conferred by subsection (1)(e) includes a power to specify circumstances by reference to any of the following —
(a)	the type of offence to which the proceeding relates;
(b)	the characteristics of the witness;
(c)	the court in which the proceedings are heard.
(3)	In deciding whether a witness must be treated as a special witness under subsection (1)(d) or (e), a court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
230.	Witnesses who may be declared to be special witness
(1)	A court may, by order, declare a witness to be a special witness in a proceeding if the court is of the opinion that, unless the witness is treated as a special witness, the witness —
(a)	is unlikely to be able to give evidence, or to give evidence satisfactorily, because of a disability; or
(b)	is likely to be intimidated or distressed, because of age, cultural background, relationship to any party in the proceeding, the nature of the subject matter of the evidence or any other factor that the court considers relevant.
(2)	An order under this section may be made —
(a)	on application by a party to the proceeding, on notice to the other parties; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(3)	The court must be satisfied that it is in the interests of justice to make the order.
(4)	The court may at any time vary or revoke an order under this section.
(5)	If the proceeding in which a person is declared to be a special witness is a proceeding before a jury, the court must instruct the jury that —
(a)	the making of the declaration is a routine practice of the court; and
(b)	the jury should not draw any inference as to the guilt of an accused or the culpability of a party from the making of the declaration on the court’s own initiative.
(6)	In deciding whether to make, vary or revoke an order declaring a witness to be a special witness, the court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
(7)	This section does not apply in respect of a witness if the witness must be treated as a special witness under section 229.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106R
	Division 2 — Support and communication assistance for children and special witnesses
231.	Use of support person
(1)	The following witnesses are entitled, while giving evidence in a proceeding, to have a person nearby to provide the witness with support —
(a)	a witness who is a child when the proceeding commences;
(b)	a special witness.
(2)	The person who provides support must be a person approved by the court and must not be a person who is a witness in, or a party to, the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106E and 106R(4)(a)
232.	Use of support animal
(1)	The following witnesses may, with the approval of the court, have an animal (a support animal) nearby to provide the witness with support while the witness is giving evidence —
(a)	a witness who is a child when the proceeding commences;
(b)	a special witness.
(2)	The use of the support animal must comply with any requirements imposed by the court or prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this subsection.
(3)	This section does not affect any entitlement that a person with a disability has to be accompanied by an assistance animal as defined in the Disability Discrimination Act 1992 (Commonwealth) section 9(2).
233.	Use of communicator
(1)	A court may, on its own initiative or on the application of a party, appoint a person to act as a communicator for a witness while the witness is giving evidence if —
(a)	the witness is a child when the proceeding commences or a special witness; and
(b)	the court considers it in the interests of justice to do so.
(2)	The functions of the person acting as a communicator are, if requested by the judge, to communicate and explain —
(a)	to the witness, questions put to the witness; and
(b)	to the court, evidence given by the witness.
(3)	The use of a communicator must comply with any requirements imposed by the court or prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this subsection.
(4)	The court may appoint any person it considers suitable and competent to act as a communicator for a witness under this section, including a family member or carer.
(5)	It is not necessary for the person appointed as a communicator to have any particular training, study or experience.
(6)	A person appointed to act as a communicator must either take an oath or make an affirmation, in a form directed by the court, that the person will exercise the person’s functions as a communicator to the best of the person’s ability.
(7)	A person who, while performing or purporting to perform a function as a communicator under this section, makes any statement the person knows to be false or misleading to a witness or the court commits a crime.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 5 years.
Summary conviction penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106F and 106R(4)(b)
	Division 3 — Admission of out-of-court representations
234.	Application of Division
This Division applies to the following proceedings —
(a)	a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence if the complainant is a child when the proceeding commences;
(b)	a criminal proceeding for a family violence offence if the complainant is a child when the proceeding commences;
(c)	a child protection proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106H and Schedule 7
235.	Term used: out-of-court representation
In this Division —
out-of-court representation —
(a)	means a representation that relates to a fact in issue in a proceeding to which this Division applies and that was made by a person to another person before the proceeding commenced, whether or not it is recorded; but
(b)	does not include —
(i)	a recorded statement that is admissible under Division 4; or
(ii)	a recorded interview that is admissible under Division 5.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106H(3)
236.	Admission of out-of-court representations
(1)	A court may admit into evidence an out-of-court representation made by a complainant if —
(a)	a transcript of the out-of-court representation or, if the out-of-court representation is not recorded in writing or electronically, details of the out-of-court representation have been given to the accused and the accused’s legal counsel; and
(b)	in the case of an out-of-court representation made to a police officer of this State that is recorded in a visual recording or audio recording —
(i)	a copy of the recording has been given to the accused’s legal counsel; or
(ii)	if the accused is not represented by legal counsel, the accused has been given a reasonable opportunity, in accordance with the regulations, to view the recording (if it is a visual recording) or to hear the recording (if it is an audio recording);
and
(c)	in the case of an out-of-court representation that is recorded in a visual recording or audio recording, and which is not an out-of-court representation made to a police officer of this State, a copy of the recording has been given to the accused and the accused’s legal counsel; and
(d)	the accused is given an opportunity to cross-examine the complainant.
(2)	Subsection (1)(b) and (c) refer to a copy of the original recording of an out-of-court representation that has not been altered, even if an altered version is to be played to the court.
(3)	If an out-of-court representation is admitted, evidence of the making and content of the out-of-court representation must be given by the person to whom the complainant made the out-of-court representation.
(4)	If an out-of-court representation is recorded in a written statement made in accordance with the Criminal Procedure Act 2004 Schedule 3 clause 4(1), the written statement may be admitted as evidence of the making and content of the out-of-court representation by the complainant.
(5)	If an out-of-court representation is recorded in a visual recording or an audio recording, the recording may be admitted as evidence of the making and content of the out-of-court representation by the complainant.
(6)	An out-of-court representation may be admitted into evidence whether or not the complainant is to give evidence in chief in the proceeding.
(7)	The admission of an out-of-court representation does not prevent a recorded interview or recorded statement being admitted as the evidence in chief of a complainant.
(8)	This section does not affect the operation of section 57.
(9)	In a child protection proceeding, this section does not affect the operation of the Children and Community Services Act 2004 section 150.
(10)	The accused is not entitled to be in possession of, or of a copy of —
(a)	a visual recording or audio recording of an out-of-court representation made to a police officer of this State; or
(b)	any visual recording or audio recording of evidence that incorporates a visual recording or audio recording of an out-of-court representation made to a police officer of this State.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106H
237.	Application of rules of evidence
(1)	The hearsay rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of an out-of-court representation to prove the existence of an asserted fact.
(2)	The opinion rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of an opinion expressed by a complainant in an out-of-court representation to prove the existence of a fact about the existence of which the opinion was expressed.
	Division 4 — Admission of recorded statements
238.	Application of Division
(1)	This Division applies to the following proceedings —
(a)	a criminal proceeding for a family violence offence;
(b)	any other criminal proceeding in the circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph.
(2)	A power to specify circumstances conferred by subsection (1)(b) includes a power to specify circumstances by reference to any of the following —
(a)	the type of offence to which the proceeding relates;
(b)	the characteristics of the complainant;
(c)	the court in which the proceedings are heard.
(3)	This Division and the regulations under this Division have effect in addition to, and do not limit the operation of, any other provision of this Act or any written law or principle or rule of common law or equity under which a recorded statement is admissible in a proceeding.
239.	Terms used
In this Division —
recorded statement means a visual recording or audio recording of a statement made by a person to a police officer about an offence, not being a statement made by a person accused of the offence;
statement admissibility regulations has the meaning given in section 241(1).
240.	Recorded statement admissible as evidence in chief
(1)	A recorded statement of a complainant is admissible under this Division as the whole or part of the evidence in chief of the complainant if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	the complainant consented to the creation of the recorded statement; and
(b)	the recorded statement was created as soon as practicable after the commission of the offence to which the proceeding relates; and
(c)	at the time the recorded statement was created, the complainant —
(i)	had reached 18 years of age; and
(ii)	was competent to give sworn or unsworn evidence; and
(iii)	was capable of giving consent to the creation of the recorded statement;
and
(d)	in the recorded statement, the complainant —
(i)	states or confirms the complainant’s name and age; and
(ii)	indicates consent to the creation of the recorded statement; and
(iii)	makes a declaration to the effect of subsection (2);
and
(e)	the police officer who created the recorded statement —
(i)	was qualified to create the recorded statement under the statement admissibility regulations; and
(ii)	complied with the Criminal Investigation Act 2006 section 124B(3);
and
(f)	the recorded statement meets any other requirements of the statement admissibility regulations to the extent required by those regulations.
(2)	The declaration required under subsection (1)(d)(iii) is as follows —
This statement is true to the best of my knowledge and belief. I have made this statement knowing that, if it is tendered in evidence, I will be guilty of an offence if I have included in the statement anything that I know to be false or that I do not believe is true.
(3)	A person who, in a recorded statement that is tendered in evidence, makes a declaration to the effect of subsection (2) and includes anything in the statement that the person knows is false or that the person does not believe is true commits a crime.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 7 years.
(4)	A recorded statement is admissible under this Division —
(a)	even if the person who made the statement that is recorded is capable of giving evidence at the proceeding; and
(b)	even if the recorded statement was created before the proceeding commenced.
241.	Statement admissibility regulations
(1)	Regulations made for the purposes of this section (the statement admissibility regulations) may specify requirements relating to the admission of a recorded statement under this Division, including, without limitation, relating to the following —
(a)	the class of police officers who are qualified to create a recorded statement;
(b)	the manner in which a statement must be created;
(c)	the manner in which a statement must be recorded;
(d)	the extent to which any specified requirements must be met.
(2)	The statement admissibility regulations may specify the class of police officers who are qualified to create a recorded statement by reference to the office or position held by the police officer, by reference to training or experience, or a combination of those criteria.
(3)	The requirements specified by the statement admissibility regulations may be different for different classes of complainant.
242.	Statement must be provided to accused
(1)	A court must not admit a recorded statement under this Division unless —
(a)	a transcript of the recorded statement has been given to the accused and the accused’s legal counsel; and
(b)	a copy of the recorded statement has been given to the accused’s legal counsel; and
(c)	if the accused is not represented by legal counsel, the accused has been given a reasonable opportunity, in accordance with the regulations, to view the recorded statement (if it is a visual recording) or hear the recorded statement (if it is an audio recording).
(2)	Subsection (1)(b) and (c) refer to a copy of the original version of the recorded statement that has not been altered, even if an altered version is to be played to the court.
(3)	The accused is not entitled to be in possession of, or of a copy of —
(a)	a recorded statement; or
(b)	any visual recording or audio recording of evidence that incorporates a recorded statement.
243.	Accused must be given opportunity to cross-examine complainant
(1)	A court must not admit a recorded statement under this Division unless the accused is given an opportunity to cross-examine the complainant.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not apply if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	the complainant is not available to give evidence; or
(b)	the complainant is out of the State and is not able to give evidence at the proceeding by means of a video link or an audio link, even if the witness might return at some future time; or
(c)	the complainant is being kept out of the way by the accused; or
(d)	all the parties consent and the interests of justice do not require the presence of the complainant.
(3)	However, if there is a prospect that the complainant, in the circumstances referred to in subsection (2)(a), might become available to give evidence, the court need not admit the statement but may adjourn the hearing of the proceeding.
(4)	Subsection (1) does not prevent any further evidence of the complainant being given under a special procedure for giving evidence.
(5)	This section does not affect the operation of section 57.
244.	Application of rules of evidence
(1)	The hearsay rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of a representation made by a complainant in a recorded statement to prove the existence of an asserted fact.
(2)	The opinion rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of an opinion expressed by a complainant in a recorded statement to prove the existence of a fact about the existence of which the opinion was expressed.
(3)	The rule that leading questions must not be put to a witness in examination in chief provided for in section 46 does not prevent a court from allowing any part of a recorded statement to be admitted as the evidence in chief of a complainant if the court is satisfied that the manner in which the recorded statement was created meets the requirements of the statement admissibility regulations to the extent required by those regulations.
245.	Presentation of recorded statement
(1)	The court may give directions as to the manner in which a recorded statement must be presented to the court and the excision of matters from it.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), the court may give any of the following directions —
(a)	a direction that the recorded statement be played to the court;
(b)	a direction that the recorded statement be tendered to the court;
(c)	a direction that a transcript of the recorded statement be tendered to the court.
(3)	A jury in the proceeding may, with the leave of the court, be given access to a transcript of a recorded statement.
246.	Further evidence
If a recorded statement is admitted as all or part of the evidence in chief of a complainant, the court may give directions as to —
(a)	the manner in which further evidence in chief of the complainant may be given at the proceeding; and
(b)	the manner in which any cross-examination or re-examination of the complainant must be conducted at the proceeding.
247.	Presence of complainant not required during presentation of statement
(1)	A complainant may, but is not required to, be present in the courtroom when a recorded statement of the complainant is played to the court.
(2)	The court may direct that arrangements be made that enable the complainant to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom when the recorded statement is played, by closed-circuit television or otherwise.
(3)	The arrangements that enable the complainant to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom must ensure that the complainant is not visible or audible to anyone in the court, other than the judge.
(4)	Subsection (1) does not prevent the court from directing the complainant to leave the courtroom while the recorded statement is played if the court considers it appropriate to do so in the interests of justice.
248.	Warning and comments to jury
(1)	A judge in any proceeding before a jury in which a recorded statement is admitted under this Division as the whole or part of the evidence in chief of a person must instruct the jury that —
(a)	the procedure is a routine practice of the court; and
(b)	the jury should not draw an inference as to the accused’s guilt from the use of the procedure.
(2)	In a proceeding before a jury, the judge, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel must not comment to the jury about a failure by the prosecutor to adduce a recorded statement of a person as the evidence in chief of a person under this Division.
(3)	Subsection (2) does not prevent a judge from directing the jury about a contravention of that subsection by the accused or the accused’s legal counsel if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
249.	Other regulations
The regulations may make further provision for recorded statements that are admissible under this Division or created for use as evidence under this Division, including, without limitation, for or with respect to the following —
(a)	the alteration, playing, custody, possession, storage, copying, transcription or destruction of recorded statements;
(b)	the records that are to be kept about recorded statements;
(c)	access to a recorded statement by the prosecutor for the purposes of any proceeding in which the recorded statement may be admitted as evidence in chief of a person;
(d)	access to and use of a recorded statement for the purposes of any proceeding in the Children’s Court or the Family Court (whether or not it has been admitted in evidence under this Division);
(e)	access to and use of a recorded statement otherwise than for the purposes of a proceeding in which the statement may be admitted under this Division or as referred to in paragraph (d);
(f)	the manner in which compliance with any of the requirements for admissibility under this Division may be proved in a proceeding.
	Division 5 — Admission of recorded interviews
250.	Application of Division
(1)	This Division applies to a criminal proceeding.
(2)	This Division, and the regulations under this Division, do not affect the operation of any other provision of this Act or any written law or principle or rule of common law or equity under which a recorded interview is admissible in a proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HD
251.	Terms used
In this Division —
interview admissibility regulations has the meaning given in section 253(1);
recorded interview means a visual recording of an interview with a person about an offence, not being an interview with a person accused of the offence.
252.	Recorded interview admissible as evidence in chief
(1)	A recorded interview with a person is admissible under this Division as the whole or part of the evidence in chief of the person in the following proceedings —
(a)	a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence, if the person interviewed —
(i)	is the complainant; and
(ii)	was a child at the time of the offence (regardless of the person’s age at the time of the interview);
(b)	any criminal proceeding, if the court is satisfied that the person interviewed —
(i)	was a child at the time of the interview; or
(ii)	is a person with a mental impairment who, because of that mental impairment, is a special witness in the proceeding.
(2)	The court must not admit the recorded interview under this Division unless —
(a)	the court is satisfied that —
(i)	the person who conducted the interview in the recorded interview was qualified to conduct the interview under the interview admissibility regulations; and
(ii)	the recorded interview meets any other requirements of the interview admissibility regulations to the extent required by those regulations;
and
(b)	the accused is given an opportunity to cross-examine the person interviewed.
(3)	A recorded interview with a person is admissible under this Division —
(a)	whether or not the interview was conducted with the consent of a parent or guardian of the person; and
(b)	irrespective of the age or maturity of the person at the time of the proceeding; and
(c)	even if the person is capable of giving evidence at the proceeding; and
(d)	even if conducted before the proceeding commenced.
(4)	This section does not affect the operation of section 57.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HA and 106HB
253.	Regulations may specify admissibility requirements for interviews
(1)	Regulations made for the purposes of this section (the interview admissibility regulations) may specify requirements relating to the admission of recorded interviews under this Division, including, without limitation, the following —
(a)	the class of persons who are qualified to conduct the interview that is recorded;
(b)	the manner in which the interview must be conducted;
(c)	the manner in which the interview must be recorded;
(d)	the information that must be recorded;
(e)	requirements relating to apparent level of understanding or cognitive functioning of the person being interviewed;
(f)	the extent to which any specified requirements must be met.
(2)	The interview admissibility regulations may specify the class of persons who are qualified to conduct an interview by reference to the office or position held by the person, by reference to training or experience, or by a combination of those criteria.
(3)	The requirements specified by the interview admissibility regulations may be different for different classes of person interviewed.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HC
254.	Interview must be provided to accused
(1)	A court must not admit a recorded interview under this Division unless —
(a)	a transcript of the interview has been given to the accused and the accused’s legal counsel; and
(b)	the accused and the accused’s legal counsel have been given a reasonable opportunity, in accordance with the regulations, to view the recorded interview.
(2)	Subsection (1)(b) refers to a copy of the original version of the recorded interview that has not been altered, even if an altered version is to be played to the court.
(3)	The accused is not entitled to be in possession of, or of a copy of —
(a)	a recorded interview; or
(b)	any visual recording or audio recording of evidence that incorporates a recorded interview.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(2) and (3)
255.	Application of rules of evidence
(1)	The hearsay rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of a representation made by a person in a recorded interview to prove the existence of an asserted fact.
(2)	The opinion rule does not prevent the admission or use of evidence of an opinion expressed by a person in a recorded interview to prove the existence of a fact about the existence of which the opinion was expressed.
(3)	The rule that leading questions must not be put to a witness in examination in chief provided for in section 46 does not prevent a court from allowing any part of a recorded interview to be admitted as the evidence in chief of a person if the court is satisfied that the manner in which the interview was conducted complies with the interview admissibility regulations to the extent required by those regulations.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(4)
256.	Presentation of recorded interview
(1)	A court may give directions as to the manner in which a recorded interview must be presented to the court and the excision of matters from it.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), the court may give any of the following directions —
(a)	a direction that the recorded interview be played to the court;
(b)	a direction that the recorded interview be tendered to the court;
(c)	a direction that a transcript of the recorded interview be tendered to the court.
(3)	A jury in the proceeding may, with the leave of the court, be given access to a transcript of a recorded interview.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(5)(a)
257.	Further evidence
If a recorded interview is admitted as all or part of the evidence in chief of a person, the court may give directions as to —
(a)	the manner in which further evidence in chief of the person may be given at the proceeding; and
(b)	the manner in which any cross-examination or re-examination of the person must be conducted at the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(5)(b)
258.	Presence of person interviewed not required during presentation of interview
(1)	A person interviewed in a recorded interview may, but is not required to, be present in the courtroom when the recorded interview is played to the court.
(2)	The court may direct that arrangements be made that enable the person to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom when the recorded interview is played, by closed-circuit television or otherwise.
(3)	The arrangements that enable the person to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom must ensure that the person is not visible or audible to anyone in the court, other than the judge.
(4)	Subsection (1) does not prevent the court from directing the person to leave the courtroom while the recorded interview is played if the court considers it appropriate to do so in the interests of justice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(6a)
259.	Warning and comments to jury
(1)	A judge in any proceeding before a jury in which a recorded interview is admitted under this Division as the whole or part of the evidence in chief of a person must instruct the jury that —
(a)	the procedure is a routine practice of the court; and
(b)	the jury should not draw an inference as to the guilt of the accused from the use of the procedure.
(2)	In a proceeding before a jury, the judge, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel must not comment to the jury about a failure by the prosecutor to adduce a recorded interview with a person as the evidence in chief of the person under this Division.
(3)	Subsection (2) does not prevent a judge from directing the jury about a contravention of that subsection by the accused or the accused’s legal counsel if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB(7)
260.	Other regulations
The regulations may make further provision for recorded interviews that are admissible under this Division or made for use as evidence under this Division, including, without limitation, for or with respect to the following —
(a)	the alteration, playing, custody, possession, storage, copying, transcription or destruction of recorded interviews;
(b)	the records that are to be kept about a recorded interview;
(c)	access to a recorded interview by the prosecutor for the purposes of any proceeding in which the recorded interview may be admitted as evidence in chief of a person;
(d)	access to and use of a recorded interview for the purposes of any proceeding in the Children’s Court or the Family Court (whether or not it has been admitted in evidence under this Division);
(e)	access to and use of a recorded interview otherwise than for the purposes of a proceeding in which the interview may be admitted under this Division or as referred to in paragraph (d);
(f)	the manner in which compliance with any of the requirements for admissibility under this Division may be proved in a proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HC
	Division 6 — Pre-recording procedure
261.	Terms used
In this Division —
pre-recording order has the meaning given in section 262(1);
special hearing means a special hearing held for the purposes of a pre-recording procedure.
262.	General provisions about pre-recording orders
(1)	A pre-recording order is an order of a court under section 263 directing that the whole or any part of the evidence of a witness in a proceeding be given using a pre-recording procedure.
(2)	A pre-recording order may be made by a court —
(a)	on the application of the party proposing to call the witness; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(3)	A pre-recording order may include any directions the court making the order thinks fit relating to the pre-recording procedure, including, but not limited to, the following —
(a)	the conduct of the special hearing at which the evidence of the witness is to be given;
(b)	arrangements for examination of the witness, including the presentation and incorporation of any recorded statement or recorded interview of the witness at the special hearing;
(c)	arrangements for cross-examination or re-examination of the witness;
(d)	the place at which the special hearing is to be held (whether in the courtroom or a separate room);
(e)	the persons who may be present in the same room as the witness when the witness’s evidence is given;
(f)	arrangements for an accused or a party to observe or listen to the special hearing.
(4)	A pre-recording order must not be inconsistent with the requirements for a pre-recording procedure specified in this Division.
(5)	A pre-recording order may be varied or revoked by further order of the court made —
(a)	on the application of a party; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(6)	A party to a proceeding must be served with a copy of an application by another party for —
(a)	a pre-recording order; or
(b)	the variation or revocation of a pre-recording order.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106I, 106K and 106RA
263.	Circumstances in which pre-recording order may be made
(1)	A court may make a pre-recording order directing that the whole or any part of the evidence of a witness be given using a pre-recording procedure —
(a)	if the witness is a child when the proceeding commences; or
(b)	if the witness —
(i)	is the complainant in a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence; and
(ii)	was a child at the time of the offence (regardless of the witness’s age at the time of the proceeding);
or
(c)	if the witness is a special witness in a criminal proceeding; or
(d)	if the court is satisfied that the witness is likely to be out of the State at the time of the proceeding and will not be able to give evidence at the proceeding by means of a video link or an audio link; or
(e)	in any circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph; or
(f)	in any other circumstances that, in the opinion of the court, justify the use of a pre-recording procedure.
(2)	A court must have regard to the interests of justice in deciding whether to make a pre-recording order in respect of a witness under this section, including —
(a)	the needs of the witness; and
(b)	the length of time until a trial is likely to be held.
(3)	A power to specify circumstances under subsection (1)(e) includes a power to specify circumstances by reference to any of the following —
(a)	the characteristics of the witness;
(b)	the type of proceeding;
(c)	the location of the proceeding.
(4)	In deciding whether the court is satisfied of the circumstances referred to in subsection (1)(e) or (f), the court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106I and 106RA
264.	Conduct of special hearing
(1)	A witness may give evidence at a special hearing in accordance with a pre-recording order, subject to the control of the presiding judge.
(2)	The usual rules of evidence apply to the special hearing, except as otherwise provided by this Division.
(3)	More than 1 special hearing may be held for the purposes of obtaining the evidence of the witness and, in that case, this Division applies in relation to each special hearing with any necessary modifications.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106K(3) and (5)
265.	Restrictions on persons who may be present at special hearing
(1)	A person (including an accused) must not be in the same room as a witness when the witness’s evidence is given at a special hearing unless the person is the presiding judge or a person authorised by a pre-recording order.
(2)	In a criminal proceeding or child protection proceeding, an accused must not be in, and cannot be authorised by a pre-recording order to be in, the same room as a witness when the witness’s evidence is given at a special hearing —
(a)	if the witness is a child when the proceeding commences; or
(b)	if the witness —
(i)	is the complainant in a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence; and
(ii)	was a child at the time of the offence (regardless of the witness’s age at the time of the proceeding);
or
(c)	if the witness is a special witness; or
(d)	in any other circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph.
(3)	However, the accused must —
(a)	be able to observe the special hearing by means of a video link or, if section 266 applies, to listen to the special hearing by means of an audio link; and
(b)	at all times have the means of communicating with the accused’s legal counsel or, if the accused is not represented, with the court.
(4)	This section applies despite the Criminal Procedure Act 2004 section 88.
(5)	If an accused gives evidence at a special hearing under a pre-recording order, a reference in subsections (1) to (3) to the accused is a reference to any other accused in the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106K(3)
266.	Alternative arrangements for attendance at special hearing
(1)	A court may direct that an accused or party listen to a special hearing by means of an audio link if, in the opinion of the court, it is not desirable for the accused or party to attend court due to the person’s health or another reason.
(2)	If the court directs that the accused or party listen to a special hearing by means of an audio link, the accused or party must be provided with a reasonable opportunity to view a copy of the visual recording of evidence from the special hearing before the evidence is presented to the court.
(3)	A person must not make a copy of, or otherwise reproduce, any audio of a special hearing, or any part of a special hearing, heard by means of an audio link under subsection (1).
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(4)	A person must not play, supply or offer to supply any copy of, or reproduction of, any audio of a special hearing, or any part of a special hearing, heard by means of an audio link under subsection (1).
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(5)	A person must not publish any audio of a special hearing, or any part of a special hearing, heard by means of an audio link under subsection (1).
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $100 000.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106K(3A), (3B), (4A), (4B) and (4C)
267.	Use of recorded statement or interview at special hearing
This Division does not prevent a recorded statement or recorded interview from being presented as the whole or part of a witness’s evidence in chief in a special hearing.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106K(4)
268.	Admissibility of recording of evidence taken at special hearing
(1)	A recording of evidence given by a witness at a special hearing is admissible in the proceeding for which it was made, and in any related proceeding, to the same extent as if it were given orally in the proceeding in accordance with the usual rules and practice of the court concerned.
(2)	If a pre-recording order is made on the ground that the witness is likely to be out of the State at the time of the proceeding, subsection (1) applies only if the court is satisfied at the proceeding that —
(a)	the witness is not available to give evidence; or
(b)	the witness is out of the State and is not able to give evidence at the proceeding by means of a video link or an audio link, even if the witness might return at some future time; or
(c)	the witness is being kept out of the way by a party; or
(d)	all of the parties consent and the interests of justice do not require the presence of the witness.
(3)	However, if there is a prospect that a witness referred to in subsection (2)(a) might become available to give evidence, the court need not admit the evidence but may adjourn the hearing of the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106T
269.	Presentation of recording of evidence
(1)	The court may give directions as to the manner in which a recording of evidence given by a witness at a special hearing must be presented to the court and the excision of matters from it.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), the court may give any of the following directions —
(a)	a direction that the recording of evidence be played to the court;
(b)	a direction that the recording of evidence be tendered to the court;
(c)	a direction that a transcript of the recording of evidence be tendered to the court.
(3)	A jury in the proceeding may, with the leave of the court, be given access to a transcript of a recording of evidence.
270.	Presence of witness not required during presentation of recording of evidence
(1)	A witness may, but is not required to, be present in the courtroom when a recording of evidence given by the witness is played to the court.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not prevent the court from directing the witness to leave the courtroom while the recording of evidence is played if the court considers it appropriate to do so in the interests of justice.
(3)	The court may direct that arrangements be made that enable the witness to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom when the recording of evidence is played, by closed-circuit television or otherwise.
(4)	The arrangements that enable the witness to view the proceeding from outside the courtroom must ensure that the witness is not visible or audible to anyone in the court, other than the judge.
(5)	This section does not prevent the court from directing that a witness be visible or audible to the courtroom while a recording of evidence is played if the witness has reached 18 years of age and the court considers it appropriate to do so in the interests of justice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106I(1)(b) and 106T(2c)
271.	Witness may be required to give further evidence
(1)	A witness who has given evidence in a special hearing may be called to give further evidence in the proceeding, but only with the leave of the court.
(2)	The court may give leave only if satisfied that —
(a)	the further evidence is required —
(i)	to clarify evidence given in the special hearing; or
(ii)	because of a matter which the party seeking leave could not reasonably have been aware of at the time of the special hearing;
and
(b)	it is in the interests of justice to do so.
(3)	The further evidence must be limited to the matters in relation to which the leave is granted.
(4)	The further evidence may be given in the absence of the jury (if any) if the court considers it appropriate in the interests of justice.
(5)	Subsection (1) does not prevent the court from directing the use of a pre-recording procedure, video link separation procedure or screening procedure in respect of the giving of further evidence.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106T(3) and (4)
272.	Identification of accused
If the evidence of a witness in a criminal proceeding is given in a special hearing, and the identification of an accused is in issue, the witness cannot be required to be in the presence of the accused for that purpose —
(a)	for any longer than is necessary for that purpose; or
(b)	before the evidence of the witness (including cross-examination and re-examination) is completed.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106Q
273.	Warning to jury
A judge in any proceeding before a jury in which the evidence of a witness is given using a pre-recording procedure must instruct the jury that —
(a)	the procedure is a routine practice of the court; and
(b)	the jury should not draw an inference as to the guilt of an accused or the culpability of a party from the use of the procedure.
	Division 7 — Video link separation and screening procedures
274.	Application of Division
(1)	This Division applies despite the Criminal Procedure Act 2004 section 88.
(2)	This Division does not affect the power of a court to order the use of video link or a screening procedure under any other provision of this Act or any other written law.
275.	Terms used
In this Division —
excluded party has the meaning given in section 276(1)(b);
video link separation order has the meaning given in section 276(1).
276.	General provisions about video link separation orders
(1)	A video link separation order is an order of a court under section 277 or 278 directing that the whole or any part of the evidence of a witness in a proceeding be given using a video link separation procedure so that —
(a)	in a criminal proceeding — the witness gives the evidence in a separate place from an accused; or
(b)	in a civil proceeding — the witness gives the evidence in a separate place from any party the court considers appropriate to exclude (an excluded party).
(2)	A video link separation order may be made by a court —
(a)	on the application of the party proposing to call the witness; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(3)	A video link separation order may include any directions the court making the order thinks fit relating to the video link separation procedure, including, but not limited to, directions about the following —
(a)	the place at which the evidence of the witness must be given, or an accused or an excluded party must remain, while the evidence is given;
(b)	the persons who may be present in the same room as the witness when the witness’s evidence is given;
(c)	the persons, or classes of persons, who are authorised to have possession of the recording of evidence made when the witness’s evidence is given.
(4)	A video link separation order must not be inconsistent with the requirements for a video link separation procedure specified in this Division.
(5)	A video link separation order may be varied or revoked by further order of the court made —
(a)	on the application of a party; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(6)	A party to a proceeding must be served with a copy of an application by another party for —
(a)	a video link separation order; or
(b)	an order varying or revoking a video link separation order.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106N and 106R(4)
277.	Circumstances in which video link separation procedure is mandatory
(1)	A court must make a video link separation order directing that the whole of the evidence of a witness be given using a video link separation procedure —
(a)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding and the witness is a child (other than the accused) when the proceeding commences; or
(b)	if the proceeding is a child protection proceeding and the witness is the affected child; or
(c)	if the witness is a special witness in the proceeding (other than the accused); or
(d)	in any other circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph.
(2)	Despite subsection (1), the court may decline to make a video link separation order if —
(a)	the court dispenses with the use of a video link separation procedure under section 311; or
(b)	the evidence of the witness is given using a pre-recording procedure; or
(c)	the court is satisfied that the necessary facilities for a video link separation procedure are not available and cannot reasonably be made available by the court.
(3)	If a court declines to make a video link separation order under subsection (2)(c), the court must —
(a)	instead of making the order, direct that the evidence of the witness be given using a screening procedure; and
(b)	consider whether any other arrangements could be made to support or assist the witness while giving evidence.
(4)	The court must not decline to make a video link separation order under subsection (2)(c) in the circumstances (if any) specified in the regulations for the purposes of this subsection.
(5)	A power to specify circumstances under subsection (1)(d) or (4) includes a power to specify circumstances by reference to any of the following —
(a)	the characteristics of the witness;
(b)	the type of proceeding;
(c)	the location of the proceeding.
(6)	In deciding whether the court is satisfied of the circumstances referred to in subsection (1)(d) or (4), the court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
278.	Other circumstances in which video link separation procedure may be used
(1)	A court may make a video link separation order directing that the whole or any part of the evidence of a witness be given using a video link separation procedure —
(a)	if the witness is an accused in a criminal proceeding in which there is more than 1 accused, and is a child when the proceeding commences; or
(b)	if the witness is an accused in a criminal proceeding in which there is more than 1 accused, and is a special witness; or
(c)	in any other circumstances specified in the regulations for the purposes of this paragraph; or
(d)	in any other circumstances that, in the opinion of the court, justify the use of a video link separation procedure.
(2)	A court must have regard to the interests of justice in deciding whether to make a video link separation order in respect of a witness under this section.
(3)	A power to specify circumstances under subsection (1)(c) includes a power to specify circumstances by reference to any of the following —
(a)	the characteristics of the witness;
(b)	the type of proceeding;
(c)	the location of the proceeding.
(4)	In deciding whether the court is satisfied of the circumstances referred to in subsection (1)(c) or (d), the court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
(5)	This section does not apply if a court is required to make a video link separation order under section 277.
279.	Access to legal counsel
An accused or an excluded party must at all times have the means of communicating with the person’s legal counsel while a witness gives evidence using a video link separation procedure.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106N(3)
280.	Evidence of witness to be recorded
(1)	If a witness gives evidence using a video link separation procedure, the evidence must be recorded on a visual recording.
(2)	A recording of evidence given by a witness using a video link separation procedure is admissible in the proceeding for which the evidence was obtained, and in any related proceeding, to the same extent as if it were given orally in the proceeding in accordance with the usual rules and practice of the court concerned.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106N(3a) and 106T(1) and (2)
281.	Use of screening procedure
(1)	If, instead of making a video link separation order, a court directs that the evidence of the witness be given using a screening procedure, the court must ensure that —
(a)	the screening procedure does not cause significant hardship to the witness; and
(b)	the witness is still able to access any person who has been approved by the court to provide support to the witness while giving evidence; and
(c)	the arrangements for the witness to give evidence are otherwise in the interests of justice.
(2)	The witness’s evidence must be recorded on a visual recording if the necessary facilities are available to do so.
(3)	A recording of evidence given by a witness while using a screening procedure is admissible in the proceeding for which the evidence was obtained, and in any related proceeding, to the same extent as if it were given orally in the proceeding in accordance with the usual rules and practice of the court concerned.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106N(4) and (5) and 106T(1) and (2)
282.	Identification of accused
If the evidence of a witness in a criminal proceeding is given using a video link separation procedure or a screening procedure under this Division, and the identification of an accused is in issue, the witness cannot be required to be in the presence of the accused for that purpose —
(a)	for any longer than is necessary for that purpose; or
(b)	before the evidence of the witness (including cross-examination and re-examination) is completed.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106Q
283.	Instruction to jury
A judge in any proceeding before a jury in which the evidence of a witness is given using a video link separation procedure or a screening procedure under this Division must instruct the jury that —
(a)	the procedure is a routine practice of the court; and
(b)	the jury should not draw an inference as to the guilt of an accused or the culpability of a party from the use of the procedure.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106P
	Division 8 — Witness intermediaries
284.	Terms used
In this Division —
accredited witness intermediary means a person who is accredited, in accordance with the regulations, to act as a witness intermediary;
witness intermediary service means the witness intermediary service referred to in section 285.
285.	Establishment of witness intermediary service
(1)	There is to be a witness intermediary service.
(2)	The witness intermediary service is comprised of persons who are accredited, in accordance with the regulations, to act as witness intermediaries.
(3)	The regulations may make provision for the establishment of the witness intermediary service, including for or with respect to the following —
(a)	accreditation as a witness intermediary, including any qualifications, training or experience required and any circumstances that disqualify a person from acting as a witness intermediary (either generally or in a particular case);
(b)	ongoing training and assessment of witness intermediaries.
286.	Proceedings for which witness intermediary service is available
(1)	The witness intermediary service is available for the following proceedings —
(a)	a criminal proceeding;
(b)	a child protection proceeding;
(c)	any other civil proceeding in the circumstances (if any) provided for by the regulations.
(2)	The regulations may provide that the witness intermediary service is available only for a proceeding in a specified area or areas.
(3)	For the purposes of subsection (2), an area may be specified by reference to —
(a)	the area in which an offence occurred or is alleged to have occurred; or
(b)	the area in which a court hears or is to hear a proceeding; or
(c)	any other matter.
287.	General functions of witness intermediary
(1)	The principal function of a witness intermediary is to facilitate communication to and by a witness for the purpose of ensuring that any evidence given by the witness is as complete, accurate and coherent as possible.
(2)	A witness intermediary is an officer of the court.
(3)	As an officer of the court, a witness intermediary has an overriding duty to the court to act honestly and impartially in the exercise of their functions.
(4)	The regulations may make further provision for the functions of a witness intermediary.
288.	Specific functions in proceedings
(1)	If there is a witness intermediary for a witness in a proceeding, any evidence given by the witness in the proceeding must be given in the presence of the witness intermediary.
(2)	A failure to comply with subsection (1) does not affect the admissibility of any evidence.
(3)	The witness intermediary may —
(a)	make recommendations to the court about how best to communicate with the witness; and
(b)	intervene in the questioning of the witness where a communication difficulty is identified; and
(c)	if requested by the judge, communicate and explain —
(i)	to the witness, questions put to the witness; and
(ii)	to the court, evidence given by the witness.
(4)	The witness intermediary must comply with any directions of the court in exercising the witness intermediary’s functions.
(5)	A witness intermediary who exercises functions in a courtroom or in connection with a special procedure for giving evidence must either take an oath, or make an affirmation, in a form directed by the court, that they will exercise their functions as a witness intermediary to the best of their ability.
(6)	The appointment of a witness intermediary for a witness does not prevent the witness —
(a)	having a support person under section 231 while giving evidence; or
(b)	using a communicator under section 233 to give evidence.
289.	Assessment of whether witness requires witness intermediary
(1)	In any proceeding for which the witness intermediary service is available, the court may request an assessment (a witness assessment) of whether a witness requires the assistance of a witness intermediary to give evidence in the proceeding.
(2)	A witness assessment must be carried out by an accredited witness intermediary.
(3)	A court may request a witness assessment —
(a)	on the application of a party; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(4)	The regulations may make further provision for witness assessments.
290.	Witnesses who are eligible for witness assessment
(1)	A court must request a witness assessment in relation to a witness —
(a)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding and the witness is a child (other than the accused) under 10 years of age when the proceeding commences; or
(b)	if the proceeding is a criminal proceeding for a sexual offence and the witness is a child who is the complainant.
(2)	Subsection (1)(b) does not apply if the court is satisfied that a witness assessment is not necessary.
(3)	The court must state its reasons for not requiring a witness assessment in a case referred to in subsection (1)(b).
(4)	A court may request a witness assessment in relation to a witness not referred to in subsection (1), other than an accused —
(a)	if the proceeding is a child protection proceeding and the witness is the affected child; or
(b)	if the witness is a special witness; or
(c)	if the witness appears to have specific communication needs and to require the assistance of a witness intermediary to give evidence that is as complete, accurate and coherent as possible; or
(d)	if the court otherwise considers it appropriate.
(5)	The regulations may specify other circumstances in which the court may request a witness assessment in relation to a witness.
(6)	The court, when deciding whether to request a witness assessment (other than as required by subsection (1)(a)) —
(a)	must have regard to the submissions of the parties and to the views of the witness; and
(b)	may inform itself as it thinks fit.
291.	Appointment of witness intermediary in proceeding
(1)	A court must appoint an accredited witness intermediary as a witness intermediary for a witness in a proceeding if —
(a)	the witness intermediary service is available for the proceeding; and
(b)	a witness assessment requested by the court indicates that the witness requires the assistance of a witness intermediary to give evidence in the proceeding; and
(c)	the court is satisfied, having regard to the witness assessment, that the witness requires the assistance of a witness intermediary to give evidence in the proceeding.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not apply if the court is satisfied that —
(a)	there is no witness intermediary available to meet the needs of the witness; or
(b)	it is otherwise impractical to appoint, or utilise the services of, a witness intermediary.
(3)	If a witness assessment indicates that a witness who is a child when the assessment is made requires the assistance of a witness intermediary to give evidence in the proceeding, the fact that the witness reaches 18 years of age during the proceeding does not affect that assessment.
(4)	The regulations may make further provision for the appointment of a witness intermediary in a proceeding, including the duration of the appointment.
292.	Witness who declines witness assessment or services of witness intermediary
(1)	Despite any other provision of this Division, a court may decide not to request a witness assessment or to dispense with the services of a witness intermediary if satisfied that the witness has decided to decline the witness assessment or the services of a witness intermediary.
(2)	The court must be satisfied that —
(a)	the witness understands their decision and its effect; and
(b)	the witness’s decision was made freely and voluntarily.
(3)	A witness must not be questioned in a proceeding about a decision to decline a witness assessment or to give evidence without the assistance of a witness intermediary except by or with the approval of the court for the purposes of satisfying this section.
(4)	In a criminal proceeding before a jury, the judge, the prosecutor, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel must not comment to the jury about a decision by a witness to decline a witness assessment or to give evidence without the assistance of a witness intermediary.
(5)	Subsection (4) does not prevent a judge from directing the jury about a contravention of that subsection by the prosecutor, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
	Division 9 — Communication directions
293.	Term used: communication directions
In this Division —
communication directions has the meaning given in section 294(1).
294.	Communication directions relating to witnesses
(1)	A court may give directions (communication directions) in relation to a witness for the purpose of ensuring that the communication, support or other needs of the witness in a proceeding are met in a manner that is fair and effective.
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), the court may give communication directions relating to any of the following matters —
(a)	the manner in which a witness must be questioned;
(b)	the duration of questioning and breaks in questioning;
(c)	the questions that may, or must not be, put to a witness (including by giving directions about disallowable questions under section 50);
(d)	if there are more than 2 parties — the allocation among them of topics about which a witness may be questioned;
(e)	the use of models, plans, maps or similar aids to question a witness;
(f)	the cross-examination of a witness;
(g)	if a party intends to lead evidence that contradicts or challenges the evidence of a witness or that otherwise discredits a witness — that the party is not obliged to put that evidence in its entirety to the witness in cross-examination.
(3)	If a court requests a witness assessment for a witness, the court must have regard to that witness assessment in deciding whether to give any communication directions.
295.	Ground rules hearing
(1)	A court may hold a hearing (a ground rules hearing) for the purposes of —
(a)	considering the communication, support or other needs of a witness; and
(b)	deciding whether any communication directions should be made to meet those needs fairly and effectively.
(2)	A ground rules hearing may be held —
(a)	on the application of a party; or
(b)	on the court’s own initiative.
(3)	An application for a ground rules hearing may be made to the court orally or in writing.
(4)	A court must hold a ground rules hearing in relation to a witness if a witness intermediary has been appointed for the witness, unless subsection (5) applies.
(5)	A court may give communication directions in relation to a witness, without holding a ground rules hearing, if —
(a)	the communication directions have been agreed by the parties and the witness intermediary (if any) for the witness; and
(b)	the court is satisfied that those communication directions are appropriate.
296.	Timing of ground rules hearing
(1)	If a ground rules hearing is to be held, it must, if practicable, be held a reasonable period before the commencement of any hearing at which the witness is to give evidence.
(2)	The court may extend the time for holding the ground rules hearing if the court considers that it is in the interests of justice to do so.
(3)	The court may extend the time under subsection (2) —
(a)	before or after the time expires; and
(b)	more than once.
297.	Conduct of ground rules hearing
(1)	The following persons must attend a ground rules hearing —
(a)	in a criminal proceeding — a person acting for the prosecution and the legal counsel acting for the accused or, if the accused is unrepresented, the accused;
(b)	in a civil proceeding — the legal counsel of the parties or, if a party is unrepresented, the party (other than the affected child in a child protection proceeding);
(c)	any witness intermediary for the witness in the proceeding.
(2)	A witness is not required to attend a ground rules hearing.
(3)	The court may order that a ground rules hearing be held in the absence of a witness if a witness intermediary has been appointed for the witness.
(4)	Rules of court may provide for ground rules hearings.
298.	Other powers of court not affected
This Division does not affect any other power of a court to give directions about the questioning of a witness.
	Division 10 — Access to and use of protected recordings
299.	Terms used
In this Division —
Director of Public Prosecutions means —
(a)	the Director of Public Prosecutions appointed under the Director of Public Prosecutions Act 1991 section 5; or
(b)	a person acting in that office under section 8 of that Act;
protected recording means —
(a)	a visual recording or audio recording of an out-of-court representation made to a police officer of this State, being a representation that may be admitted into evidence under Division 3 (whether or not it has been admitted under that Division); or
(b)	a recorded statement that is admissible as evidence under Division 4 or created for use as evidence under Division 4 (whether or not it has been admitted under that Division); or
(c)	a recorded interview that is admissible as evidence under Division 5 or made for use as evidence under Division 5 (whether or not it has been admitted under that Division); or
(d)	a visual recording or audio recording of evidence given under a special procedure for giving evidence.
300.	Possessing or supplying protected recordings
(1)	A person must not, without authority —
(a)	be in possession of any protected recording; or
(b)	supply or offer to supply any protected recording to any person.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(2)	A person has authority for the purposes of subsection (1) only if the person is —
(a)	a police officer acting in the course of the police officer’s official duties; or
(b)	the Director of Public Prosecutions or a person acting under the authority of the Director of Public Prosecutions; or
(c)	legal counsel acting for or representing the State; or
(d)	legal counsel for a party to a proceeding to which the protected recording relates, other than the State; or
(e)	a person engaged by a person referred to in paragraph (a), (b), (c) or (d) acting for a purpose connected with the conduct or defence of a proceeding to which the protected recording relates; or
(f)	a public official acting in good faith for a purpose connected with the person’s duties as a public official; or
(g)	acting as authorised by a court; or
(h)	acting as authorised by this Act or regulations made for the purposes of this paragraph; or
(i)	acting as authorised by the Criminal Investigation Act 2006.
(3)	Subsection (2)(d) and (e) do not apply if the protected recording is —
(a)	a recorded interview; or
(b)	any visual recording or audio recording of evidence that incorporates a recorded interview.
(4)	A person ceases to have authority under subsection (2)(d) for the purposes of subsection (1) —
(a)	if the person ceases to act for the party to the proceeding before it is completed — at the end of the period of 10 days beginning on the day after the day on which the person ceased to act for the party to the proceeding; or
(b)	if paragraph (a) does not apply — at the end of the period of 10 days beginning on the day after the expiry of the time allowed for commencing an appeal in relation to the proceeding.
(5)	A person ceases to have authority under subsection (2)(e) for the purposes of subsection (1) —
(a)	if the person ceases to be engaged by the person before the proceeding is completed — at the end of the period of 10 days beginning on the day after the day on which the person ceased to be engaged by the person; or
(b)	if the person is engaged by legal counsel referred to in subsection (2)(d) who ceases to act for the party to the proceeding before it is completed — at the end of the period of 10 days beginning on the day after the day on which the person becomes aware that legal counsel has ceased to act for the party to the proceeding; or
(c)	if paragraphs (a) and (b) do not apply — at the end of the period of 10 days beginning on the day after the expiry of the time allowed for commencing an appeal in relation to the proceeding.
(6)	For the purposes of subsections (4)(b) and (5)(c), it is irrelevant that a person may apply, or has applied, to a court for an extension of the time allowed for commencing an appeal in relation to the proceeding.
(7)	If a person who is legal counsel for a party to a proceeding is given a protected recording that relates to the proceeding, the person must, before the person ceases to have authority under subsection (2)(d) to be in possession of the protected recording, take all reasonable steps to ensure that it is returned to —
(a)	a person referred to in subsection (2)(a), (b) or (c) acting for or representing, or who acted for or represented, the State in the proceeding; or
(b)	if there is no person referred to in subsection (2)(a), (b) or (c) acting for or representing, or who acted for or represented, the State in the proceeding — the court that is dealing with, or that dealt with, the proceeding.
Penalty for this subsection: a fine of $10 000.
(8)	A direction under section 308(1) may provide that 1 or more of subsections (3) to (7) do not apply in relation to a protected recording (with or without making alternative provision).
(9)	Subsection (7) does not apply if the person has authority under subsection (2)(g) or (h) to possess the protected recording.
(10)	A person is not entitled to be in possession of any protected recording relating to an offence of which the person is accused and, accordingly, has no authority to possess, supply, or offer to supply that protected recording.
(11)	Subsection (10) continues to apply after any prosecution of the offence has been completed or terminated.
(12)	This section does not prevent an accused from being in possession of a protected recording that is a recording of evidence given by that accused.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106MA
301.	Altering or destroying original protected recording
(1)	A person must not, without authority, alter or destroy or permit another person to alter or destroy an original version of a protected recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(2)	A person has authority for the purposes of subsection (1) only if the person is —
(a)	a public official acting in good faith for a purpose connected with the person’s duties as a public official; or
(b)	acting as authorised by a court; or
(c)	acting as authorised by this Act or regulations made for the purposes of this paragraph; or
(d)	acting as authorised by the Criminal Investigation Act 2006.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106MA
302.	Altering copy of protected recording
A person must not alter a copy of any protected recording unless the person has authority to be in possession of the protected recording.
Penalty: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
303.	Copying protected recording
(1)	A person must not, without authority, copy or permit another person to copy any protected recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(2)	A person has authority for the purposes of subsection (1) only if the person is —
(a)	a police officer acting in the course of the police officer’s official duties; or
(b)	the Director of Public Prosecutions or a person acting under the authority of the Director of Public Prosecutions; or
(c)	legal counsel acting for or representing the State; or
(d)	a public official acting in good faith for a purpose connected with the person’s duties as a public official; or
(e)	acting as authorised by a court; or
(f)	acting as authorised by this Act or regulations made for the purposes of this paragraph; or
(g)	acting as authorised by the Criminal Investigation Act 2006.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106MA
304.	Playing protected recordings
(1)	A person must not, without authority, play any protected recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(2)	A person has authority for the purposes of subsection (1) only if the person —
(a)	plays the protected recording for a purpose connected with the conduct or defence of a proceeding to which the protected recording relates; or
(b)	is a police officer acting in the course of the police officer’s official duties; or
