(c)	is a public official acting in good faith for purposes connected with the person’s duties as a public official; or
(d)	is acting as authorised by a court; or
(e)	is acting as authorised by this Act or regulations made for the purposes of this paragraph.
(3)	This section does not authorise —
(a)	an accused to play any protected recording relating to the proceeding in which the person is accused; or
(b)	any other person to play, in the presence of the accused, the protected recording for a purpose connected with the conduct or defence of the proceeding unless the person, or an employee or agent of the person, is present with the accused for the entire period during which the recording is played.
(4)	Subsection (3) does not apply to a protected recording that is a recording of evidence given by the accused if it is that accused —
(a)	who plays the recording; or
(b)	in whose presence the recording is played.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106MA
305.	Protected recordings may be played for instructional purposes
(1)	A protected recording may be played to any of the following persons for the purposes of instruction —
(a)	a police officer or police trainee;
(b)	an officer of the Commission (as defined in the Corruption, Crime and Misconduct Act 2003 section 3(1));
(c)	a lawyer or a person studying to be a lawyer;
(d)	a person prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this section.
(2)	A protected recording may be played under subsection (1) only if —
(a)	all proceedings in relation to the subject matter of the protected recording have concluded; and
(b)	all reasonable measures are taken to prevent the identification of the person being recorded, interviewed or giving evidence in the protected recording; and
(c)	any further requirements specified in the regulations relating to the use or playing of the protected recording for instructional purposes are satisfied.
306.	Publication of protected recordings prohibited
(1)	A person must not publish any protected recording except in accordance with the approval of the Supreme Court.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $100 000.
(2)	The Supreme Court may approve the publication of a protected recording only if satisfied that exceptional circumstances justify approval being given.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106MB
307.	Playing or tendering altered versions of protected recordings in proceedings
(1)	A party proposing to play or tender any protected recording in a proceeding must notify the court if the protected recording has been altered from the original version of the protected recording.
(2)	A party proposing to tender a transcript of a protected recording in a proceeding must notify the court if the transcript has been altered from the original version of the protected recording.
(3)	A protected recording, or a transcript of a protected recording, that has been altered from the original version of the protected recording may be played or tendered to the court only with the leave of the court.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106HB
308.	Court may give directions about protected recordings
(1)	The Supreme Court, the Family Court, the District Court, the Magistrates Court, the Children’s Court or any court that admits a protected recording as evidence or directs the use of a special procedure for giving evidence may give directions as to the possession, supply, copying, editing, destruction or playing of a protected recording.
(2)	A court must not authorise a person to be in possession of a protected recording if the protected recording relates to an offence of which the person is accused.
(3)	Subsection (2) continues to apply after any prosecution of the offence has been completed or terminated.
(4)	Subsection (2) does not apply to the possession by an accused of a protected recording of evidence given by that accused.
	Division 11 — Miscellaneous
309.	Hearing to consider orders that should be made
(1)	In any proceeding in which it is likely that the court will be required or asked to do any of the following in relation to a witness, the party who proposes to call the witness concerned must apply for a directions hearing for the purpose of having the matter dealt with before the proceeding —
(a)	decide whether the witness must be treated as a special witness or should be declared to be a special witness;
(b)	make any order or direction in relation to an out-of-court representation, recorded statement or recorded interview of the witness;
(c)	make any order or direction, or give any approval, in relation to a special procedure for giving evidence, as it affects the witness;
(d)	hold a ground rules hearing in relation to the witness;
(e)	request a witness assessment in relation to the witness.
(2)	Rules of court may provide for directions hearings under this section.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106S
310.	Disclosure of protected communications in connection with support measures
(1)	In this section —
Department means the department of the Public Service principally assisting in the administration of this Act;
Justice document means a document that records any report, opinion, advice, recommendation or correspondence relating to a sexual offence complainant made by an officer or employee of, or person engaged by, the Department in connection with a service offered to witnesses in criminal proceedings or victims of crime.
(2)	The prosecutor in a criminal proceeding may, without the leave of the court, tender a Justice document in support of the prosecutor’s submission at any of the following hearings, even if the document records a protected communication —
(a)	a hearing to determine whether the sexual offence complainant should be treated as or declared to be a special witness;
(b)	a hearing to determine whether the court should make any order or direction in relation to a special procedure for giving evidence as it affects the sexual offence complainant.
(3)	Section 166 does not prevent the disclosure or admission of a Justice document for the purpose of a hearing mentioned in subsection (2).
(4)	If a Justice document that records a protected communication is admitted for the purpose of a hearing mentioned in subsection (2), section 174 applies in the same way as it applies to the disclosure of a document under Part 4 Division 3.
Example for this subsection:
The court may make an order suppressing the publication of all or part of the Justice document to protect the safety and welfare of the sexual offence complainant.
(5)	This section does not affect the application of Part 4 Division 3 to the disclosure or admission of a Justice document for the purpose of any other part of the criminal proceeding.
311.	Dispensing with special procedure for giving evidence
(1)	A court may, by order, dispense with the use of a special procedure for giving evidence if the court is satisfied that the witness is able to give evidence, and consents to giving evidence, without using the special procedure for giving evidence.
(2)	The court must not make the order unless the court is satisfied of the following matters —
(a)	that the witness understands their decision and its effect;
(b)	that the decision was made freely and voluntarily.
(3)	In deciding whether to make the order, the court may inform itself as it thinks fit.
(4)	A witness must not be questioned in a proceeding about a decision to give evidence without using a special procedure for giving evidence except by or with the approval of the court for the purposes of satisfying this section.
(5)	In a criminal proceeding before a jury, the judge, the prosecutor, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel must not comment to the jury about a decision by a witness to give evidence without using a special procedure for giving evidence.
(6)	Subsection (5) does not prevent a judge from directing the jury about a contravention of that subsection by the prosecutor, the accused or the accused’s legal counsel if it is in the interests of justice to do so.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106R(8)
	Part 8 — Proof
	Division 1 — Standard of proof
312.	Civil proceeding: standard of proof
(1)	In a civil proceeding, the court must find the case of a party proved if it is satisfied that the case has been proved on the balance of probabilities.
(2)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account in deciding whether it is satisfied, it must take into account —
(a)	the nature of the cause of action or defence; and
(b)	the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding; and
(c)	the gravity of the matters alleged.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 140
313.	Criminal proceeding: standard of proof
(1)	In a criminal proceeding, the court must not find the case of the prosecution proved unless it is satisfied that it has been proved beyond reasonable doubt.
(2)	In a criminal proceeding, the court must find the case of an accused proved if it is satisfied that the case has been proved on the balance of probabilities.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 141
314.	Admissibility of evidence: standard of proof
(1)	Except as otherwise provided by this Act, in any proceeding the court must find that the facts necessary for deciding any of the following questions have been proved if it is satisfied that they have been proved on the balance of probabilities —
(a)	a question whether evidence should be admitted or not admitted, whether in the exercise of a discretion or not;
(b)	any other question arising under this Act.
(2)	In determining whether it is satisfied, the matters that the court must take into account include —
(a)	the importance of the evidence in the proceeding; and
(b)	the gravity of the matters alleged in relation to the question.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 142
	Division 2 — Corroboration
315.	Corroboration not required
(1)	It is not necessary that evidence on which a party relies be corroborated.
(2)	Despite any rule, whether of law or practice, to the contrary, if there is a jury, the judge must not —
(a)	warn the jury that it is dangerous to act on uncorroborated evidence or give a warning to the same or similar effect; or
(b)	direct the jury regarding the absence of corroboration.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 106D
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 164
	Division 3 — Warnings and information
316.	Term used: delay
In this Division —
delay, in making a complaint, includes —
(a)	a failure to pursue or to continue to pursue a complaint in a timely manner; and
(b)	a failure to make a complaint at the first opportunity, or on a subsequent opportunity, to complain.
317.	Unreliable evidence
(1)	This section applies to evidence of a kind that may be unreliable, including the following kinds of evidence —
(a)	evidence in relation to which Part 3 Division 2 or 4 applies;
(b)	identification evidence;
(c)	evidence given in a criminal proceeding by a witness, being a witness who might reasonably be supposed to have been criminally concerned in the events giving rise to the proceeding;
(d)	evidence given in a criminal proceeding by a witness who is a prison informer;
(e)	oral evidence of questioning by an investigating official of an accused that is questioning recorded in writing that has not been signed, or otherwise acknowledged in writing, by the accused;
(f)	in a proceeding against the estate of a deceased person — evidence adduced by or on behalf of a person seeking relief in the proceeding that is evidence about a matter about which the deceased person could have given evidence if the deceased person were alive.
(2)	In a proceeding before a jury, the judge must do the following if a party requests it —
(a)	warn the jury that the evidence may be unreliable;
(b)	inform the jury of matters that may cause it to be unreliable;
(c)	warn the jury of the need for caution in determining whether to accept the evidence and the weight to be given to it.
(3)	The judge need not comply with subsection (2) if satisfied there is good reason for not complying.
(4)	It is not necessary that a particular form of words be used in giving the warning or information.
(5)	This section does not affect any other power of the judge to give a warning to, or to inform, the jury.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 165
318.	Warnings about evidence given by children
(1)	A judge in any proceeding in which evidence is given by a child before a jury must not do any of the following —
(a)	warn the jury, or suggest to the jury, that children as a class are unreliable witnesses;
(b)	warn the jury, or suggest to the jury, that the evidence of children as a class is inherently less credible or reliable, or requires more careful scrutiny, than the evidence of adults;
(c)	give a warning, or make a suggestion, to the jury about the unreliability of the particular child’s evidence solely on account of the age of the child.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not prevent a judge from —
(a)	informing the jury that the evidence of the particular child may be unreliable and the reasons why it may be unreliable; and
(b)	warning or informing the jury of the need for caution in determining whether to accept the evidence of the particular child and the weight to be given to it.
(3)	A judge may exercise the power conferred by subsection (2) only if —
(a)	a party requests it; and
(b)	the party satisfies the court that there are circumstances (other than solely the age of the child) particular to the child that affect the reliability of the child’s evidence and that warrant the giving of a warning or the information.
(4)	This section does not affect any other power of a judge to give a warning to, or to inform, the jury.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 165A
319.	Warnings about delays causing forensic disadvantage
(1)	A rule of law or practice that requires a judge in a criminal proceeding to give a warning of a kind known as a Longman warning is abolished.
Note for this subsection:
See Longman v The Queen (1989) 168 CLR 79.
(2)	If, on application by an accused in a criminal proceeding before a jury, the court is satisfied that the accused has suffered a significant forensic disadvantage because of delay, the judge must —
(a)	explain to the jury the nature of the forensic disadvantage; and
(b)	direct that the jury must take the forensic disadvantage into account when scrutinising the evidence.
(3)	The judge need not comply with subsection (2) if satisfied there is good reason for not complying.
(4)	The mere fact of a delay is not sufficient to establish a significant forensic disadvantage.
(5)	An explanation or direction under subsection (2) —
(a)	must be specific to the circumstances of the particular case; and
(b)	must explain to the jury that the forensic disadvantage suffered by the accused was not merely due to the delay; and
(c)	must not take the form of a warning; and
(d)	must not warn the jury, or suggest to the jury, that because of the delay or the forensic disadvantage suffered by the accused —
(i)	it is dangerous or unsafe to convict; or
(ii)	evidence of a complainant should be scrutinised with great care.
(6)	This section does not affect any other power of a judge to give a warning to, or to inform, the jury.
320.	Prohibited directions about delays in complaint about sexual and family violence offences
(1)	In any criminal proceeding before a jury for a sexual offence or a family violence offence, if evidence is adduced that suggests that a complainant delayed in making a complaint about the offence or failed to make a complaint about the offence, there is no requirement that the judge warn or direct the jury that the delay or failure affects the credibility of the complainant.
(2)	A judge must not warn the jury, direct the jury or otherwise suggest to the jury that a delay in making a complaint or a failure to make a complaint about a sexual offence or a family violence offence affects the credibility of the complainant except as provided for by subsection (3).
(3)	A judge may warn or direct a jury that a delay in making a complaint or a failure to make a complaint affects the credibility of a complainant if —
(a)	the accused requests the warning or direction; and
(b)	the court is satisfied that the warning or direction is justified in the particular circumstances of the case.
(4)	In giving any warning or direction under subsection (3), the judge must not do any of the following —
(a)	warn the jury, or suggest to the jury, that it is dangerous or unsafe to convict because of the delay in making, or failure to make, a complaint;
(b)	warn the jury, or suggest to the jury, that because of the delay in making, or failure to make, a complaint, the evidence of the complainant should be scrutinised with great care.
(5)	This section does not affect any other power of a judge to give a warning to, or to inform, the jury.
321.	Required direction for delays in complaint about sexual and family violence offences
(1)	In a criminal proceeding before a jury for a sexual offence or a family violence offence, the judge must give the jury a direction under this section —
(a)	if the judge considers that there is likely to be evidence adduced in the trial that suggests that the complainant delayed in making a complaint or did not make a complaint about the offence; or
(b)	if at any other time evidence is adduced in the trial or a comment is made to the jury that suggests that the complainant delayed in making a complaint or did not make a complaint about the offence.
(2)	If subsection (1)(a) applies, the direction must be given before the evidence is adduced and may be given before any evidence is adduced in the trial.
(3)	If subsection (1)(b) applies, the direction must be given as soon as practicable.
(4)	The judge must direct the jury that experience shows that —
(a)	people may react differently to sexual offences or family violence (as the case requires) and there is no typical, proper or normal response to a sexual offence or family violence; and
(b)	some people may complain immediately to the first person they see, while others may not complain for some time and others may never make a complaint; and
(c)	delay in making a complaint about a sexual offence or family violence is a common occurrence.
(5)	In the case of family violence, the judge may also include in the direction any matter that could be included in a direction on family violence under section 144(1)(b), and section 144(3) applies in relation to that part of the direction in the same way as it applies to a direction on family violence.
(6)	The judge may repeat a direction under this section at any time during the criminal proceeding.
(7)	This section does not affect any other power of a judge to give a warning to, or to inform, the jury.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 36BD
	Division 4 — Requests between parties relating to evidence
322.	Application of Division
This Division applies to a civil proceeding.
323.	Term used: request
In this Division —
request means a request that a party (the requesting party) makes to another party to do 1 or more of the following —
(a)	to produce to the requesting party the whole or a part of a specified document or thing;
(b)	to permit the requesting party, adequately and in an appropriate way, to examine, test or copy the whole or a part of a specified document or thing;
(c)	to call as a witness a specified person believed to be concerned in the production or maintenance of a specified document or thing;
(d)	to call as a witness a specified person in whose possession or under whose control a specified document or thing is believed to be or to have been at any time;
(e)	in relation to a document of a kind referred to in section 6(1)(b) or (c) — to permit the requesting party, adequately and in an appropriate way, to examine and test the document and the manner in which it was produced and has been kept;
(f)	in relation to evidence of a previous representation — to call as a witness the person who made the previous representation;
(g)	in relation to evidence that a person has been convicted of an offence, being evidence that can be admitted or used in the proceeding under Part 3 Division 5 — to call as a witness a person who gave evidence in the proceeding in which the person was convicted of the offence.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 166
324.	Requests may be made about certain matters
A party may make a reasonable request to another party for the purpose of determining a question that relates to any of the following —
(a)	a previous representation;
(b)	evidence of a conviction of a person for an offence;
(c)	the authenticity, identity or admissibility of a document or thing.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 167
325.	Time limits for making requests
(1)	If a party has given to another party written notice of its intention to adduce evidence of a previous representation, the other party may make a request to the party relating to the representation only if the request is made within 21 days after the notice was given.
(2)	Despite subsection (1), the court may give the other party leave to make a request relating to the representation after the end of the 21-day period if it is satisfied that there is good reason to do so.
(3)	If a party has given to another party written notice of its intention to adduce evidence of a person’s conviction of an offence in order to prove a fact in issue, the other party may make a request relating to evidence of the conviction only if the request is made within 21 days after the notice was given.
(4)	Despite subsection (3), the court may give the other party leave to make a request relating to evidence of the conviction after the end of the 21-day period if it is satisfied that there is good reason to do so.
(5)	If a party has served on another party a copy of a document that it intends to tender in evidence, the other party may make a request relating to the document only if the request is made within 21 days after service of the copy.
(6)	If the copy of the document served under subsection (5) is accompanied by, or has endorsed on it, a notice stating that the document is to be tendered to prove the contents of another document, the other party may make a request relating to the other document only if the request is made within 21 days after service of the copy.
(7)	Despite subsections (5) and (6), the court may give the other party leave to make a request relating to the document, or other document, after the end of the 21-day period if it is satisfied that there is good reason to do so.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 168
326.	Failure or refusal to comply with requests
(1)	If a party has, without reasonable cause, failed or refused to comply with a request, the court may, on application by the other party, make 1 or more of the following orders —
(a)	an order directing the party to comply with the request;
(b)	an order that the party produce a specified document or thing, or call as a witness a specified person;
(c)	an order that the evidence in relation to which the request was made must not be admitted in evidence;
(d)	any order with respect to adjournment or costs as is just.
(2)	If the party has, within a reasonable time after receiving the request, informed the other party that it refuses to comply with the request, any application under subsection (1) by the other party must be made within a reasonable time after being informed.
(3)	The court may, on application, direct that evidence in relation to which a request was made must not be admitted in evidence if an order made by it under subsection (1)(a) or (b) is not complied with.
(4)	Without limiting the circumstances that may constitute reasonable cause for a party to fail or refuse to comply with a request, it is reasonable cause to fail or refuse to comply with a request if —
(a)	the document or thing to be produced is not available to the party; or
(b)	the existence and contents of the document are not in issue in the proceeding in which evidence of the document is proposed to be adduced; or
(c)	the person to be called as a witness is not available.
(5)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account in relation to the exercise of a power under subsection (1), it must take into account —
(a)	the importance in the proceeding of the evidence in relation to which the request was made; and
(b)	whether there is likely to be a dispute about the matter to which the evidence relates; and
(c)	whether there is a reasonable doubt as to the authenticity or accuracy of the evidence that is, or the document the contents of which are, sought to be proved; and
(d)	whether there is a reasonable doubt as to the authenticity of the document or thing that is sought to be tendered; and
(e)	if the request relates to evidence of a previous representation — whether there is a reasonable doubt as to the accuracy of the representation or of the evidence on which it was based; and
(f)	if the request relates to evidence that a person has been convicted of an offence and the request is to call as a witness a person who gave evidence in the proceeding in which the person was convicted — whether another person is available to give evidence about the conviction or the facts that were in issue in the proceeding in which the conviction was obtained; and
(g)	whether compliance with the request would involve undue expense or delay or would not be reasonably practicable; and
(h)	whether compliance with the request would involve an unwarranted breach of the privacy of a person or cause distress, humiliation or embarrassment to a person; and
(i)	the nature of the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 169
	Part 9 — Judicial notice and facilitation of proof
	Division 1 — Matters of common knowledge
327.	Judicial notice to be taken of matters of common knowledge
(1)	Proof is not required about knowledge that is not reasonably open to question and that is —
(a)	common knowledge in the locality in which the proceeding is being held or generally; or
(b)	capable of verification by reference to a document the authority of which cannot reasonably be questioned.
(2)	The judge may acquire knowledge of the kind referred to in subsection (1) in any way the judge thinks fit.
(3)	The court (including, if there is a jury, the jury) must take knowledge of the kind referred to in subsection (1) into account.
(4)	The judge must give a party an opportunity to make submissions, and to refer to relevant information, relating to the acquiring or taking into account of knowledge of the kind referred to in subsection (1) as is necessary to ensure that the party is not unfairly prejudiced.
(5)	This section does not exclude the application of the principles and rules of common law and equity relating to the effect of a certificate given by or on behalf of the Crown with respect to a matter of international affairs.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 144 and 145
	Division 2 — Proof of laws
328.	Judicial notice to be taken of Australian laws
(1)	Proof is not required about the provisions and coming into operation (in whole or in part) of —
(a)	a Commonwealth Act, a State Act, an Act or Ordinance of a Territory or an Imperial Act in force in Australia; or
(b)	a regulation, rule or by-law made, or purporting to be made, under such an Act or Ordinance; or
(c)	a proclamation or order of the Governor-General, the Governor of a State or the Administrator or Executive of a Territory made, or purporting to be made, under such an Act or Ordinance; or
(d)	an instrument of a legislative character (including rules of court) made, or purporting to be made, under such an Act or Ordinance, being an instrument that is required by or under a law to be published, or the making of which is required by or under a law to be notified, in any government or official gazette (by whatever name called) or any government or official website.
(2)	A judge may inform themselves about any matter referred to in subsection (1) in any way that the judge thinks fit.
(3)	A reference in this section to an Act, being an Act of an Australian Parliament, includes a reference to a private Act passed by that Parliament.
(4)	This section does not exclude the application of the principles and rules of common law and equity relating to the effect of a certificate given by or on behalf of the Crown with respect to a matter of international affairs.
(5)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 143 and 145
329.	Proof of laws of foreign countries
(1)	Evidence of a statute, proclamation, treaty or act of state of a foreign country may be adduced in a proceeding by producing —
(a)	a document, containing the statute, proclamation, treaty or act of state, that purports to have been printed or published by the government or official printer of the country or by authority of the government or administration of the country; or
(b)	a document, containing the statute, proclamation, treaty or act of state, that appears to the court to be a reliable source of information; or
(c)	a document that is or would be used in the courts of the country to inform the courts about, or to prove, the statute, proclamation, treaty or act of state; or
(d)	a copy of the statute, proclamation, treaty or act of state that is proved to be an examined copy.
(2)	A reference in this section to a statute of a foreign country includes a reference to the following —
(a)	a regulation, rule or by-law of the foreign country;
(b)	an instrument of a legislative character (including rules of court) made under a statute of the foreign country.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 174
330.	Evidence of law reports of foreign countries
(1)	Evidence of the unwritten or common law of a foreign country may be adduced by producing a document containing reports of judgments of courts of the country if the document is or would be used in the courts of the country to inform the courts about the unwritten or common law of the country.
(2)	Evidence of the interpretation of a statute of a foreign country may be adduced by producing a document containing reports of judgments of courts of the country if the document is or would be used in the courts of the country to inform the courts about the interpretation of the statute.
(3)	The court may, but is not required to, receive as evidence of the unwritten or common law of a foreign country, or of the interpretation of a statute of a foreign country, any treatise or explanation relating to that law contained in a text book or other published document about the laws of that foreign country.
(4)	A reference in this section to a statute of a foreign country includes a reference to the following —
(a)	a regulation, rule or by-law of the foreign country;
(b)	an instrument of a legislative character (including rules of court) made under a statute of the foreign country.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 71
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 175
331.	Questions of foreign law to be decided by judge
If, in a proceeding in which there is a jury, it is necessary to ascertain the law of another country that is applicable to the facts of the case, any question as to the effect of the evidence adduced with respect to that law must be decided by the judge alone.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 176
	Division 3 — Proof of public documents and other official matters
332.	Presumption as to authenticity of official publications
(1)	It is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that a document that purports to be any of the following is what it purports to be and was published on the day on which it purports to have been published —
(a)	a government or official gazette (by whatever name called) of this State, the Commonwealth, another State, a Territory or a foreign country;
(b)	a document printed or published by the Government Printer or by the government or official printer of the Commonwealth, another State or a Territory;
(c)	a document printed or published by authority of the government or administration of this State, the Commonwealth, another State, a Territory or a foreign country.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 153
333.	Presumption as to regularity of acts notified in official publications
(1)	If there is produced to a court a document to which this section applies, and the doing of any of the following acts is notified or published in the document, it is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that the act was duly done and, if the day on which the act was done appears in the document, that it was done on that day —
(a)	an act of the Governor-General or of the Governor of a State or the Administrator of a Territory;
(b)	an act of a person authorised or empowered to do the act by an Australian law or a law of a foreign country.
(2)	This section applies to the following documents —
(a)	a copy of a government or official gazette (by whatever name called) of this State, the Commonwealth, another State, a Territory or a foreign country;
(b)	a document that purports to have been printed or published by the Government Printer or by the government or official printer of the Commonwealth, another State or a Territory;
(c)	a document that purports to have been printed or published by authority of the government or administration of this State, the Commonwealth, another State, a Territory or a foreign country.
(3)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 153
334.	Presumption as to authenticity of Parliamentary documents
(1)	It is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that a document purporting to have been printed or published by authority of an Australian Parliament, a House of an Australian Parliament, a committee of a House of an Australian Parliament or a committee of an Australian Parliament —
(a)	is what it purports to be; and
(b)	was published on the day on which it purports to have been published.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 154
335.	Presumption as to authenticity of Commonwealth records and public documents
(1)	In this section —
Commonwealth record has the meaning given in the Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) Dictionary Part 1.
(2)	Evidence of a Commonwealth record or of a public document of this State, another State or a Territory may be adduced by producing a document that —
(a)	purports to be a Commonwealth record or public document and to be signed or sealed by —
(i)	a Minister of the Commonwealth, or a Minister of this or another State or a Territory, as the case requires; or
(ii)	a person who might reasonably be supposed to have custody of the record or document;
or
(b)	purports to be a copy of or extract from the record or document that is certified to be a true copy or extract by —
(i)	a Minister of the Commonwealth, or a Minister of this or another State or a Territory, as the case requires; or
(ii)	a person who might reasonably be supposed to have custody of the record or document.
(3)	If the document is produced, it is presumed, unless evidence that is sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced, that —
(a)	the document is the record, public document, copy or extract that it purports to be; and
(b)	the Minister of the Commonwealth, the Minister of this or another State or a Territory or person signed or sealed the record or document, or certified the copy or extract as a true copy or extract, as the case requires.
(4)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 155
336.	Presumption as to authenticity of copies, extracts and summaries of public documents
(1)	A document that purports to be a copy of, or an extract from or summary of, a public document is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, to be a copy of the public document, or an extract from or summary of the public document, if it purports to have been —
(a)	sealed with the seal of a person who, or a body that, might reasonably be supposed to have the custody of the public document; or
(b)	certified as a copy, extract or summary by a person who might reasonably be supposed to have custody of the public document.
(2)	If an officer entrusted with the custody of a public document is required by a court to produce the public document, it is sufficient compliance with the requirement for the officer to produce a copy of, or extract from, the public document if it purports to be signed and certified by the officer as a true copy or extract.
(3)	It is sufficient production of a copy or extract for the purposes of subsection (2) if the officer sends it by prepaid post, or causes it to be delivered, to —
(a)	the proper officer of the court in which it is to be produced; or
(b)	the person before whom it is to be produced.
(4)	The court before which a copy or extract is produced under subsection (2) may direct the officer to produce the original public document.
(5)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 156
337.	Presumption as to authenticity of official seals and signatures
(1)	In this section —
office holder means —
(a)	the Sovereign; or
(b)	the Governor-General; or
(c)	the Governor of a State; or
(d)	the Administrator of a Territory; or
(e)	a person holding any other office under an Australian law or a law of a foreign country.
(2)	If the imprint of a seal appears on a document and purports to be the imprint of any of the following, it is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that the imprint is the imprint of that seal, and the document was duly sealed as it purports to have been sealed —
(a)	a Royal Great Seal;
(b)	the Great Seal of Australia;
(c)	another seal of the Commonwealth;
(d)	a seal of a State, a Territory or a foreign country;
(e)	the seal of a body (including a court or a tribunal), or a body corporate, established by or under Royal Charter or by an Australian law or the law of a foreign country.
(3)	If the imprint of a seal appears on a document and purports to be the imprint of the seal of an office holder, it is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that —
(a)	the imprint is the imprint of that seal; and
(b)	the document was duly sealed by the office holder acting in the office holder’s official capacity; and
(c)	the office holder held the relevant office when the document was sealed.
(4)	If a document purports to have been signed by an office holder in the office holder’s official capacity, it is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that —
(a)	the document was signed by the office holder acting in that capacity; and
(b)	the office holder held the relevant office when the document was signed.
(5)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 150 and 151
338.	Proof of public documents of other States or Territories
(1)	A public document, or a certified copy of a public document, of another State or a Territory that purports to be sealed, or signed and sealed, or signed alone, as directed by the law of that State or Territory and that is admissible for a purpose in that State or Territory under the law of that State or Territory is admissible in evidence to the same extent and for that purpose in this State —
(a)	without proof of the seal or signature or of the official character of the person appearing to have signed the document; and
(b)	without further proof in every case in which the original document could have been received in evidence.
(2)	A public document of another State or a Territory that is admissible in evidence for any purpose in that State or Territory under the law of that State or Territory without proof of the following matters is admissible in evidence to the same extent and for any purpose in this State without proof of those matters —
(a)	the seal or signature authenticating the document;
(b)	the judicial or official character of the person appearing to have signed the document.
(3)	This section only applies to documents that are public records of another State or a Territory.
(4)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 158
339.	Proof of public documents relating to judgments, acts and processes of courts
(1)	Evidence of a public document that is a judgment (including reasons for judgment), act or other process of an Australian court or a foreign court, or that is a document lodged with an Australian court or a foreign court, may be adduced by producing a document that purports to be a copy of the public document and that —
(a)	is proved to be an examined copy; or
(b)	purports to be sealed with the seal of that court; or
(c)	purports to be signed by a judge, magistrate, registrar or other proper officer of that court.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 157
340.	Proof of official statistics
(1)	In this section —
Australian Statistician —
(a)	means the Australian Statistician referred to in the Australian Bureau of Statistics Act 1975 (Commonwealth) section 5(2); and
(b)	includes any person to whom the powers of the Australian Statistician under the Census and Statistics Act 1905 (Commonwealth) section 12 have been delegated.
(2)	A document containing statistics or abstracts is evidence that those statistics or abstracts were compiled and analysed by the Australian Statistician under the Census and Statistics Act 1905 (Commonwealth) if the document purports —
(a)	to be published by the Australian Statistician; and
(b)	to contain statistics or abstracts compiled and analysed by the Australian Statistician under the Census and Statistics Act 1905 (Commonwealth).
(3)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 159
	Division 4 — Proof of other matters relating to documents and things
341.	Presumption relating to documents and things produced by processes and devices
(1)	This section applies to a document or thing —
(a)	that is produced wholly or partly by a device or process; and
(b)	that is tendered by a party who asserts that, in producing the document or thing, the device or process has produced a particular outcome.
(2)	If it is reasonably open to find that the device or process is one that, or is of a kind that, if properly used, ordinarily produces that outcome, it is presumed (unless evidence sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced) that, in producing the document or thing on the occasion in question, the device or process produced that outcome.
Example for this section:
It would not be necessary to call evidence to prove that a photocopier normally produced complete copies of documents and that it was working properly when it was used to photocopy a particular document.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 146
342.	Presumption relating to business documents produced by processes and devices
(1)	This section applies to a document —
(a)	that is produced wholly or partly by a device or process; and
(b)	that is tendered by a party who asserts that, in producing the document, the device or process has produced a particular outcome.
(2)	It is presumed (unless evidence sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced) that, in producing the document on the occasion in question, the device or process produced that outcome if —
(a)	the document is, or was at the time it was produced, part of the records of, or kept for the purposes of, a business (whether or not the business is still in existence); and
(b)	the device or process is or was at that time used for the purposes of the business.
(3)	Subsection (2) does not apply to the contents of a document that was produced —
(a)	for the purpose of conducting, or for or in contemplation of or in connection with, an Australian or overseas proceeding; or
(b)	in connection with an investigation relating or leading to a criminal proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 147
343.	Findings about accuracy of documents produced by devices and processes
(1)	This section applies to a document that is tendered by a party who asserts that the document is admissible as evidence of the contents of another document because —
(a)	the document is a copy or accurate reproduction of the other document; and
(b)	the document has been produced by a device that reproduces the contents of documents or has been made by any other process or in any other way.
(2)	In making a finding about whether the document is a copy or accurate reproduction of another document, the court is not bound by the rules of evidence and may make that finding —
(a)	based on its own knowledge about the nature and reliability of the device or process by which, or way in which, the document was produced; or
(b)	based on evidence of a person with knowledge and experience of the device or process by which, or way in which, the copy or reproduction was made; or
(c)	based on evidence of a person who has compared the contents of both documents and found them to be identical; or
(d)	on any other basis it considers appropriate in the circumstances.
(3)	The court must state the reason for making a finding that the document is or is not a copy or accurate reproduction of another document if requested to do so by a party to the proceeding.
(4)	If a document is made by a device or process that is prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this subsection, the device or process is presumed to produce a copy or accurate reproduction of the document purportedly copied or reproduced, unless the contrary is proved.
(5)	This section does not apply to or in respect of a copy of a document if the copy is admissible under another written law.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 73A
344.	Presumption as to documents attested by justices, lawyers and notaries public
It is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that a document was attested or verified by, or signed or acknowledged before, a justice of the peace, lawyer or notary public, if —
(a)	an Australian law requires, authorises or permits it to be attested, verified, signed or acknowledged by a justice of the peace, a lawyer or a notary public, as the case may be; and
(b)	it purports to have been attested, verified, signed or acknowledged as referred to in paragraph (a).
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 148
345.	Testimony of attesting witness not required
It is not necessary to adduce the evidence of an attesting witness to a document (not being a testamentary document) to prove that the document was signed or attested as it purports to have been signed or attested.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 149
346.	Presumption as to documents produced from proper custody
(1)	If a document that is or purports to be more than 20 years of age is produced from proper custody, it is presumed, unless the contrary is proved, that —
(a)	the document is the document that it purports to be; and
(b)	if it purports to have been executed or attested by a person — it was duly executed or attested by that person.
(2)	The presumption referred to in subsection (1) extends to a copy of a document referred to in subsection (1) that is admitted into evidence as if the copy were the document.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 73N
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 152
347.	Proof of foreign documents admissible in foreign countries
(1)	A document that is admissible in evidence for a purpose in a foreign court prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this section without proof of the following matters is admissible in evidence to the same extent and for the same purpose in this State without proof of those matters —
(a)	the seal or signature authenticating the document; or
(b)	the judicial or official character of the person appearing to have signed the document.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 62
	Division 5 — Proof of communications
348.	Presumption relating to receipt of postal articles
(1)	In this section —
postal article has the meaning given in the Australian Postal Corporation Act 1989 (Commonwealth) section 3;
working day means a day that is not —
(a)	a Saturday or a Sunday; or
(b)	a public holiday or a bank holiday in the place to which the postal article was addressed.
(2)	It is presumed (unless evidence sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced) that a postal article sent by prepaid post addressed to a person at a specified address in Australia or in an external Territory was received by the person at that address on the 7th working day after having been posted.
(3)	This section does not apply if —
(a)	the proceeding relates to a contract; and
(b)	all the parties to the proceeding are parties to the contract; and
(c)	subsection (2) is inconsistent with a term of the contract.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 160
349.	Presumptions relating to electronic communications
(1)	If a document purports to contain a record of an electronic communication, it is presumed (unless evidence sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced) that the communication —
(a)	was sent or made in the form of electronic communication that appears from the document to have been the form by which it was sent or made; and
(b)	was sent or made by or on behalf of the person by or on whose behalf it appears from the document to have been sent or made; and
(c)	was sent or made on the day on which, at the time at which and from the place from which it appears from the document to have been sent or made; and
(d)	was received at the destination to which it appears from the document to have been sent; and
(e)	if it appears from the document that the sending of the communication concluded at a particular time — was received at that destination at that time.
(2)	A provision of subsection (1) does not apply if —
(a)	the proceeding relates to a contract; and
(b)	all the parties to the proceeding are parties to the contract; and
(c)	the provision is inconsistent with a term of the contract.
(3)	This section does not apply to a document that purports to contain a record of a message transmitted by means of a lettergram or telegram.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 161
350.	Presumption relating to receipt of lettergrams and telegrams
(1)	If a document purports to contain a record of a message transmitted by means of a lettergram or telegram, it is presumed (unless evidence sufficient to raise doubt about the presumption is adduced) that the message was received by the person to whom it was addressed 24 hours after the message was delivered to a post office for transmission as a lettergram or telegram.
(2)	This section does not apply if —
(a)	the proceeding relates to a contract; and
(b)	all the parties to the proceeding are parties to the contract; and
(c)	subsection (1) is inconsistent with a term of the contract.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 162
	Division 6 — Proof of matters relating to documents or things by affidavit or written statement
351.	Application of Division
This Division applies to evidence of a fact that is, because of a provision of this Act referred to in the Table, to be proved in relation to a document or thing.
Table

Subject matter

Proof of contents of documents

Hearsay rule exception for business records

Hearsay rule exception for tags, labels and writing

Hearsay rule exception for electronic communication
