Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 170(1)
352.	Persons who may give evidence
(1)	Evidence may be given by —
(a)	a person who, at the relevant time or afterwards, had a position of responsibility in relation to making or keeping the document or thing; or
(b)	any of the following persons (each of whom is an authorised person) —
(i)	a person who is an authorised witness for an affidavit or statutory declaration that is made outside the State under the Oaths, Affidavits and Statutory Declarations Act 2005 section 9(8) or 12(6)(b) or (c);
(ii)	a police officer of or above the rank of sergeant;
(iii)	a person authorised by the Minister for the purposes of this section.
(2)	Despite subsection (1)(b), evidence must not be given by an authorised person who, at the relevant time or afterwards, did not have a position of responsibility in relation to making or keeping the document or thing unless it appears to the court that —
(a)	it is not reasonably practicable for the evidence to be given by a person who had, at the relevant time or afterwards, a position of responsibility in relation to making or keeping the document or thing; or
(b)	having regard to all the circumstances of the case, undue expense would be caused by calling a person who had, at the relevant time or afterwards, a position of responsibility in relation to making or keeping the document or thing, as a witness.
(3)	Subsection (1)(b) does not apply to evidence of a fact that must be proved in relation to a document or thing because of section 76, 77 or 78.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 171
353.	Form in which evidence may be given
Evidence may be given by affidavit or, if the evidence relates to a public document, by a written statement.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 170(2)
354.	Evidence based on knowledge, belief or information
(1)	Despite Part 3, evidence may include evidence based on the knowledge and belief of the person who gives it, or on information that that person has.
(2)	An affidavit or statement that includes evidence based on knowledge, information or belief must set out the source of the knowledge or information or the basis of the belief.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 172
355.	Notification of other parties
(1)	A copy of the affidavit or statement must be served on each party within a reasonable time before the hearing of the proceeding.
(2)	The party who tenders the affidavit or statement must, if another party requests it, call the deponent or person who made the statement to give evidence but need not otherwise do so.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 173
	Division 7 — Proof of other matters by certificate or affidavit
356.	Certificate of expert evidence
(1)	Evidence of a person’s opinion may be adduced by tendering a certificate (an expert certificate) signed by the person that —
(a)	states the person’s name and address; and
(b)	states that the person has specialised knowledge based on the person’s training, study or experience, as specified in the certificate; and
(c)	sets out an opinion that the person holds and that is expressed to be wholly or substantially based on that knowledge.
(2)	Subsection (1) does not apply unless the party seeking to tender the expert certificate has served on each other party —
(a)	a copy of the certificate; and
(b)	a written notice stating that the party proposes to tender the certificate as evidence of the opinion.
(3)	Service must be effected not later than —
(a)	21 days before the hearing; or
(b)	if, on application by the party before or after service, the court substitutes a different period — the beginning of that period.
(4)	Service for the purposes of subsection (2) may be proved by affidavit.
(5)	A party on whom the documents referred to in subsection (2) are served may, by written notice served on the party proposing to tender the expert certificate, require the party to call the person who signed the certificate to give evidence.
(6)	The expert certificate is not admissible as evidence if that requirement is made.
(7)	The court may make any order with respect to costs that it considers just against a party who has, without reasonable cause, required a party to call a person to give evidence under this section.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 177
357.	Certificate evidence of convictions, acquittals, sentences and other court matters
(1)	In this section —
acquittal includes the dismissal of the charge in question by an applicable court;
applicable court means an Australian court or a foreign court.
(2)	This section applies to the following facts —
(a)	the conviction or acquittal before or by an applicable court of a person charged with an offence;
(b)	the sentencing of a person to any punishment or pecuniary penalty by an applicable court;
(c)	an order by an applicable court;
(d)	the pendency or existence at any time before an applicable court of a civil or criminal proceeding.
(3)	Evidence of a fact to which this section applies may be given by a certificate signed by a judge, a magistrate or a registrar or other proper officer of the applicable court —
(a)	showing the fact, or purporting to contain particulars, of the record, indictment, conviction, acquittal, sentence, order or proceeding in question; and
(b)	stating the time and place of the conviction, acquittal, sentence, order or proceeding; and
(c)	stating the title of the applicable court.
(4)	A certificate given under this section showing a conviction, acquittal, sentence or order is also evidence of the particular offence or matter in respect of which the conviction, acquittal, sentence or order was had, passed or made, if stated in the certificate.
(5)	A certificate given under this section showing the pendency or existence of a proceeding is also evidence of the particular nature and occasion, or ground and cause, of the proceeding, if stated in the certificate.
(6)	A certificate given under this section purporting to contain particulars of a record, indictment, conviction, acquittal, sentence, order or proceeding is also evidence of the matters stated in the certificate.
(7)	This section does not affect the operation of section 107 or any other provision of this Act that limits the admissibility of evidence of a fact to which this section applies.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 178
358.	Affidavit evidence of identity of convicted persons by State and Territory police
(1)	This section applies if a member of a police force of a State or Territory —
(a)	makes an affidavit in the form prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this section; and
(b)	states in the affidavit that the member is a fingerprint expert for that police force.
(2)	For the purpose of proving before a court the identity of a person alleged to have been convicted in that State or Territory of an offence, the affidavit is evidence in a proceeding that the person whose fingerprints are shown on a fingerprint card referred to in the affidavit and marked for identification —
(a)	is the person referred to in a certificate of conviction, or certified copy of conviction annexed to the affidavit, as having been convicted of an offence; and
(b)	was convicted of that offence; and
(c)	was convicted of any other offence of which the person is stated in the affidavit to have been convicted.
(3)	For the purposes of this section, if a Territory does not have its own police force, the police force performing the policing functions of the Territory is taken to be the police force of the Territory.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 179
359.	Affidavit evidence of identity of convicted persons by AFP employees and special members
(1)	In this section —
AFP employee has the meaning given in the Australian Federal Police Act 1979 (Commonwealth) section 4(1);
AFP special member means a special member of the Australian Federal Police as defined in the Australian Federal Police Act 1979 (Commonwealth) section 4(1).
(2)	This section applies if an AFP employee or AFP special member —
(a)	makes an affidavit in the form prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this section; and
(b)	states in the affidavit that the AFP employee or AFP special member is a fingerprint expert for the Australian Federal Police.
(3)	For the purpose of proving before a court the identity of a person alleged to have been convicted of any offence against a law of the Commonwealth, the affidavit is evidence in a proceeding that the person whose fingerprints are shown on a fingerprint card referred to in the affidavit and marked for identification —
(a)	is the person referred to in a certificate of conviction, or certified copy of conviction annexed to the affidavit, as having been convicted of an offence; and
(b)	was convicted of that offence; and
(c)	was convicted of any other offence of which the person is stated in the affidavit to have been convicted.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 180
360.	Forensic biology certificates
(1)	In this section and section 361 —
examination, of a thing, includes testing the thing;
forensic scientist means a person appointed under section 362(1) as a forensic scientist for the purposes of this section;
thing includes anything in or on or otherwise attached to the thing.
(2)	This section applies to a criminal proceeding.
(3)	A certificate (a forensic biology certificate) purporting to be signed by a forensic scientist and stating any of the following matters is evidence of the matter —
(a)	that a stated thing was received at a stated laboratory on a stated day;
(b)	that the thing was examined at the laboratory on a stated day or between stated days;
(c)	that a DNA profile was, or a stated number of DNA profiles were, obtained from the thing;
(d)	that a forensic scientist —
(i)	examined the laboratory’s records relating to the receipt, storage and examination of the thing, including any examination of the thing that was done by a person other than the forensic scientist; and
(ii)	confirms that the records indicate that all quality assurance procedures for the receipt, storage and examination of the thing that applied in the laboratory at the time the thing was examined were complied with.
(4)	In subsection (3) —
DNA profile means the result from DNA analysis.
(5)	Any equipment used in examining the thing at the laboratory is taken to have given accurate results in the absence of evidence to the contrary.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 50B
361.	Procedure for using forensic biology certificates
(1)	In this section and section 362 —
authorised officer, for a laboratory, means a person who holds an office prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of this definition in relation to that laboratory.
(2)	In this section —
forensic biology certificate has the meaning given in section 360(3).
(3)	A party who intends to rely on a forensic biology certificate in a criminal proceeding must —
(a)	give a copy of the certificate to each other party at least 14 days before the day set for the start of the trial in the proceeding; and
(b)	at the hearing, call the forensic scientist who gave the certificate to give evidence, unless the parties agree otherwise.
(4)	If the authorised officer for a laboratory receives a written request from a party for a copy of the laboratory’s records relating to the receipt, storage and examination of the thing referred to in a forensic biology certificate, the authorised officer must give the party a copy of the records within 10 days after receiving the request.
(5)	If a party intends to dispute a matter stated in the forensic biology certificate, the party must, at least 7 days before the day set for the start of the trial in the proceeding, give the authorised officer and each other party written notice of the matter to be disputed.
(6)	A party disputing a matter stated in the forensic biology certificate may, with the leave of the court, require the party relying on the certificate to call any person involved in the receipt, storage or examination of the thing referred to in the certificate to give evidence at the hearing.
(7)	The court may give leave only if satisfied —
(a)	that an irregularity may exist in relation to the receipt, storage or examination of the thing about which the person to be called is able to give evidence; and
(b)	that it is in the interests of justice that the person be called to give evidence.
(8)	The court may, by order, extend or shorten a time fixed by this section by fixing a new time or by extending or shortening the time.
(9)	An application for an order under subsection (8) may be made before or after the time fixed by this section expires.
(10)	A document required to be given under this section must be given in accordance with any regulations or rules of court made for the purposes of this section.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 50B
362.	Appointment of persons as forensic scientists for purposes of s. 360
(1)	The authorised officer for a laboratory may, in writing, appoint a person as a forensic scientist for the purposes of section 360 if satisfied that the person has the necessary qualifications and experience to be a forensic scientist.
(2)	A person may be appointed as a forensic scientist for the purposes of section 360 for a fixed or indefinite period.
(3)	The authorised officer for a laboratory may, in writing, revoke an appointment under subsection (1) at any time.
363.	Proof of service of statutory notifications, notices, orders and directions
(1)	The service, giving or sending under an Australian law of a written notification, notice, order or direction may be proved by affidavit of the person who served, gave or sent it.
(2)	A person who, for the purposes of a proceeding, makes an affidavit referred to in this section is not, because of making the affidavit, excused from attending for cross-examination if required to do so by a party to the proceeding.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 181
	Division 8 — Proof of particular matters in criminal proceedings
364.	Proof of intention to injure, deceive or defraud
In any criminal proceeding in which it is necessary to prove an intention to injure or deceive or defraud, or an intention to enable another person to deceive or defraud, it is not necessary to prove an intention to injure or deceive or defraud a particular person or an intention to enable any particular person to deceive or defraud any particular person.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 29
365.	Evidence of stealing or receiving property from aircraft, vehicle, vessel or related premises
(1)	In this section —
aircraft, vehicle and vessel have the meanings given in The Criminal Code section 1(1);
shipping document includes a bill of lading, manifest, shipping receipt, delivery order, packing list, waybill or invoice.
(2)	This section applies to a criminal proceeding for an offence of —
(a)	stealing property from an aircraft, vehicle or vessel; or
(b)	stealing property from any building, structure or place used in connection with the docking, loading or unloading of an aircraft, vehicle or vessel; or
(c)	receiving any property stolen as referred to in paragraph (a) or (b); or
(d)	being in possession of anything that may reasonably be suspected of being stolen as referred to in paragraph (a) or (b).
(3)	In the proceeding —
(a)	evidence may be given of any writing, printing or marks on the property the subject of the proceeding without producing or giving notice to produce the original writing, printing or marks; and
(b)	any apparently genuine document purporting to be a shipping document for the property or for property that may reasonably be assumed to be the same as the property the subject of the proceeding —
(i)	is admissible in evidence on production and without further proof; and
(ii)	is evidence of the particulars contained in the document and that the ownership of the property is in the consignee referred to in the document or an assignee of that consignee.
(4)	In deciding whether any property described in a shipping document is the same as the property the subject of the proceeding, regard must be had to the following —
(a)	the description of the property in the document;
(b)	any writing, printing or marks on the property, or on any package containing the property, as detailed in the document;
(c)	the source from which the document was obtained;
(d)	how the document was produced;
(e)	the circumstances in which the document came into the custody of the person producing it;
(f)	any other relevant circumstances.
(5)	This section applies if evidence is adduced that the stealing occurred in or from a building, structure or place referred to in subsection (2)(b), whether or not the building, structure or place is specified in the charge for the offence.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 41A
366.	Proof of stealing money
In a criminal proceeding for an offence of stealing money —
(a)	an entry in a book of account kept or made by the accused, or under the accused’s supervision, that purports to be an entry of the receipt of any money is evidence that the money was received by the accused; and
(b)	it is not necessary to prove that the accused stole a specific sum of money if, on an examination of any books of account or entries in a book of account kept or made by the accused, or under the accused’s supervision, or any other evidence —
(i)	there is proof of a general deficiency; and
(ii)	the trier of fact is satisfied that the accused stole the deficient money or any part of it.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 44
	Part 10 — Miscellaneous
367.	Inferences
If a question arises about the application of a provision of this Act in relation to a document or thing, the court may —
(a)	examine the document or thing; and
(b)	draw any reasonable inferences from it as well as from other matters from which inferences may properly be drawn.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 183
368.	Accused may admit matters and give consents
(1)	In or before a criminal proceeding, an accused may —
(a)	admit any matter of fact that a party to a civil proceeding may admit; and
(b)	give any consent that a party to a civil proceeding may give.
(2)	An accused’s admission or consent is not effective for the purposes of subsection (1) unless —
(a)	the accused has been advised to make the admission or give the consent by the accused’s legal counsel; or
(b)	the court is satisfied that the accused understands the consequences of making the admission or giving the consent.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 184
369.	Impounding documents
(1)	A court may direct that a document that has been tendered or produced before the court (whether or not it is admitted in evidence) must be impounded and kept in the custody of an officer of the court or of another person for the period, and subject to the conditions, that the court thinks fit.
(2)	This section extends to a proceeding before a person acting judicially.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 188
370.	Preliminary questions
(1)	In this section, a preliminary question is a question as to whether a particular fact exists, being a question of fact that must be decided by the court before it makes a determination as to whether —
(a)	evidence should be admitted (whether in the exercise of a discretion or not); or
(b)	evidence can be used against a person; or
(c)	a witness is competent or compellable.
(2)	If there is a jury, each of the following preliminary questions must be heard and determined in the jury’s absence —
(a)	a question of whether particular evidence is evidence of an admission, or evidence to which section 204 applies;
(b)	a question of whether evidence of an admission, or evidence to which section 204 applies, should be admitted.
(3)	In the hearing of a preliminary question about whether an accused’s admission should be admitted into evidence (whether in the exercise of a discretion or not) in a criminal proceeding, the issue of the admission’s truth or untruth must be disregarded unless the issue is introduced by the accused.
(4)	If there is a jury, the jury must not be present at a hearing to decide any other preliminary question unless the court otherwise orders.
(5)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account in deciding whether to make that order, it must take into account —
(a)	whether the evidence to be adduced in the course of that hearing is likely to be prejudicial to the accused; and
(b)	whether the evidence concerned will be adduced in the course of the hearing to decide the preliminary question; and
(c)	whether the evidence to be adduced in the course of that hearing would be admitted if adduced at another stage of the hearing (other than in another hearing to decide a preliminary question or, in a criminal proceeding, a hearing in relation to sentencing).
(6)	Section 186 applies to a hearing to decide a preliminary question, despite section 186(8).
(7)	In the application of Part 3, 4 or 5 to a hearing to determine a preliminary question, the facts in issue are taken to include the question of fact to which the hearing relates.
(8)	If a jury in a proceeding was not present at a hearing to determine a preliminary question, evidence must not be adduced in the proceeding of evidence given by a witness at the hearing unless —
(a)	it is inconsistent with other evidence given by the witness in the proceeding; or
(b)	the witness has died.
(9)	If, after a hearing under this section, a document or thing is ruled admissible as evidence, it is not necessary for the party proposing to adduce the evidence to tender the evidence again.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 189
371.	Waiver of rules of evidence
(1)	A court may, if the parties consent, by order dispense with the application of any of the following in relation to particular evidence or generally —
(a)	the provisions of Part 2 Division 1 Subdivision 3, 4 or 5;
(b)	the provisions of Part 2 Division 2 or 3;
(c)	the provisions of Part 3 Divisions 2 to 8.
(2)	In a criminal proceeding, an accused’s consent is not effective for the purposes of subsection (1) unless —
(a)	the accused has been advised to do so by the accused’s legal counsel; or
(b)	the court is satisfied that the accused understands the consequences of giving the consent.
(3)	In a civil proceeding, the court may order that any of the provisions mentioned in subsection (1) do not apply in relation to evidence if —
(a)	the matter to which the evidence relates is not genuinely in dispute; or
(b)	the application of those provisions would cause or involve unnecessary expense or delay.
(4)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account in deciding whether to exercise the power conferred by subsection (3), it must take into account —
(a)	the importance of the evidence in the proceeding; and
(b)	the nature of the cause of action or defence and the nature of the subject matter of the proceeding; and
(c)	the probative value of the evidence; and
(d)	the powers of the court (if any) to adjourn the hearing, to make another order or to give a direction in relation to the evidence.
(5)	This section does not permit a court to dispense with the application of section 56 or 57.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 190
372.	Agreements as to facts
(1)	In this section —
agreed fact means a fact that the parties to a proceeding have agreed is not, for the purposes of the proceeding, to be disputed.
(2)	In a proceeding —
(a)	evidence is not required to prove the existence of an agreed fact unless the court gives leave; and
(b)	evidence must not be adduced to contradict or qualify an agreed fact unless the court gives leave.
(3)	Subsection (2) does not apply unless the agreed fact —
(a)	is stated in an agreement in writing signed by the parties or by legal counsel or prosecutors representing the parties and adduced in evidence in the proceeding; or
(b)	with the leave of the court, is stated by a party before the court with the agreement of all other parties.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 191
373.	Leave, permission or direction may be subject to conditions
(1)	If, because of this Act, a court may give any leave, permission or direction, the leave, permission or direction may be given subject to the conditions that the court thinks fit.
(2)	Without limiting the matters that the court may take into account in deciding whether to give the leave, permission or direction, the court must take into account —
(a)	the extent to which to do so would be likely to add unduly to, or to shorten, the length of the hearing; and
(b)	the extent to which to do so would be unfair to a party or to a witness; and
(c)	the importance of the evidence in relation to which the leave, permission or direction is sought; and
(d)	the nature of the proceeding; and
(e)	the power (if any) of the court to adjourn the hearing or to make another order or to give a direction in relation to the evidence.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 192
374.	Advance rulings and findings
If a question arises in any proceeding about any of the following, the court may, if it considers it appropriate to do so, give a ruling or make a finding in relation to the question before the evidence concerned is adduced in the proceeding —
(a)	the admissibility or use of evidence proposed to be adduced;
(b)	the operation of a provision of this Act or another law in relation to evidence proposed to be adduced;
(c)	the giving of any leave, permission or direction that the court may give under this Act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 192A
375.	Additional powers of court in relation to discovery and disclosure
(1)	The powers of a court in relation to discovery and disclosure extend to enabling the court to make any orders the court thinks fit to ensure that the parties to a proceeding can adequately, and in an appropriate matter, inspect documents, including orders about any of the following —
(a)	methods of inspection;
(b)	adjournments;
(c)	costs.
(2)	A reference in this section to the powers of a court in relation to discovery and disclosure is a reference to the powers of a court in relation to —
(a)	the discovery or inspection of documents; and
(b)	ordering disclosure and exchange of evidence, intended evidence, documents and reports.
(3)	The power of the court to make an order for costs under subsection (1) is subject to the Criminal Procedure Act 2004 section 123.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 193(1)
376.	Additional power to make rules of court
(1)	The power of a person or body to make rules of court extends to making rules, not inconsistent with this Act or the regulations, prescribing matters —
(a)	required or permitted by this Act to be prescribed; or
(b)	necessary or convenient to be prescribed for carrying out or giving effect to the purposes of this Act; or
(c)	necessary or convenient to be prescribed for giving effect to the purposes of the Courts and Tribunals (Electronic Processes Facilitation) Act 2013 Part 2 (as applied by section 16 of this Act).
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), rules made under that subsection may provide for any of the following —
(a)	the discovery, exchange, inspection or disclosure of intended evidence, documents and reports of persons intended to be called by a party to give evidence in a proceeding;
(b)	the exclusion of evidence, or its admission subject to specified conditions, if the rules are not complied with.
(3)	The Courts and Tribunals (Electronic Processes Facilitation) Act 2013 section 20(3) and (4) apply as if the power conferred by subsection (1)(c) of this section were a power conferred by section 20(1) or (2) of that Act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 133
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 193(2) to (4)
377.	Witness who fails to attend proceeding
(1)	If a witness fails to appear when called in any proceeding and it is proved that the witness was duly bound by recognisance or served with a subpoena, summons or order to attend as a witness in the proceeding, the court may —
(a)	order the witness to show cause why execution of the recognisance or an attachment for disobedience to the subpoena, summons or order should not be issued against the witness; or
(b)	if it is proved that the non-appearance is without just cause or reasonable excuse, and that the witness will probably be able to give relevant evidence in the proceeding — issue a warrant to bring the witness before the court to give evidence at the proceeding.
(2)	Matters may be proved under this section orally or by affidavit.
(3)	An order to show cause under subsection (1)(a) may —
(a)	be made returnable before the court then sitting or at some future sitting; or
(b)	in respect of the non-appearance of a witness at a circuit court (as defined in the Supreme Court Act 1935 section 48) or on the trial of a case pending in the Supreme Court, be made returnable in the Supreme Court.
(4)	On return of the order to show cause, the court may deal with the case in the same way as the Supreme Court would deal with an order to similar effect made by the Supreme Court.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 16 and 17
378.	Payments to witnesses and others for eligible service
(1)	In this section —
eligible service has the meaning given in subsection (2);
inquest has the meaning given in the Coroners Act 1996 section 3;
liable person, in respect of a payment for eligible service, means —
(a)	if the eligible service is in a criminal proceeding and the prosecution was commenced by or on behalf of a local government or a statutory body — the local government or statutory body; or
(b)	in any other case — the State.
(2)	A person does eligible service if the person is, or attends at any place in order to be, any of the following —
(a)	a witness who is called by the prosecutor in a criminal proceeding;
(b)	a witness who is called by the accused in a criminal proceeding and who is a child or a special witness;
(c)	a witness in an inquest;
(d)	an interpreter in a criminal proceeding or an inquest;
(e)	a person approved under section 231 to provide a witness with support in a proceeding;
(f)	a person appointed under section 233 to act as a communicator in a proceeding;
(g)	a person who participates in a program the purpose of which is to prepare the following persons to give evidence in a criminal proceeding or an inquest —
(i)	a child who may be a witness;
(ii)	a person who is or may be declared to be a special witness;
(h)	a person who accompanies and assists another person described in paragraph (g) in order to enable the other person to attend the program described in that paragraph;
(i)	a person who accompanies and assists another person to attend and be a witness in a criminal proceeding or an inquest, if the other person is —
(i)	a child who may be a witness; or
(ii)	a person who is or may be declared to be a special witness.
(3)	A person who does eligible service is entitled to be paid by the liable person any allowances and expenses for doing that eligible service provided for by the regulations.
(4)	If a person does eligible service during any period in which the person is employed under a contract of service, the employer under the contract of service must pay to the person the earnings that the person could reasonably expect to have been paid under that contract during that period.
Penalty for this subsection: a fine of $2 000.
(5)	Subsection (4) applies even if doing the eligible service causes a breach of contract.
(6)	An employer who, in accordance with subsection (4), pays a person for a period in which the person does eligible service is entitled to be paid by the liable person the fees provided for by the regulations for the eligible service.
(7)	A person who does eligible service is entitled to be paid by the liable person the fees provided for by the regulations for doing that eligible service if —
(a)	subsection (4) does not apply to the person; or
(b)	subsection (4) applies to the person but the employer fails to pay the person in accordance with that subsection.
(8)	The regulations may specify circumstances in which subsections (6) and (7) do not apply.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 119
379.	Recovery of amounts payable
(1)	A claim to a liable person for payment under section 378 must be made and determined in accordance with the regulations.
(2)	The regulations may require information in support of a claim for payment to be verified by statutory declaration.
(3)	On the application of an accused who calls a witness in a criminal proceeding, the Minister may authorise the State to pay all or some of the amounts that would be payable under section 378 if the witness were called by the prosecutor in the proceeding.
(4)	An amount paid under section 378, or this section, by the State must be charged to the Consolidated Account, which this subsection appropriates accordingly.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 119
380.	Prohibited question not to be published
(1)	A person must not, without the express permission of a court, publish —
(a)	any question that the court has disallowed under section 50; or
(b)	any question that the court has disallowed because any answer that is likely to be given to the question would contravene the credibility rule; or
(c)	any question in respect of which the court has refused to give leave under Part 3 Division 7.
Penalty for this subsection: a fine of $6 000.
(2)	A person who contravenes subsection (1) —
(a)	is taken to be in contempt of the court that disallowed the question or refused to give leave; and
(b)	is punishable accordingly.
(3)	If a person’s act constitutes both an offence under subsection (1) and a contempt of court under subsection (2), the person is not liable to be punished twice.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 27
Evidence Act 1995 (Commonwealth) s. 195
381.	Identity of certain witnesses not to be published
(1)	This section applies —
(a)	in relation to a criminal proceeding — if a person is charged with a sexual offence; or
(b)	in relation to a civil proceeding — if it is alleged that a witness is the victim of sexual abuse or sexual harassment.
(2)	For the purposes of subsection (1)(a), a person is charged with a sexual offence if —
(a)	the person is charged in a prosecution notice or an indictment with committing a sexual offence; or
(b)	the person appears before a court charged with a sexual offence; or
(c)	a court before which the person is appearing commits the person for trial for a charge alleging a sexual offence.
(3)	The following information must not be published, except with the express permission of a court —
(a)	information that is likely to enable members of the public to identify an alleged victim of a sexual offence, sexual abuse or sexual harassment;
(b)	if an alleged victim of a sexual offence, sexual abuse or sexual harassment attends school — information that is likely to enable members of the public to identify the school at which the alleged victim attends.
(4)	If information is published in contravention of subsection (3), each of the following persons commits an offence —
(a)	in the case of information published in a newspaper or periodical publication — any proprietor, editor or publisher of the newspaper or periodical publication at the time the information was published;
(b)	in the case of information published in a program that is broadcast —
(i)	the person who transmitted or provided the program; and
(ii)	any person having functions in relation to the program that were comparable to the functions of an editor of a newspaper at the time the information was published;
(c)	in the case of information published in a manner not mentioned in paragraph (a) or (b) — the person who published the information.
Penalty for this subsection:
(a)	for an individual, a fine of $5 000;
(b)	for a body corporate, a fine of $25 000.
(5)	A court has jurisdiction to give permission under subsection (3) if the court is hearing or has jurisdiction to hear the proceeding concerned.
(6)	The giving of permission under subsection (3) does not excuse a contravention of the subsection that occurred before permission was given.
(7)	It is a defence to a charge of an offence under subsection (4) to prove that —
(a)	the person to whom the published matter relates gave written authorisation to the publication of the matter before it was published; and
(b)	at the time of giving that authorisation, the person who gave it —
(i)	had reached 18 years of age; and
(ii)	was not, because of a disability, incapable of making reasonable judgments in respect of the publication of the matter concerned.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 36C
382.	Customs prosecutions
This Act, as it applies to a Customs prosecution (as defined in the Customs Act 1901 (Commonwealth) section 244), does not affect the operation of the Customs Act 1901 (Commonwealth).
383.	Regulations
(1)	The Governor may make regulations prescribing all matters that are required or permitted by this Act to be prescribed, or are necessary or convenient to be prescribed for giving effect to the purposes of —
(a)	this Act; or
(b)	the Courts and Tribunals (Electronic Processes Facilitation) Act 2013 Part 2 (as it applies to this Act).
(2)	Without limiting subsection (1), the regulations may provide for the fees and expenses payable in relation to the taking of evidence or receiving of a submission by video link or audio link and the person liable for payment.
(3)	The regulations may provide that a contravention of a regulation is an offence and prescribe, for an offence against the regulations, a penalty of imprisonment for a term not exceeding 12 months and a fine not exceeding $100 000.
(4)	The Courts and Tribunals (Electronic Processes Facilitation) Act 2013 section 20(3) and (4) apply as if the power conferred by subsection (1)(b) of this section were a power conferred by section 20(1) or (2) of that Act.
Compare:
Evidence Act 1906 s. 131 and 133
384.	Review of Act
(1)	The Minister must review the operation and effectiveness of this Act, and prepare a report based on the review, as soon as practicable after the 5th anniversary and the 10th anniversary of the day on which this section comes into operation.
(2)	The Minister must cause the report to be laid before each House of Parliament as soon as practicable after it is prepared, but not later than 12 months after the 5th anniversary or the 10th anniversary, as the case may be.
385.	Review of provisions relating to recorded statements
(1)	In this section —
recorded statement provisions means —
(a)	Part 7 Division 4; and
(b)	the Criminal Investigation Act 2006 Part 11A.
(2)	The Minister must review the operation and effectiveness of the recorded statement provisions, and prepare a report based on the review, as soon as practicable after the 2nd anniversary of the day on which Part 7 Division 4 comes into operation.
(3)	The Minister must cause the report to be laid before each House of Parliament as soon as practicable after it is prepared, but not later than 12 months after the 2nd anniversary.
	Part 11 — Repeal, savings and transitional provisions
	Division 1 — Repeals
386.	Evidence Act 1906 repealed
The Evidence Act 1906 is repealed.
387.	Regulations under Evidence Act 1906 repealed
The following regulations are repealed —
(a)	the Evidence (Admissible Reproductions) Regulations 2003;
(b)	the Evidence (Examination of witnesses outside the State) Regulations 1991;
(c)	the Evidence (Fees, Allowances and Expenses) Regulations 2008;
(d)	the Evidence (Prescribed Persons) Regulations 2005;
(e)	the Evidence (Video and Audio Links Fees and Expenses) Regulations 1999;
(f)	the Evidence (Visual Recording of Interviews with Children and Persons with Mental Impairment) Regulations 2004.
	Division 2 — Savings and transitional provisions
388.	Terms used
In this Division —
former Act means the Evidence Act 1906;
former Act recording means —
(a)	a visual recording (as defined in section 228(1)) or an audio recording made by a police officer of this State before repeal day of a relevant statement (as defined in section 106H(3) of the former Act), being a relevant statement that was admitted or could have been admitted into evidence in a proceeding under section 106H of the former Act (whether or not it has been admitted into evidence under that section); or
(b)	a visual recording (as defined in section 228(1)) of an interview conducted before repeal day, being a recording that was admitted or could have been admitted as evidence in a proceeding under section 106HB of the former Act (whether or not it has been admitted as evidence under that section); or
(c)	a visual recording of evidence (as defined in section 106A of the former Act) made before repeal day;
repeal day means the day on which section 386 comes into operation.
389.	Application of Act to proceedings
(1)	A provision of this Act applies to a proceeding commenced on or after the day on which the provision comes into operation.
(2)	If a proceeding commenced before the day on which a provision of this Act comes into operation, the provision applies to that part of the proceeding that takes place on or after the day on which the provision comes into operation, other than a hearing in the proceeding that commenced before the day on which the provision comes into operation.
(3)	The former Act continues to apply to a hearing in a proceeding if the hearing commenced before repeal day and —
(a)	continued on or after repeal day; or
(b)	was adjourned until repeal day or a day after repeal day.
(4)	This section is subject to the other provisions of this Division.
390.	Documents served or given
(1)	A reference in a provision of this Act to a document served or given to a person includes a reference to a document served or given before the day on which the provision comes into operation.
(2)	A document served or given before the day on which a provision of this Act comes into operation is taken to have been served or given in accordance with the requirements (if any) of that provision if it was served or given in accordance with the requirements (if any) —
(a)	that applied under the former Act to the service or giving of the document in the circumstances provided for by the provision of this Act; or
(b)	that would have applied if the provision of this Act had already come into operation.
(3)	If a provision of this Act specifies a time limit for doing a thing that starts when a document is served or given, and the document was served or given before the day on which the provision comes into operation, the time limit is taken to end on the later of the following days —
(a)	the day that is 21 days after the day on which the provision comes into operation;
(b)	the day on which the time limit would have ended but for this section.
391.	Documents and other things examined, copied or viewed
If a provision of this Act requires a party to have been given a reasonable opportunity to examine, copy or view any document or other thing, that requirement is taken to have been complied with if that reasonable opportunity was given before the day on which the provision comes into operation in accordance with any requirements of a written law that were applicable at the time that it was given.
392.	Things done between parties
(1)	A reference in section 113 or 115 to a notice is taken to include a reference to a notice of the kind referred to in that section that was given before the day on which the section comes into operation.
(2)	A reference in section 324 or 355(2) to a request is taken to include a reference to a request of the kind referred to in that section that was made before the day on which the section comes into operation.
(3)	A reference in section 372(3) to an agreement is taken to include a reference to an agreement of the kind referred to in that section that was made before the day on which the section comes into operation.
393.	Identification evidence
(1)	Section 134 does not apply to identification evidence that is an assertion or a report of an assertion that was made before the day on which the section comes into operation.
(2)	This section does not affect the application of Part 5 to identification evidence.
394.	Privileges
(1)	Part 4 Division 1 applies to —
(a)	a confidential communication made before, on or after the day on which that Division comes into operation; and
(b)	a confidential document prepared before, on or after the day on which that Division comes into operation.
(2)	Part 4 Division 2 applies to a protected confidence made before, on or after the day on which that Division comes into operation.
(3)	Part 4 Division 3 applies to a protected communication made before, on or after the day on which that Division comes into operation.
(4)	Part 4 Division 4 applies to information given to a journalist before, on or after the day on which that Division comes into operation.
395.	Cautioning of persons
(1)	Section 205 does not apply to evidence of a statement made or an act done by a person before the day on which that section comes into operation.
(2)	This section does not prevent a court from deciding that evidence referred to in subsection (1) was obtained improperly for the purposes of section 204.
396.	Out-of-court representations
In Part 7, a reference to an out-of-court representation is taken to include a reference to an out-of-court representation made before the day on which section 235 comes into operation.
397.	Recorded interviews
(1)	In Part 7, a reference to a recorded interview is taken to include a reference to a recorded interview made before the day on which section 251 comes into operation.
(2)	For the purposes of Part 7 Division 5, if a recorded interview was made before repeal day —
(a)	the interview in the recorded interview is taken to have been conducted by a person authorised to conduct recorded interviews under the interview admissibility regulations if it was conducted by a person of a prescribed class under section 106HA(1)(a) or (1a)(a) of the former Act; and
(b)	the recorded interview is taken to meet any other requirements of the interview admissibility regulations to the extent required by those regulations if, at the time of recording, the manner in which the interview was conducted and recorded met the prescribed requirements to the prescribed extent under section 106HA(1)(b) or (1a)(b) of the former Act.
(3)	For the purposes of section 260, a reference to recorded interviews that are admissible under Part 7 Division 5 or made for use as evidence under that Division includes a reference to recorded interviews made before repeal day that were admissible under section 106HB of the former Act or made for use as evidence under that section.
(4)	Accordingly, the power to make regulations for the purposes of section 260 extends to recorded interviews referred to in subsection (3).
398.	Visual recordings of evidence
(1)	A reference in section 268(1) or 269(1) to a recording of evidence given by a witness at a special hearing is taken to include a reference to a visual recording of evidence made in a special hearing held pursuant to an order under section 106K or 106RA of the former Act.
(2)	A reference in section 268(2) to a pre-recording order made on the ground that the witness is likely to be out of the State at the time of the proceeding is taken to include a reference to an order made under section 106RA(1) of the former Act on the ground in section 106RA(4)(b) of the former Act.
(3)	A reference in section 280(2) to a recording of evidence given by a witness using a video link separation procedure is taken to include a reference to a visual recording of evidence made under section 106N(3a) of the former Act.
(4)	A reference in section 281(3) to a recording of evidence given by a witness while using a screening procedure is taken to include a reference to a visual recording of evidence made under section 106N(5) of the former Act.
399.	Restrictions on use of audio evidence under former Act
(1)	A person must not make a copy of, or otherwise reproduce, any audio of a proceeding, or any part of a proceeding, heard by means of an audio link under section 106K(3)(a)(ii) of the former Act.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(2)	A person must not play, supply or offer to supply any copy of, or reproduction of, any audio of a proceeding, or any part of a proceeding, heard by means of an audio link under section 106K(3)(a)(ii) of the former Act.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(3)	A person must not publish any audio of a proceeding, or any part of a proceeding, heard by means of an audio link under section 106K(3)(a)(ii) of the former Act.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $100 000.
400.	Restrictions on use of former Act recordings
(1)	In this section —
alter, a recording, has a meaning affected by section 228(1);
destroy, a recording, has a meaning affected by section 228(1).
(2)	A person must not, without authority —
(a)	be in possession of a former Act recording; or
(b)	supply or offer to supply a former Act recording to any person.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(3)	A person who is in possession of a former Act recording does not commit an offence against subsection (2) by virtue of being in possession of the recording if —
(a)	the person acquired that possession before this section comes into operation; and
(b)	the possession was lawful at the time the person acquired it.
(4)	A person must not, without authority, alter or destroy or permit another person to alter or destroy a former Act recording that is an original recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(5)	A person must not alter a copy of a former Act recording unless the person has authority to be in possession of the recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(6)	A person must not, without authority, copy or permit another person to copy a former Act recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(7)	A person must not, without authority, play a former Act recording.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $24 000.
(8)	A person has authority to do a thing for the purposes of this section only if the person —
(a)	would have authority to do the thing concerned under Part 7 Division 10 if the former Act recording were a protected recording (as defined in section 299); or
(b)	is acting as authorised by regulations made for the purposes of this paragraph.
(9)	This section applies even if a proceeding in connection with which the former Act recording was made or in which the former Act recording was admitted as evidence, or a hearing of the proceeding, commenced, concluded or was terminated before repeal day.
401.	Publication of former Act recordings prohibited
(1)	A person must not publish a former Act recording except in accordance with the approval of the Supreme Court.
Penalty for this subsection: imprisonment for 2 years or a fine of $100 000.
(2)	The Supreme Court may approve the publication of a former Act recording only if satisfied that exceptional circumstances justify approval being given.
(3)	This section applies even if a proceeding in connection with which the former Act recording was made or in which the former Act recording was admitted as evidence, or a hearing of the proceeding, commenced, concluded or was terminated before repeal day.
402.	Playing or tendering altered recording in proceeding
In section 307, a reference to a protected recording is taken to include a reference to a former Act recording.
403.	Power to give directions about former Act recordings
In section 308, a reference to a protected recording is taken to include a reference to a former Act recording.
404.	Use of Justice documents
Section 310 extends to a Justice document prepared before the day on which the section comes into operation.
405.	Facilitation of proof of documents
Part 9 Divisions 3 and 4 extend to a document produced, attested, verified, signed, executed, acknowledged, printed, published, sealed or certified before the day or days on which those Divisions come into operation.
406.	Admission of police affidavits
(1)	An affidavit made before the day on which section 358 comes into operation that was substantially in the form of the Sixth Schedule to the former Act is taken to have been made in the form prescribed by the regulations for the purposes of section 358.
(2)	Accordingly, section 358 applies to the use of the affidavit in a proceeding to which this Act applies.
407.	DNA certificates given before commencement
Sections 360 and 361 apply to a certificate given by a forensic scientist under section 50B of the former Act before the day on which section 360 comes into operation in the same way as they apply to a forensic biology certificate referred to in section 360.
408.	Restrictions on publishing names of complainants
(1)	Section 381(3) applies to the publication of information in relation to a criminal proceeding on or after the day on which that section comes into operation, including if a person is charged with a sexual offence before the day on which that section comes into operation.
(2)	Section 381(3) does not apply to the publication of information in relation to a civil proceeding that commenced before the day on which that section comes into operation.
409.	Continuation of orders, directions, leave and other things done under former Act
(1)	Any order given, direction made, leave granted or other thing done under the former Act by a court, judge or person acting judicially (as that term is defined in section 3 of the former Act) that had effect immediately before repeal day continues to have effect despite the repeal of the former Act.
(2)	The former Act continues to apply in respect of the order, direction, leave or other thing as if it were still in force.
(3)	In particular —
(a)	section 11A(2) and (3) of the former Act continue to apply to any order made under section 11A(1) of the former Act; and
(b)	section 27(2) of the former Act continues to apply to any question or inquiry that is the subject of an order under section 27(1)(a) or (b) of the former Act.
410.	Saving of self-incrimination certificates given under former Act
A certificate given under section 11 or 12 of the former Act continues to have the same effect as it would have if the former Act had not been repealed.
411.	Construction of references
(1)	A reference in any other written law or in any instrument to the former Act is to be read, from repeal day, as a reference to this Act.
(2)	A reference in any other written law or in any instrument to a provision of the former Act is to be read, from repeal day, as a reference to the provision of this Act that, having regard to the reference and context in which the reference occurs, most nearly corresponds to the provision of the former Act.
(3)	This section does not apply in relation to a proceeding, a hearing of a proceeding or other matter to which this Act does not apply.
412.	Transitional regulations
(1)	In this section —
specified means specified or described in the regulations;
transitional matter —
(a)	means a matter or issue of a transitional nature that arises as a result of —
(i)	the repeal of the former Act; or
(ii)	an amendment made under Part 12; or
(iii)	the enactment of this Act;
and
(b)	includes a savings or application matter or issue.
(2)	If there is not sufficient provision in this Division for dealing with a transitional matter, regulations may prescribe anything required, necessary or convenient to be prescribed in relation to the matter.
(3)	Without limiting subsection (2), regulations made for the purposes of that subsection may provide that specified provisions of this Act —
(a)	do not apply to, or in relation to, a specified matter or thing; or
(b)	apply with specified modifications to, or in relation to, a specified matter or thing.
(4)	If regulations made for the purposes of subsection (2) provide that a specified state of affairs is taken to have existed, or not to have existed, on and from a day that is earlier than the day on which the regulations are published in accordance with the Interpretation Act 1984 section 41(1)(a) but not earlier than the day on which this section comes into operation, the regulations have effect according to their terms.
(5)	If regulations made for the purposes of subsection (2) contain a provision of a kind described in subsection (4), the provision does not operate so as to —
(a)	affect, in a manner prejudicial to any person (other than the State or an authority of the State), the rights of that person existing before the day of publication of those regulations; or
(b)	impose liabilities on any person (other than the State or an authority of the State) in respect of anything done or omitted to be done before the day of publication of those regulations.

428.	Section 46A inserted
After section 46 insert:
46A.	Interaction with protections in Evidence Act 2025
(1)	In this section —
child care record has the meaning given in section 46(1);
protected communication has the meaning given in the Evidence Act 2025 section 165(1);
protected disclosure provisions means the Evidence Act 2025 Part 4 Division 3.
(2)	If a child care record contains a protected communication —
(a)	a party to a criminal proceeding cannot require the production or disclosure of the protected communication except with the leave of the court in accordance with the protected disclosure provisions; and
(b)	section 46(3) and (4) do not apply to the production of the child care record in the criminal proceeding.
(3)	Section 46(5) to (8) apply to a child care record containing a protected communication that is produced in a criminal proceeding with the leave of the court under the protected disclosure provisions, or to enable the court to determine an application for leave under the protected disclosure provisions, as if the child care record had been produced in response to a requirement under section 46(2).
(4)	Section 46(5) to (8) apply to a document containing any protected information (as defined in the Education and Care Services National Law (Western Australia) section 273(3)) that is produced in a criminal proceeding with the leave of the court under the protected disclosure provisions, or to enable the court to determine an application for leave under the protected disclosure provisions, as if —
(a)	the document were a child care record; and
(b)	the document had been produced in response to a requirement under section 46(2).

444.	Part 11A inserted
After section 124 insert:
Part 11A — Creating recorded statements for use as evidence
124A.	Terms used
In this Part —
offence includes a suspected offence;
recorded statement has the meaning given in the Evidence Act 2025 section 239;
statement admissibility regulations has the meaning given in the Evidence Act 2025 section 241(1).
124B.	Police officer may create recorded statement for use as evidence
(1)	A police officer may create a recorded statement from a person who is reasonably suspected to be the victim of an offence for use as evidence under the Evidence Act 2025 Part 7 Division 4 in a proceeding to which that Division applies.
(2)	The police officer may create the recorded statement only if —
(a)	the police officer is qualified to create a recorded statement under the statement admissibility regulations; and
(b)	the person making the statement to the police officer has reached 18 years of age.
(3)	The police officer must, before interviewing the person about the offence in a recorded statement, advise the person —
(a)	that the recorded statement may be used in evidence at a hearing; and
(b)	that the person may be called to give evidence in cross-examination or in further examination; and
(c)	about the access that the accused will have to the recorded statement if a proceeding is commenced.
124C.	Consent of person making statement
(1)	A police officer must not create a recorded statement from a person for use as evidence under the Evidence Act 2025 Part 7 Division 4 without the consent of the person making the statement.
(2)	The person is not obliged to give that consent.
124D.	Recordings to be retained by police
(1)	The Commissioner of Police must keep any recorded statement created by a police officer for use as evidence under the Evidence Act 2025 Part 7 Division 4, or a copy of it, in safe custody for at least 10 years.
(2)	If the Supreme Court is satisfied there is good cause to keep a recorded statement for more than 10 years, it may order the Commissioner of Police to keep the recorded statement for an additional period set by the Court.
(3)	The Commissioner of Police must ensure that the prosecutor in any proceedings in which the recorded statement may be admitted as the evidence in chief of a person is given a copy of the statement.
(4)	The Commissioner of Police may, in writing, authorise a person to destroy a recorded statement after the expiry of the period for which it must be kept.
(5)	The person is authorised to destroy a recorded statement in accordance with the direction of the Commissioner of Police.
(6)	The powers conferred by this section are subject to any regulations referred to in the Evidence Act 2025 section 249.

suspect that the arrested suspect does not have to say or do anything but that anything the arrested suspect does say or do may be used in evidence;

456.	Section 137B inserted
After section 137A insert:
137B.	Restrictions on disclosure of recorded evidence
(1)	In this section —
evidentiary material has the meaning given by section 42;
serve, a person, means to serve the person in accordance with Schedule 2 clause 2, 3 or 4.
(2)	A prosecutor must not serve on an accused a copy of any recording or recorded statement if —
(a)	it is a recording to which Schedule 3 clause 8 applies; or
(b)	the accused is not entitled to be in possession of the recording or recorded statement under the Evidence Act 2025 section 236(10), 242(3), 254(3), 300(1) or 400(2).
(3)	If another provision of this Act requires a prosecutor to serve on an accused evidentiary material that is a copy of a recording or a recorded statement, and subsection (2) applies to the copy of the recording or recorded statement other than because of the Evidence Act 2025 section 254(3), the prosecutor must comply with that requirement by —
(a)	serving a copy of the recording or recorded statement on the accused’s legal practitioner; or
(b)	if the accused is not represented by a legal practitioner —
(i)	serving a transcript of the recording or recorded statement on the accused; and
(ii)	giving the accused a reasonable opportunity to view the recording or recorded statement in accordance with any applicable requirements of the Evidence Act 2025.
(4)	If another provision of this Act requires a prosecutor to serve on an accused evidentiary material that is a copy of a recording or a recorded statement, and subsection (2) applies to the copy of the recording or recorded statement because of the Evidence Act 2025 section 254(3), the prosecutor must comply with that requirement by —
(a)	serving a transcript of the recording or recorded statement on the accused’s legal practitioner or, if the accused is not represented by a legal practitioner, the accused; and
(b)	giving the accused and the accused’s legal practitioner a reasonable opportunity to view the recording or recorded statement in accordance with any applicable requirements of the Evidence Act 2025.
(5)	A prosecutor who complies with subsection (3) or (4) is taken, for the purposes of this Act, to have served a copy of the relevant evidentiary material on the accused.
(6)	This section applies to any relevant authorised officer (as defined in section 80) who is required by this Act to serve evidentiary material on an accused in the same way as it applies to a prosecutor.

After Schedule 3 clause 7 insert:
8.	Access of accused to some recorded evidence restricted
(1)	In this clause —
special procedure for giving evidence has the meaning given in the Evidence Act 2025 section 228(1).
(2)	A court that conducts an examination under clause 6 must not give to the accused a copy of an electronic recording of a witness’s evidence at the examination if —
(a)	the evidence of the witness at the examination is given using a special procedure for giving evidence; or
(b)	the court considers that any evidence of the witness at trial may be given using a special procedure for giving evidence.
(3)	If this clause prevents the court from giving an accused a copy of an electronic recording of a witness’s evidence, the court must comply with clause 6(7) by —
(a)	giving a copy of the recording to the accused’s legal practitioner; or
(b)	if the accused is not represented by a legal practitioner, giving a transcript of the recording to the accused.
(4)	If an accused is not given a copy of an electronic recording of evidence given at an examination under clause 6 because the accused is not represented by a legal practitioner, the recording of evidence is not admissible in evidence under clause 7 unless the court is satisfied that the prosecutor has given the accused a reasonable opportunity to view the recording (if it is an audio visual recording) or to hear the recording (if it is an audio recording).
	Division 13 — Criminal Property Confiscation Act 2000 amended
465.	Act amended
This Division amends the Criminal Property Confiscation Act 2000.
466.	Sections 109A to 109E inserted
After section 109 insert:
109A.	Terms used
(1)	In this section and sections 109B to 109D —
business record means a book of account or other document prepared or used in the ordinary course of a business for the purpose of recording any matter relating to the business;
derived means derived, by the use of a computer or otherwise, by calculation, comparison, selection, sorting, consolidation or by accounting, statistical or logical procedures;
qualified person, in relation to a statement, means a person who —
(a)	if the statement is not admissible in evidence unless made by an expert on the subject of the statement — was at the time of making of the statement such an expert; or
(b)	otherwise — had, at the time of making of the statement, or may reasonably be supposed to have had at that time, personal knowledge of the matters dealt with by the statement;
statement includes any representation of fact or opinion whether made in words or otherwise.
(2)	A reference in subsection (1) to a business includes a reference to the following —
(a)	a profession, calling, occupation, trade or undertaking;
(b)	an activity engaged in or carried on by the Commonwealth, a State or a Territory;
(c)	an activity engaged in or carried on by the government of a foreign country;
(d)	an activity engaged in or carried on by a person or body holding office or exercising power under or because of the Commonwealth Constitution, an Australian law or a law of a foreign country, being an activity engaged in or carried on in the performance of the functions of the office or in the exercise of the power (otherwise than in a private capacity);
(e)	the proceedings of an Australian Parliament, a House of an Australian Parliament, a committee of a House of an Australian Parliament or a committee of an Australian Parliament;
(f)	the proceedings of a legislature of a foreign country, including a House or committee (however described) of a legislature of a foreign country.
(3)	A reference in subsection (1) to a business also includes a reference to each of the following —
(a)	a business that is not engaged in or carried on for profit;
(b)	a business engaged in or carried on outside Australia.
109B.	Admissibility of business records
(1)	Subsection (2) applies if, in a proceeding on an application under this Act, oral evidence of a fact or opinion would be admissible.
(2)	A statement in a document and tending to establish the fact or opinion is, on production of the document, admissible as evidence of that fact or opinion, if —
(a)	the statement is, or directly or indirectly reproduces, or is derived from, a business record; and
(b)	the court is satisfied that the business record is a genuine business record.
(3)	If a statement referred to in subsection (2) is made by a qualified person, that person must not be called as a witness unless the court otherwise orders.
(4)	This section —
(a)	makes a statement admissible despite —
(i)	the hearsay rule as defined in the Evidence Act 2025 section 3(1); and
(ii)	the rules against secondary evidence of the contents of a document; and
(iii)	if applicable, the fact that the person who made the statement, or the person who made a statement from which the information in the statement is reproduced or derived, is a witness in the proceeding (whether or not the person gives evidence consistent or inconsistent with the statement); and
(iv)	if applicable, the fact that the statement is in such a form that it would not be admissible if given as oral evidence;
but
(b)	does not make admissible a statement that is otherwise inadmissible.
(5)	For the purpose of deciding under subsection (2) whether or not a statement is admissible as evidence, the court may draw any reasonable inference from —
(a)	the form or contents of the document containing the statement; or
(b)	any other circumstances.
109C.	Weight of evidence admitted under s. 109B
(1)	This section applies to a court estimating the weight, if any, to be given to a statement admitted as evidence in a proceeding under section 109B.
(2)	The court must have regard to all the circumstances from which any inference can reasonably be drawn as to the accuracy or otherwise of the statement, including —
(a)	the question of whether or not the statement was made contemporaneously with the occurrence or existence of the facts stated; and
(b)	the question of whether or not the qualified person, or any person concerned with making or keeping the document containing the statement, had any incentive to conceal or misrepresent the facts; and
(c)	the question of whether or not the information in the statement was of a kind that was collected systematically; and
(d)	the question of whether or not the information in the statement was collected under a duty to do so; and
(e)	if the statement wholly or partly reproduces or is derived from information from 1 or more devices — the reliability of the device or devices; and
(f)	if the statement reproduces or is derived from any information — the reliability of the means of reproduction or derivation.
109D.	Evidence as to credibility of qualified persons
(1)	This section applies if —
(a)	a statement is admitted as evidence in a proceeding under section 109B; and
(b)	the qualified person who made the statement is not called as a witness in the proceeding.
(2)	Evidence that, if the qualified person had been called, would have been admissible under the Evidence Act 2025 Part 3 Division 7 Subdivision 2 for the purpose of destroying or supporting the person’s credibility is admissible in the proceeding for that purpose.
(3)	Evidence tending to prove that, whether before or after the qualified person made the statement, the person made another statement (whether orally, in a document or otherwise) inconsistent with the statement is admissible in the proceeding for the purpose of showing that the person has contradicted themself.
(4)	Nothing in subsection (2) or (3) allows evidence to be given of a matter if, had the qualified person been called as a witness and denied the matter in cross-examination, evidence of that matter could not have been adduced by the cross-examining party.
(5)	Evidence proving that the qualified person has been convicted of an indictable offence is admissible in the proceeding to the same extent as it would be if —
(a)	the person had been called; and
(b)	on being questioned as to whether or not the person had been convicted of an indictable offence, the person had —
(i)	denied the fact; or
(ii)	not admitted the fact; or
(iii)	refused to answer the question.
109E.	Dispute as to happening of event
(1)	Subsection (2) applies if —
(a)	in a proceeding on an application under this Act the happening of an event of a particular kind is in question; and
(b)	a system has been followed of making and keeping a record of the occurrence of all events of that kind.
(2)	Oral or other evidence to establish that there is no record of the happening of the event is admissible in the proceeding to prove that the event did not happen.
(3)	If evidence is, or is proposed to be, tendered in a proceeding under subsection (2), the court may —
(a)	order that the whole or part of the record be produced; and
(b)	if the order is not complied with —
(i)	reject the evidence; or
(ii)	if the evidence has been received, exclude it.
(4)	Subsection (5) applies to a court estimating the weight, if any, to be given to evidence admitted in a proceeding under subsection (2).
(5)	The court must have regard to all the circumstances from which any inference can reasonably be drawn as to the accuracy or otherwise of the evidence, including whether or not any person concerned with the making or keeping of the relevant record had any incentive to omit recording the happening of the event in question.
